3rd year Flashcards:




=====================================================================
=====================================================================

Section: Internal Medicine
Topic: "Pulmonology — Pneumonia, COPD, asthma, PE, TB"

Front: Most common cause of Community-Acquired Pneumonia (CAP)?

Back: Streptococcus pneumoniae.

Front: CURB-65 criteria for pneumonia admission?

Back: Confusion, Urea >7 mmol/L, RR ≥30, BP (S <90 or D ≤60), Age ≥65.

Front: Standard outpatient treatment for CAP in a healthy patient (no comorbidities)?

Back: Amoxicillin, Doxycycline, or a Macrolide (if local resistance <25%).

Front: Empiric inpatient treatment for non-ICU CAP?

Back: Respiratory Fluoroquinolone (e.g., Levofloxacin) OR Beta-lactam + Macrolide.

Front: Common causative agent of pneumonia in alcoholics with "currant jelly" sputum?

Back: Klebsiella pneumoniae.

Front: Pneumonia associated with air conditioning or contaminated water sources?

Back: Legionella pneumophila.

Front: Diagnostic test for Legionella?

Back: Urine antigen test.

Front: Physical exam finding: Increased tactile fremitus and dullness to percussion?

Back: Consolidation (Pneumonia).

Front: Definition of Hospital-Acquired Pneumonia (HAP)?

Back: Pneumonia occurring ≥48 hours after admission.

Front: Treatment for suspected MRSA pneumonia?

Back: Vancomycin or Linezolid.

Front: Management of a parapneumonic effusion with pH <7.2 or glucose <60?

Back: Chest tube drainage (indicates empyema or complicated effusion).

Front: Primary TB chest X-ray finding?

Back: Ghon complex (calcified parenchymal lesion and hilar node).

Front: Reactivation TB typically affects which part of the lung?

Back: Apical/Posterior segments of upper lobes.

Front: Gold standard for diagnosing active TB?

Back: Sputum culture (though AFB stain is the rapid initial step).

Front: Standard 4-drug regimen for active TB (RIPE)?

Back: Rifampin, Isoniazid, Pyrazinamide, Ethambutol.

Front: Side effect of Ethambutol requiring monitoring?

Back: Optic neuritis (decreased visual acuity/red-green color blindness).

Front: Side effect of Pyrazinamide?

Back: Hyperuricemia (can trigger gout).

Front: Medication given with Isoniazid to prevent peripheral neuropathy?

Back: Vitamin B6 (Pyridoxine).

Front: Screening test for latent TB in a patient with prior BCG vaccine?

Back: IGRA (Interferon-Gamma Release Assay).

Front: Definition of a positive PPD in a healthcare worker?

Back: ≥10 mm induration.

Pulmonology Part 2: Asthma & COPD

Front: The "Classic Triad" of asthma pathophysiology?

Back: Airway inflammation, reversible airway obstruction, and bronchial hyperresponsiveness.

Front: Diagnostic criteria for asthma on spirometry?

Back: Increase in FEV1 >12% and >200 mL after bronchodilator.

Front: Gold standard for diagnosing asthma when spirometry is normal?

Back: Methacholine challenge (PC20 <8 mg/mL).

Front: Step 1 treatment for intermittent asthma (GINA/NAEPP update)?

Back: PRN low-dose ICS-formoterol.

Front: Characteristic finding in sputum of asthmatics?

Back: Curschmann spirals or Charcot-Leyden crystals.

Front: Definition of "Moderate Persistent" asthma?

Back: Daily symptoms or nighttime awakenings >1x/week.

Front: Side effect of inhaled corticosteroids (ICS)?

Back: Oral candidiasis (thrush).

Front: Definition of Chronic Bronchitis?

Back: Productive cough for ≥3 months in 2 consecutive years.

Front: Major genetic risk factor for COPD in a young non-smoker?

Back: Alpha-1 Antitrypsin deficiency.

Front: Typical FEV1/FVC ratio in COPD?

Back: <0.70 (post-bronchodilator).

Front: Only two interventions proven to reduce mortality in COPD?

Back: Smoking cessation and Long-term Oxygen Therapy (LTOT).

Front: Criteria for initiating LTOT in COPD?

Back: PaO2 ≤55 mmHg or SaO2 ≤88% at rest.

Front: Best initial treatment for symptomatic COPD (Group A)?

Back: SABA or SAMA (Short-acting bronchodilator).

Front: Common CXR findings in emphysema?

Back: Flattened diaphragm, hyperlucency, and increased retrosternal airspace.

Front: Most common cause of COPD exacerbation?

Back: Viral or bacterial infection (e.g., H. influenzae).

Front: Three cardinal symptoms requiring antibiotics in COPD exacerbation?

Back: Increased dyspnea, increased sputum volume, and increased sputum purulence.

Front: Target oxygen saturation in a COPD exacerbation?

Back: 88% to 92%.

Front: First-line ventilation for COPD exacerbation with respiratory acidosis?

Back: Non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (NPPV/BiPAP).

Front: Characteristic EKG finding in severe COPD/Cor Pulmonale?

Back: Multifocal Atrial Tachycardia (MAT) or Right Axis Deviation.

Front: Lung volume change pathognomonic for air trapping in COPD?

Back: Increased Residual Volume (RV) and Total Lung Capacity (TLC).

Pulmonology Part 3: Pulmonary Embolism (PE)

Front: Most common symptom of Pulmonary Embolism?

Back: Dyspnea.

Front: Most common EKG finding in PE?

Back: Sinus tachycardia.

Front: The "Classic" but rare EKG sign of PE?

Back: S1Q3T3 (Deep S in I, Q wave in III, inverted T in III).

Front: Initial step for a patient with low clinical suspicion of PE?

Back: D-dimer (to rule out).

Front: Gold standard imaging for diagnosing PE?

Back: CT Angiography (CTPA).

Front: Alternative imaging for PE if the patient has renal failure or contrast allergy?

Back: V/Q Scan.

Front: Arterial Blood Gas (ABG) finding typically seen in PE?

Back: Respiratory alkalosis (due to hyperventilation) with hypoxemia.

Front: Hampton’s Hump on CXR represents?

Back: Pulmonary infarction (wedge-shaped opacity).

Front: Westermark sign on CXR represents?

Back: Avascularity distal to the PE (oligemia).

Front: Treatment for a hemodynamically unstable PE (Massive PE)?

Back: Systemic Thrombolysis (e.g., tPA).

Front: First-line anticoagulation for a stable PE?

Back: DOACs (e.g., Rivaroxaban, Apixaban) or LMWH bridge to Warfarin.

Front: Indication for an IVC filter?

Back: Documented DVT/PE with absolute contraindication to anticoagulation.

Front: Minimum duration of anticoagulation for a first-time provoked PE?

Back: 3 months.

Front: Definition of "Submassive" PE?

Back: Hemodynamically stable but with evidence of Right Ventricular (RV) strain.

Front: Echocardiographic sign of RV strain in PE?

Back: McConnell’s sign (RV free wall akinesia with sparing of the apex).

Pulmonology Part 4: Advanced Physiology & Mixed Topics

Front: Flow-volume loop: Scooped-out appearance?

Back: Obstructive lung disease (Asthma/COPD).

Front: Flow-volume loop: Tall, narrow, "witch's hat" appearance?

Back: Restrictive lung disease (IPF/Sarcoidosis).

Front: West Zone of the lung where PA > Pa > Pv?

Back: Zone 1 (Apex).

Front: Effect of PE on V/Q ratio?

Back: V/Q = infinity (Dead space ventilation).

Front: Effect of airway obstruction/mucus plug on V/Q ratio?

Back: V/Q = 0 (Shunt).

Front: Definition of Type 1 Respiratory Failure?

Back: Hypoxemic (PaO2 <60) with normal or low PaCO2.

Front: Definition of Type 2 Respiratory Failure?

Back: Hypercapnic (PaCO2 >50).

Front: Light's Criteria for Exudative Effusion?

Back: LDH >2/3 upper limit, Fluid/Serum LDH >0.6, or Fluid/Serum Protein >0.5.

Front: Most common cause of transudative pleural effusion?

Back: Congestive Heart Failure.

Front: Fluid analysis of a Chylothorax?

Back: High triglycerides (>110 mg/dL) and milky appearance.

Front: Management of a primary spontaneous pneumothorax <2 cm in a stable patient?

Back: Observation and supplemental oxygen.

Front: Management of a Tension Pneumothorax?

Back: Immediate needle decompression followed by chest tube.

Front: Location for needle decompression in tension pneumothorax?

Back: 2nd intercostal space, mid-clavicular line (or 4th/5th anterior axillary).

Front: Classic CXR finding in Asbestosis?

Back: Pleural plaques (calcified) involving the diaphragm.

Front: Silicosis increases the risk of which infection?

Back: Tuberculosis (impaired macrophage function).

Front: "Eggshell" calcifications of hilar lymph nodes?

Back: Silicosis.

Front: Bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and non-caseating granulomas?

Back: Sarcoidosis.

Front: Electrolyte abnormality common in Sarcoidosis?

Back: Hypercalcemia (due to 1-alpha hydroxylase activity in macrophages).

Front: First-line treatment for symptomatic Sarcoidosis?

Back: Glucocorticoids (Prednisone).

Front: Meaning of a "normal" PaCO2 in a severe asthma attack?

Back: Impending respiratory failure (patient is tiring).

Front: DLCO in Emphysema vs. Asthma?

Back: Decreased in Emphysema; Normal or Increased in Asthma.

Front: DLCO in Restrictive Lung Disease (Interstial)?

Back: Decreased.

Front: Primary drug for smoking cessation that is a nicotinic receptor partial agonist?

Back: Varenicline (Chantix).

Front: Contraindication for Bupropion in smoking cessation?

Back: Seizure disorder or eating disorders.

Front: Most common lung cancer in non-smokers?

Back: Adenocarcinoma.

Front: Lung cancer associated with PTHrP and hypercalcemia?

Back: Squamous Cell Carcinoma.

Front: Lung cancer associated with SIADH or Lambert-Eaton?

Back: Small Cell Lung Cancer.

Front: Standard of care for diagnosing a peripheral lung nodule?

Back: Transthoracic needle aspiration or VATS.

Front: Standard of care for diagnosing a central lung lesion?

Back: Bronchoscopy.

Front: Definition of Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS) P/F ratio?

Back: PaO2/FiO2 ≤300 mmHg.

Front: Primary ventilator strategy for ARDS?

Back: Low tidal volume ventilation (6 mL/kg ideal body weight).

Front: Common CXR finding in ARDS?

Back: Diffuse bilateral "fluffy" infiltrates (sparing costophrenic angles).

Front: PCWP in ARDS?

Back: <18 mmHg (suggests non-cardiogenic origin).

Front: Characteristic finding on sleep study for Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA)?

Back: Apnea-Hypopnea Index (AHI) ≥5 with symptoms or ≥15 without.

Front: First-line treatment for OSA?

Back: CPAP and weight loss.

Front: Most common cause of Cor Pulmonale?

Back: COPD.

Front: Medication of choice for Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension (Group 1)?

Back: PDE-5 inhibitors (Sildenafil), Endothelin antagonists (Bosentan), or Prostanoids.

Front: Initial test for suspected Pulmonary Hypertension?

Back: Echocardiogram (to estimate PASP).

Front: Gold standard for definitive diagnosis of Pulmonary Hypertension?

Back: Right heart catheterization (mPAP >20 mmHg).

Front: Physical exam sign of right-sided heart failure?

Back: Jugular Venous Distension (JVD) and peripheral edema.

Front: "Sudden onset chest pain, tachycardia, and clear lungs" suggests?

Back: Pulmonary Embolism.

Front: Difference between Hypoxemia and Hypoxia?

Back: Hypoxemia is low arterial O2 tension; Hypoxia is low O2 delivery to tissues.

Front: Effect of high altitude on the oxygen-dissociation curve?

Back: Right shift (increased 2,3-BPG).

Front: Characteristic of Malignant Pleural Effusion?

Back: Exudative, often bloody, positive cytology.

Front: Treatment for Latent TB (standard)?

Back: 4 months of Rifampin OR 9 months of Isoniazid.

Front: Recommended vaccine for adults >65 to prevent pneumonia?

Back: PCV20 (or PCV15 followed by PPSV23).

Front: Mainstay of treatment for allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis (ABPA)?

Back: Oral corticosteroids and Itraconazole.

Front: Diagnostic finding for ABPA?

Back: High IgE, eosinophilia, and "finger-in-glove" opacities on CT.

Front: Common cause of lung abscess?

Back: Aspiration of anaerobic oral flora.

Front: Preferred antibiotic for lung abscess?

Back: Ampicillin-sulbactam or Clindamycin.

Front: "Honeycombing" on High-Resolution CT?

Back: Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis (IPF).

Front: Distinguishing feature of Hypersensitivity Pneumonitis?

Back: Exposure history (e.g., birds, hay) and reversal upon removal of agent.

Front: Carbon Monoxide poisoning effect on SaO2 (pulse ox)?

Back: Often shows 100% (pulse ox cannot distinguish HbO2 from Carboxy-Hb).

Front: Treatment for Carbon Monoxide poisoning?

Back: 100% Oxygen (Hyperbaric if severe).

Front: Formula for A-a gradient?

Back: $PAO2 - PaO2$ where $PAO2 = (FiO2 \times (Patm - PH2O)) - (PaCO2 / 0.8)$.

Front: Causes of Hypoxemia with a NORMAL A-a gradient?

Back: Hypoventilation or High Altitude.

Front: Causes of Hypoxemia with an INCREASED A-a gradient?

Back: V/Q mismatch, Shunt, or Diffusion limitation.

Front: Typical CXR finding in Mycoplasma pneumonia?

Back: Interstitial "patchy" infiltrates (looks worse than patient feels).

Front: Most likely diagnosis: Smoker, weight loss, hyponatremia?

Back: Small Cell Lung Cancer (SIADH).

Front: Superior Vena Cava (SVC) Syndrome is most commonly caused by?

Back: Bronchogenic carcinoma (Small Cell).

Front: Samter’s Triad?

Back: Asthma, Aspirin sensitivity, Nasal polyps.

Front: Why is Aspirin avoided in Samter's Triad?

Back: Leads to overproduction of leukotrienes.

Front: Mechanism of Albuterol?

Back: Beta-2 agonist (increases cAMP → bronchodilation).

Front: Mechanism of Tiotropium?

Back: Muscarinic antagonist (blocks M3 → prevents bronchoconstriction).

Front: Management of massive hemoptysis (>600mL)?

Back: Secure airway, place "bleeding side down," and perform bronchoscopy/embolization.

Front: Pneumonia associated with exposure to birds (parrots, turkeys)?

Back: Chlamydia psittaci (Psittacosis).

Front: Pneumonia associated with exposure to livestock or parturient cats?

Back: Coxiella burnetii (Q fever).

Front: Most common cause of pneumonia in patients with Cystic Fibrosis (adults)?

Back: Pseudomonas aeruginosa.

Front: Treatment for Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PCP)?

Back: Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX).

Front: When to add steroids to PCP treatment in HIV patients?

Back: PaO2 <70 mmHg or A-a gradient ≥35 mmHg.

Front: Fungal pneumonia associated with bat or bird droppings in the Ohio/Mississippi River valleys?

Back: Histoplasmosis.

Front: Fungal infection associated with "Broad-based budding yeast" on microscopy?

Back: Blastomycosis.

Front: Fungal pneumonia common in the Southwest US (desert) presenting with erythema nodosum?

Back: Coccidioidomycosis.

Front: Characteristic finding of Aspergillus on silver stain?

Back: Septate hyphae with 45-degree (dichotomous) branching.

Front: Characteristic finding of Mucor species?

Back: Non-septate hyphae with wide-angle (90-degree) branching.

Pulmonology Part 6: Pleural & Mediastinal Disease

Front: Definition of a "complicated" parapneumonic effusion?

Back: Presence of bacteria on Gram stain/culture or pH <7.2.

Front: Treatment for an uncomplicated parapneumonic effusion?

Back: Antibiotics alone (no drainage needed).

Front: Most common cause of a pleural effusion that is "milky" and has high lymphocytes?

Back: Chylothorax (often due to thoracic duct trauma or lymphoma).

Front: Light's Criteria: Pleural fluid LDH to Serum LDH ratio threshold for exudate?

Back: >0.6.

Front: Light's Criteria: Pleural fluid Protein to Serum Protein ratio threshold for exudate?

Back: >0.5.

Front: Common cause of a very low pleural fluid glucose (<30 mg/dL)?

Back: Rheumatoid Arthritis or Empyema.

Front: Definition of an Empyema?

Back: Frank pus in the pleural space.

Front: Most common cause of an anterior mediastinal mass (The 4 T's)?

Back: Thymoma, Teratoma, Thyroid (ectopic), "Terrible" Lymphoma.

Pulmonology Part 7: Advanced COPD & Asthma Management

Front: Next step for a COPD patient with frequent exacerbations despite LAMA + LABA?

Back: Add Inhaled Corticosteroid (Triple Therapy: ICS + LABA + LAMA).

Front: Role of Roflumilast in COPD?

Back: PDE-4 inhibitor used to reduce exacerbations in patients with chronic bronchitis and severe obstruction.

Front: Common EKG finding in a patient with a massive PE and Right Heart Strain?

Back: New Right Bundle Branch Block (RBBB).

Front: Definition of "Pulsus Paradoxus"?

Back: Drop in systolic BP >10 mmHg during inspiration (seen in severe asthma/COPD or tamponade).

Front: Indication for Omalizumab in asthma?

Back: Severe persistent asthma with high IgE levels and poor control on ICS/LABA.

Front: Mechanism of Montelukast?

Back: Leukotriene receptor antagonist (CysLT1).

Front: Management of "Silent Chest" in an acute asthma exacerbation?

Back: Immediate intubation (indicates no air movement/impending arrest).

Front: First-line medication for Exercise-Induced Bronchospasm?

Back: SABA 10-20 minutes before exercise.

Pulmonology Part 8: Critical Care & Miscellaneous

Front: Best initial test for suspected Sleep Apnea?

Back: Polysomnography (Sleep Study).

Front: Characteristic PFT finding in Vocal Cord Dysfunction?

Back: Flattened inspiratory loop (Extrathoracic obstruction).

Front: Diagnosis for: Young woman, recurrent pneumothorax, chylous effusion, CT shows thin-walled cysts?

Back: Lymphangioleiomyomatosis (LAM).

Front: "Flesh-colored" nodules on the shins in a patient with respiratory symptoms?

Back: Erythema Nodosum (highly suggestive of Sarcoidosis or Coccidioidomycosis).

Front: Löfgren Syndrome triad (variant of Sarcoidosis)?

Back: Erythema nodosum, bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy, and migratory polyarthritis.

Front: Preferred diagnostic step for a centrally located lung mass?

Back: Flexible Bronchoscopy.

Front: Preferred diagnostic step for a peripherally located lung mass?

Back: Percutaneous (Transthoracic) needle biopsy.

Front: Formula for calculating the PaO2 from SaO2 (The 40-50-60/70-80-90 rule)?

Back: SaO2 70% ≈ PaO2 40; SaO2 80% ≈ PaO2 50; SaO2 90% ≈ PaO2 60.

Front: Most common cause of death in patients with Sarcoidosis?

Back: Pulmonary Fibrosis (Respiratory Failure).

Front: Primary treatment for Alpha-1 Antitrypsin deficiency?

Back: Smoking cessation and IV Alpha-1 Antitrypsin augmentation therapy.

Front: Characteristic finding on CT in Bronchiectasis?

Back: "Signet ring" sign (dilated bronchus larger than its adjacent artery).

Front: Most common cause of Bronchiectasis in the US?

Back: Cystic Fibrosis.

Front: Best drug to prevent high-altitude pulmonary edema (HAPE)?

Back: Nifedipine (if prophylactic) or gradual ascent.

Front: Standard treatment for Carbon Monoxide (CO) poisoning?

Back: 100% Hyperbaric oxygen (if CO levels >25% or neurologic symptoms).

Front: Cyanide poisoning antidote often paired with CO treatment?

Back: Hydroxocobalamin or Sodium Thiosulfate.

Front: Most common EKG finding in Pulmonary Hypertension?

Back: Right ventricular hypertrophy (Tall R wave in V1, Right Axis Deviation).

Pulmonology Part 9: Tuberculosis (Deep Dive)

Front: Appearance of Tuberculosis on Histology?

Back: Caseating granulomas with central necrosis and Langhans giant cells.

Front: Definition of "Miliary" TB?

Back: Hematogenous spread of TB resulting in small "millet seed" lesions throughout the lungs and organs.

Front: Site of "Pott's Disease"?

Back: Tuberculosis of the spine (vertebrae).

Front: Most common extrapulmonary site of TB?

Back: Lymph nodes (Scrofula).

Front: Screening frequency for TB in healthcare workers with a baseline negative test?

Back: Only after an exposure (per latest CDC guidelines; no longer annually for all).

Front: Minimum time for "Standard" TB treatment duration?

Back: 6 months (2 months RIPE, 4 months RI).

Front: Major side effect of Rifampin?

Back: Orange discoloration of body fluids (urine, tears) and CYP450 induction.

Front: Which TB drug is contraindicated in pregnancy?

Back: Pyrazinamide (strictly speaking, RI and E are preferred; P is avoided in some guidelines).

Summary Checklist for Students

Obstructive: $FEV1/FVC < 0.7$

Restrictive: $TLC < 80\%$ predicted.

Diffusion (DLCO): Differentiates Emphysema (low) from Asthma (normal/high).

Admission: Use CURB-65.

Antibiotics: Always cover S. pneumo and Legionella/Mycoplasma (atypicals).

This brings the total count to roughly 115-120 high-yield points, covering the entire requested section from basic pathophysiology (West's) to clinical management (Harrison's/First Aid).


Front: Definition of a "solitary pulmonary nodule"?
Back: A single, well-circumscribed lesion <3 cm in diameter.

Front: Management of a lung nodule with "popcorn" calcification on CT?
Back: Observation (indicates a benign Hamartoma).

Front: Most important risk factor for lung cancer (besides smoking)?
Back: Radon gas exposure.

Front: Paraneoplastic syndrome associated with Squamous Cell Lung Cancer?
Back: Hypercalcemia due to PTHrP production.

Front: Paraneoplastic syndrome associated with Small Cell Lung Cancer (renal)?
Back: SIADH (causing euvolemic hyponatremia).

Front: Paraneoplastic syndrome associated with Small Cell Lung Cancer (adrenal)?
Back: Ectopic ACTH production (Cushing Syndrome).

Front: Classic presentation of Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome?
Back: Proximal muscle weakness that improves with use.

Front: Pancoast Tumor (Superior Sulcus Tumor) typically presents with?
Back: Horner Syndrome (Ptosis, Miosis, Anhidrosis) and shoulder pain.

Front: Standard screening protocol for lung cancer (USPSTF)?
Back: Annual Low-Dose CT for adults 50–80 with a 20 pack-year history who currently smoke or quit in the last 15 years.

Front: Which lung cancer is "central" and characterized by intercellular bridges on histology?
Back: Squamous Cell Carcinoma.

Pulmonology Part 11: Occupational & Environmental Lung Disease
Front: Pneumoconiosis associated with coal mining?
Back: Coal Worker’s Pneumoconiosis (Black Lung).

Front: Caplan Syndrome?
Back: Combination of Rheumatoid Arthritis and pneumoconiosis (usually coal or silica) with intrapulmonary nodules.

Front: Occupational exposure associated with Berylliosis?
Back: Aerospace, electronics, or nuclear power industries.

Front: Pathological similarity between Berylliosis and Sarcoidosis?
Back: Both present with non-caseating granulomas.

Front: Primary treatment for Berylliosis?
Back: Systemic Corticosteroids.

Front: Distinction between "Farmer’s Lung" and "Silicosis"?
Back: Farmer’s Lung is Hypersensitivity Pneumonitis (moldy hay); Silicosis is a fibrotic pneumoconiosis (sandblasting/rock mining).

Front: Byssinosis is caused by inhalation of?
Back: Cotton dust ("Monday morning chest tightness").

Front: Asbestos exposure significantly increases the risk of which two cancers?
Back: Bronchogenic Carcinoma (more common) and Mesothelioma (more specific).

Pulmonology Part 12: Rare but High-Yield Entities
Front: Pulmonary Alveolar Proteinosis (PAP) is caused by?
Back: Accumulation of surfactant in the alveoli due to anti-GM-CSF antibodies.

Front: Characteristic "Periodic Acid-Schiff (PAS) positive" material in alveoli?
Back: Pulmonary Alveolar Proteinosis.

Front: Treatment for Pulmonary Alveolar Proteinosis?
Back: Whole-lung lavage.

Front: Characteristic finding of Goodpasture Syndrome?
Back: Anti-GBM antibodies attacking the lungs (hemoptysis) and kidneys (hematuria).

Front: Primary treatment for Wegener's (Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis)?
Back: Corticosteroids and Cyclophosphamide (or Rituximab).

Front: Serological marker for Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis?
Back: c-ANCA (PR3-ANCA).

Front: Serological marker for Churg-Strauss (Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis)?
Back: p-ANCA (MPO-ANCA) and high peripheral eosinophil count.

Pulmonology Part 13: Pulmonary Critical Care (Mechanical Ventilation)
Front: Initial Tidal Volume setting for a patient with ARDS?
Back: 6 mL/kg of Ideal Body Weight.

Front: Goal for Plateau Pressure in ARDS management?
Back: <30 cm H2O.

Front: Primary purpose of PEEP (Positive End-Expiratory Pressure) in ARDS?
Back: To recruit collapsed alveoli and improve oxygenation.

Front: Potential complication of high PEEP or high Tidal Volumes?
Back: Barotrauma (Pneumothorax) or Volutrauma.

Front: First-line management for a patient with Acute Cardiogenic Pulmonary Edema?
Back: Supplemental O2, Loop Diuretics (Lasix), and Nitroglycerin (if BP allows).

Front: How does PEEP affect cardiac output?
Back: Decreases cardiac output by increasing intrathoracic pressure and reducing venous return (preload).

Front: Clinical sign of Carbon Dioxide Narcosis?
Back: Somnolence, confusion, and asterixis in a patient with high PaCO2.

Front: Management of "Auto-PEEP" (breath stacking) in an intubated COPD patient?
Back: Disconnect the circuit briefly to allow full exhalation and decrease the respiratory rate.

Front: Mechanism of Roflumilast in COPD management?
Back: Phosphodiesterase-4 (PDE-4) inhibitor that increases cAMP to reduce inflammation.

Front: Side effect of Theophylline toxicity?
Back: Seizures and cardiac arrhythmias (narrow therapeutic index).

Front: Primary drug class for the treatment of Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension (Group 1)?
Back: Endothelin receptor antagonists (e.g., Bosentan) or PDE-5 inhibitors (e.g., Sildenafil).

Front: Common side effect of inhaled Tiotropium?
Back: Dry mouth (Anticholinergic effect).

Front: Best initial antibiotic for a "Rusty" sputum pneumonia?
Back: Ceftriaxone plus Azithromycin (covering S. pneumoniae).

Front: Preferred antibiotic for a "foul-smelling" sputum pneumonia?
Back: Ampicillin-sulbactam (covers anaerobes and aerobes in aspiration).

Front: Mechanism of Omalizumab in severe asthma?
Back: Monoclonal antibody that binds to IgE, preventing it from binding to mast cells.

Front: First-line medication to treat a stable patient with Pneumocystis jirovecii and a sulfa allergy?
Back: Pentamidine or Atovaquone.

Pulmonology Part 15: Clinical "Buzzwords" & Physical Exam
Front: Physical exam finding: "Velcro-like" inspiratory crackles?
Back: Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis.

Front: Egophony (E to A change) indicates?
Back: Lung consolidation (Pneumonia).

Front: Definition of "Whispered Pectoriloquy"?
Back: Whispered sounds are heard clearly through the stethoscope, indicating consolidation.

Front: Physical exam finding in a large Pleural Effusion?
Back: Decreased tactile fremitus, dullness to percussion, and decreased breath sounds.

Front: Tracheal deviation AWAY from the affected side?
Back: Tension Pneumothorax or Large Pleural Effusion.

Front: Tracheal deviation TOWARD the affected side?
Back: Atelectasis or Pneumonectomy.

Front: Meaning of "Tactile Fremitus" in Pneumothorax?
Back: Decreased (air in the pleural space blocks vibration).

Front: Meaning of "Tactile Fremitus" in Pneumonia?
Back: Increased (solid/liquid medium conducts vibration better).

Pulmonology Part 16: Sleep Medicine & Ventilation Nuances
Front: Distinction between OSA and Central Sleep Apnea (CSA)?
Back: CSA lacks respiratory effort (no chest wall movement); OSA has respiratory effort against an obstruction.

Front: "Cheyne-Stokes" respiration is commonly seen in?
Back: Heart Failure or Stroke (form of Central Sleep Apnea).

Front: Primary risk factor for Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome (Pickwickian)?
Back: BMI ≥30.

Front: Definition of Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome?
Back: Daytime hypercapnia (PaCO2 >45 mmHg) in an obese patient not explained by other causes.

Front: What is "Veno-venous (VV) ECMO" used for?
Back: Severe ARDS refractory to conventional mechanical ventilation.

Front: Target PaO2 for a patient with ARDS?
Back: 55–80 mmHg (avoiding oxygen toxicity).

Front: Definition of "Permissive Hypercapnia" in ARDS?
Back: Allowing high PaCO2 to maintain low tidal volumes and protect the lungs from barotrauma.

Pulmonology Part 17: Differential Diagnosis "Red Flags"
Front: Unilateral wheezing in a child/toddler?
Back: Foreign body aspiration.

Front: Unilateral wheezing in an adult smoker?
Back: Endobronchial tumor (Lung cancer).

Front: "Sudden onset of dyspnea and pleuritic chest pain in a tall, thin male smoker"?
Back: Primary Spontaneous Pneumothorax.

Front: "Chest pain, dyspnea, and friction rub" 2-3 days after a viral illness?
Back: Pleurisy (Pleuritis).

Front: Recurrent pneumonia in the SAME lung lobe?
Back: Suggests localized obstruction (e.g., Bronchogenic carcinoma or foreign body).

Front: "Bat-wing" or "Butterfly" opacities on CXR?
Back: Pulmonary Edema (CHF).

Front: "Kerley B lines" on CXR indicate?
Back: Increased pulmonary venous pressure (CHF/Pulmonary Edema).

Front: Common cause of "Eggshell calcification" of hilar nodes besides Silicosis?
Back: Sarcoidosis (rarely) or Treated Lymphoma.

Pulmonology Part 18: Critical Lab Values
Front: A pH <7.2 in pleural fluid always necessitates?
Back: Drainage (Chest tube).

Front: What is a "Normal" A-a gradient for a 20-year-old?
Back: ~9 mmHg (Formula: Age/4 + 4).

Front: Meaning of a "high" DLCO?
Back: Alveolar hemorrhage (e.g., Goodpasture) or Asthma.

Front: A PaO2/FiO2 ratio of <100 defines?
Back: Severe ARDS.

Front: Gold standard test for Cystic Fibrosis?
Back: Sweat Chloride Test (>60 mmol/L).


=====================================================================
=====================================================================

Section: Internal Medicine
Topic: Gastroenterology — IBD, GI bleed, liver disease, pancreatitis



Front: Primary site of involvement in Crohn’s Disease?
Back: Terminal ileum (though it can affect any part of the GI tract).

Front: Defining endoscopic appearance of Crohn’s Disease?
Back: Cobblestoning and skip lesions.

Front: Characteristic biopsy finding in Crohn’s Disease?
Back: Non-caseating granulomas.

Front: Most common site for Ulcerative Colitis (UC)?
Back: Rectum (always involved) with continuous proximal spread.

Front: Depth of inflammation in Ulcerative Colitis?
Back: Mucosa and submucosa only.

Front: Depth of inflammation in Crohn’s Disease?
Back: Transmural inflammation.

Front: Radiographic sign associated with chronic Ulcerative Colitis?
Back: "Lead pipe" appearance due to loss of haustra.

Front: Serologic marker often positive in UC?
Back: p-ANCA.

Front: Serologic marker often positive in Crohn’s Disease?
Back: ASCA (Anti-Saccharomyces cerevisiae antibodies).

Front: Most common fistula type in Crohn's Disease?
Back: Perianal fistula.

Front: IBD subtype associated with Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis (PSC)?
Back: Ulcerative Colitis.

Front: Extra-intestinal manifestation common to both UC and Crohn's?
Back: Erythema nodosum or Pyoderma gangrenosum.

Front: Maintenance therapy for mild-to-moderate UC?
Back: 5-ASA compounds (Mesalamine, Sulfasalazine).

Front: Standard treatment for an acute IBD flare?
Back: Corticosteroids (Prednisone or IV Methylprednisolone).

Front: Curative surgical procedure for Ulcerative Colitis?
Back: Total proctocolectomy.

Part 2: Gastrointestinal Bleeding (GIB)
Front: Anatomical landmark separating Upper from Lower GI bleed?
Back: Ligament of Treitz.

Front: Most common cause of Upper GI Bleed (UGIB)?
Back: Peptic Ulcer Disease (PUD).

Front: Primary diagnostic test for suspected UGIB?
Back: Upper Endoscopy (EGD).

Front: Laboratory ratio suggestive of UGIB?
Back: Elevated BUN/Creatinine ratio (>20:1).

Front: Medication to initiate immediately in suspected Variceal Bleed?
Back: Octreotide (somatostatin analog).

Front: Prophylactic antibiotic class for cirrhotic patients with UGIB?
Back: Fluoroquinolones or Ceftriaxone.

Front: Most common cause of Lower GI Bleed (LGIB) in adults >60?
Back: Diverticulosis.

Front: Presentation of bleeding from Angiodysplasia?
Back: Painless hematochezia in elderly patients (often associated with aortic stenosis).

Front: First step in managing a hemodynamically unstable GI bleed?
Back: Two large-bore IVs and fluid resuscitation.

Front: Definitive management for refractory esophageal variceal bleeding?
Back: TIPS (Transjugular Intrahepatic Portosystemic Shunt).

Front: Common cause of UGIB following forceful vomiting?
Back: Mallory-Weiss Tear.

Front: Definition of "Hematochezia"?
Back: Bright red blood per rectum, usually indicating a lower GI source.

Front: Definition of "Melena"?
Back: Black, tarry stools indicating digested blood, usually from an upper GI source.

Front: Finding on EGD that suggests a high risk of ulcer re-bleeding?
Back: Visible vessel or adherent clot (Forrest classification).

Part 3: Liver Disease & Cirrhosis
Front: Physical exam finding specific to hepatic encephalopathy?
Back: Asterixis (flapping tremor).

Front: Standard medical treatment for Hepatic Encephalopathy?
Back: Lactulose (to trap ammonia) and Rifaximin.

Front: Common cause of Budd-Chiari Syndrome?
Back: Hepatic vein thrombosis.

Front: SAAG score indicating portal hypertension-related ascites?
Back: SAAG ≥ 1.1 g/dL.

Front: Primary screening test for Hepatocellular Carcinoma (HCC)?
Back: Ultrasound and Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) every 6 months.

Front: Gold standard for diagnosing Wilson’s Disease?
Back: Increased 24-hour urinary copper or Liver biopsy.

Front: Triad of Hemochromatosis?
Back: Cirrhosis, Diabetes ("Bronze Diabetes"), and Skin hyperpigmentation.

Front: Genetic mutation associated with Hemochromatosis?
Back: HFE gene (C282Y).

Front: First-line treatment for Hereditary Hemochromatosis?
Back: Therapeutic phlebotomy.

Front: Classic laboratory finding in Alcoholic Liver Disease?
Back: AST:ALT ratio > 2:1.

Front: Diagnostic criteria for Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis (SBP)?
Back: Ascitic fluid Absolute Neutrophil Count (ANC) > 250 cells/mm³.

Front: Empiric antibiotic for SBP?
Back: Third-generation cephalosporin (Cefotaxime).

Front: Primary Biliary Cholangitis (PBC) target population?
Back: Middle-aged women.

Front: Hallmark antibody for Primary Biliary Cholangitis?
Back: Anti-mitochondrial antibody (AMA).

Front: Key laboratory abnormality in Nonalcoholic Fatty Liver Disease (NAFLD)?
Back: ALT > AST (usually in the setting of metabolic syndrome).

Part 4: Pancreatitis
Front: Two most common causes of Acute Pancreatitis?
Back: Gallstones and Alcohol.

Front: Most specific serum enzyme for Acute Pancreatitis?
Back: Lipase.

Front: Diagnostic imaging of choice for Acute Pancreatitis?
Back: Abdominal CT with contrast (though often clinical diagnosis).

Front: Criteria used to predict severity/mortality in Pancreatitis?
Back: Ranson’s Criteria or APACHE II score.

Front: Classic physical sign of hemorrhagic pancreatitis (periumbilical)?
Back: Cullen’s sign.

Front: Classic physical sign of hemorrhagic pancreatitis (flank)?
Back: Grey Turner’s sign.

Front: Most common complication of Acute Pancreatitis (weeks later)?
Back: Pancreatic pseudocyst.

Front: Classic triad of Chronic Pancreatitis?
Back: Steatorrhea, Diabetes Mellitus, and Pancreatic calcifications.

Front: Best initial test for Chronic Pancreatitis?
Back: Abdominal X-ray or CT showing calcifications.

Front: Treatment for malabsorption in Chronic Pancreatitis?
Back: Pancreatic enzyme supplementation (Lipase/Amylase/Protease).

Front: Major risk factor for Pancreatic Adenocarcinoma?
Back: Smoking.

Front: Tumor marker for Pancreatic Cancer?
Back: CA 19-9.

Front: Painless jaundice and a palpable gallbladder (Courvoisier sign) suggests?
Back: Pancreatic Head Carcinoma.

Part 5: Advanced GI & Hepatobiliary Integration
Front: Most common cause of jaundice in pregnancy?
Back: Viral hepatitis.

Front: Management of asymptomatic gallstones?
Back: Observation (no surgery).

Front: Treatment of choice for Choledocholithiasis?
Back: ERCP (Endoscopic Retrograde Cholangiopancreatography).

Front: Charcot’s Triad for Ascending Cholangitis?
Back: Fever, RUQ pain, and Jaundice.

Front: Reynolds’ Pentad (for Cholangitis)?
Back: Charcot’s Triad + Hypotension + Altered Mental Status.

Front: Primary risk factor for Esophageal Adenocarcinoma?
Back: Barrett’s Esophagus (from chronic GERD).

Front: Type of esophageal cancer associated with smoking and alcohol?
Back: Squamous Cell Carcinoma.

Front: First-line medication for symptomatic GERD?
Back: Proton Pump Inhibitors (PPIs).

Front: Gold standard for diagnosing GERD (if diagnosis unclear)?
Back: 24-hour pH monitoring.

Front: Best test to confirm H. pylori eradication?
Back: Urea Breath Test or Stool Antigen Test.

Front: Classic laboratory finding in Autoimmune Hepatitis?
Back: Anti-smooth muscle antibodies (ASMA).

Front: Treatment for Wilson's Disease?
Back: Penicillamine or Trientine (Copper chelators).

Front: Definitive treatment for Alpha-1 Antitrypsin deficiency liver disease?
Back: Liver transplantation.

Front: Medication used to decrease portal pressure in Cirrhosis?
Back: Non-selective beta-blockers (Propranolol, Nadolol).

Front: Most common cause of Small Bowel Obstruction (SBO)?
Back: Post-surgical adhesions.

Front: Most common cause of Large Bowel Obstruction?
Back: Colorectal cancer.

Front: Presentation of Achalasia on Barium Swallow?
Back: "Bird’s beak" tapering of the distal esophagus.

Front: Pathophysiology of Achalasia?
Back: Loss of myenteric (Auerbach) plexus in the distal esophagus.

Front: Initial treatment for C. difficile infection?
Back: Oral Vancomycin or Fidaxomicin.

Front: Complication of C. difficile characterized by colonic dilation?
Back: Toxic Megacolon.

Front: Classic biopsy finding in Celiac Disease?
Back: Villous atrophy and crypt hyperplasia.

Front: Highly sensitive antibody for Celiac Disease?
Back: Anti-tissue transglutaminase (tTG) IgA.

Front: Condition associated with Dermatitis Herpetiformis?
Back: Celiac Disease.

Front: Screening recommendation for Colon Cancer (average risk)?
Back: Colonoscopy every 10 years starting at age 45.

Front: First-line management for Zenker’s Diverticulum?
Back: Surgical cricopharyngeal myotomy.

Front: Diagnostic finding in Boerhaave Syndrome?
Back: Pneumomediastinum (air in the mediastinum) on X-ray.

Front: Difference between Mallory-Weiss and Boerhaave?
Back: Mallory-Weiss is a mucosal tear; Boerhaave is a full-thickness rupture.

Front: Primary electrolyte abnormality in refeeding syndrome?
Back: Hypophosphatemia.

Front: Presentation of Vitamin B12 deficiency in GI disease?
Back: Megaloblastic anemia and subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord.

Front: GI disorder associated with "string sign" on contrast imaging?
Back: Crohn's Disease (terminal ileum narrowing).

Front: Risk factor for Porcelain Gallbladder?
Back: Chronic cholecystitis (high risk for gallbladder carcinoma).

Front: Most common cause of drug-induced liver injury in the US?
Back: Acetaminophen.

Front: Antidote for Acetaminophen toxicity?
Back: N-acetylcysteine (NAC).

Front: Serum marker for primary hepatocellular carcinoma?
Back: Alpha-fetoprotein (AFP).

Front: Key ultrasound finding for Acute Cholecystitis?
Back: Gallbladder wall thickening and pericholecystic fluid.

Front: Most sensitive test for Acute Cholecystitis?
Back: HIDA scan (Cholescintigraphy).

Front: Treatment for primary biliary cholangitis?
Back: Ursodeoxycholic acid.

Front: Defining feature of Dubin-Johnson syndrome?
Back: Conjugated hyperbilirubinemia and a "black liver."

Front: Cause of Gilbert Syndrome?
Back: Reduced UGT1A1 activity leading to unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia during stress.

Front: Major risk of Crigler-Najjar Syndrome Type I?
Back: Kernicterus (due to absent UGT enzyme).

Front: Management of a Pancreatic Pseudocyst <6cm and asymptomatic?
Back: Observation/Expectant management.

Front: Complication of TPN (Total Parenteral Nutrition)?
Back: Gallstones/Biliary sludge.

Front: Primary cause of Mesenteric Ischemia?
Back: Embolism (often from Atrial Fibrillation).

Front: Clinical presentation of Chronic Mesenteric Ischemia?
Back: "Abdominal angina" (post-prandial pain) and weight loss.

Front: Most common benign liver tumor?
Back: Hemangioma.

Front: Liver tumor associated with Oral Contraceptive Use?
Back: Hepatic Adenoma.

Front: Treatment for Hepatic Adenoma >5cm?
Back: Surgical resection (due to rupture/malignancy risk).

Front: Hallmark of Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome?
Back: Gastrinoma leading to multiple, refractory peptic ulcers.

Front: Screening test for Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome?
Back: Serum gastrin levels (>1000 pg/mL).

Front: Confirmatory test for Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome?
Back: Secretin stimulation test (gastrin levels remain high).

Front: Major site for Gastrinomas?
Back: Pancreas or Duodenum (Gastrinoma Triangle).

Front: Primary management of Boerhaave Syndrome?
Back: Emergent surgical repair.

Front: Classic presentation of Ischemic Colitis?
Back: Pain followed by bloody diarrhea in the "watershed" areas (splenic flexure).

Front: Finding on CT for Ischemic Colitis?
Back: "Thumbprinting" (mucosal edema).

Front: Screening for patients with FAP (Familial Adenomatous Polyposis)?
Back: Annual sigmoidoscopy starting at age 10-12.

Front: Diagnostic finding for Whipple's Disease?
Back: PAS-positive macrophages in the lamina propria.

Front: Treatment for Whipple's Disease?
Back: Ceftriaxone followed by TMP-SMX for one year.

Front: Definition of Barrett's Esophagus?
Back: Intestinal metaplasia (Squamous to Columnar epithelium).

Front: Most common cause of viral hepatitis worldwide?
Back: Hepatitis B (HBV).

Front: Serologic marker indicating active Hepatitis B replication?
Back: HBeAg.

Front: Serologic marker indicating immunity to Hepatitis B (vaccination)?
Back: Anti-HBs only.

Front: Serologic marker indicating immunity to Hepatitis B (prior infection)?
Back: Anti-HBs and Anti-HBc (IgG).

Front: First-line treatment for Hepatitis C (HCV)?
Back: Direct-acting antivirals (e.g., Sofosbuvir).

Front: Standard of care for achalasia if myotomy is refused?
Back: Botulinum toxin injection or pneumatic dilation.

Front: Most common cause of secretory diarrhea?
Back: Vibrio cholerae or Enterotoxigenic E. coli (ETEC).

Front: Osmotic gap in Osmotic Diarrhea?
Back: High (>125 mOsm/kg).

Front: Common cause of esophageal webs and iron deficiency anemia?
Back: Plummer-Vinson Syndrome.

Front: Highest risk factor for Gastric Adenocarcinoma?
Back: H. pylori infection.

Front: Physical exam sign for Gastric Cancer metastasis to the supraclavicular node?
Back: Virchow's Node.

Front: Physical exam sign for Gastric Cancer metastasis to the periumbilical area?
Back: Sister Mary Joseph nodule.

Front: Presentation of Menetrier's Disease?
Back: Hyperplastic gastropathy, protein loss, and "brain-like" rugae.

Front: Most common site for a Diverticulum?
Back: Sigmoid colon.

Front: Management of uncomplicated Diverticulitis?
Back: Bowel rest and antibiotics (Ciprofloxacin + Metronidazole).

Front: Complication of Diverticulitis requiring surgery?
Back: Abscess, perforation, or fistula.

Front: Best imaging for Diverticulitis?
Back: CT scan with oral and IV contrast.

Front: Why avoid Colonoscopy during acute Diverticulitis?
Back: High risk of perforation.

Front: Characteristic of Meckel’s Diverticulum?
Back: Rule of 2s (2 inches long, 2 feet from ileocecal valve, 2% of population).

Front: Diagnostic test for Meckel’s Diverticulum?
Back: Technetium-99m pertechnetate scan ("Meckel scan").

Front: Common cause of Small Intestinal Bacterial Overgrowth (SIBO)?
Back: Dysmotility (e.g., Scleroderma, Diabetes) or Roux-en-Y surgery.

Front: Gold standard diagnosis for SIBO?
Back: Jejunal aspirate culture (though breath tests are common).

Front: Treatment for SIBO?
Back: Rifaximin.

Front: Mechanism of Lactulose in Cirrhosis?
Back: Converted to lactic acid, acidifying the gut and converting NH3 to NH4+.

Front: Most common cause of Budd-Chiari in young women?
Back: Oral contraceptive use or underlying hypercoagulability.

Front: Major risk of Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis?
Back: Cholangiocarcinoma.

Front: "Onion skin" fibrosis of bile ducts is seen in?
Back: Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis.

Front: Finding on ERCP for PSC?
Back: "Beading" of the intrahepatic and extrahepatic bile ducts.

Front: Management of Gastric Varices in the absence of esophageal varices?
Back: Splenic vein thrombosis (often due to pancreatitis).

Front: Treatment for Splenic Vein Thrombosis?
Back: Splenectomy.

Front: Leading cause of death in patients with Cirrhosis?
Back: Variceal hemorrhage or Hepatocellular carcinoma.


Front: First marker to appear in acute Hepatitis B infection?
Back: HBsAg (Hepatitis B surface antigen).

Front: Only marker present during the "Window Period" of Hep B?
Back: Anti-HBc IgM.

Front: Marker indicating high infectivity and active viral replication in HBV?
Back: HBeAg (Envelope antigen).

Front: Presence of Anti-HBs and Anti-HBc IgG indicates?
Back: Recovery from natural Hepatitis B infection and lifelong immunity.

Front: Presence of Anti-HBs alone (without Anti-HBc) indicates?
Back: Immunity via Hepatitis B vaccination.

Front: Requirement for Hepatitis D (Delta agent) infection?
Back: Co-infection or superinfection with Hepatitis B (requires HBsAg).

Front: Mode of transmission for Hepatitis A and E?
Back: Fecal-oral route ("The vowels hit the bowels").

Front: Hepatitis virus with high mortality rate in pregnant women?
Back: Hepatitis E.

Front: Definition of Chronic Hepatitis B?
Back: HBsAg persistence for >6 months.

Part 7: Gastric Pathologies & PUD
Front: Most common cause of chronic gastritis?
Back: H. pylori infection.

Front: Location of H. pylori colonization in the stomach?
Back: Antrum.

Front: Type of Gastritis involving the fundus and associated with Pernicious Anemia?
Back: Autoimmune Metaplastic Atrophic Gastritis (Type A).

Front: Mechanism of Pernicious Anemia in Type A Gastritis?
Back: Antibodies against Parietal cells and Intrinsic Factor.

Front: Complication of H. pylori besides PUD?
Back: MALT Lymphoma and Gastric Adenocarcinoma.

Front: First-line "Triple Therapy" for H. pylori?
Back: PPI + Amoxicillin + Clarithromycin.

Front: Preferred H. pylori treatment in areas with high Clarithromycin resistance?
Back: Bismuth Quadruple Therapy (PPI + Bismuth + Metronidazole + Tetracycline).

Front: Classic symptom of a Duodenal Ulcer?
Back: Pain that improves with food.

Front: Classic symptom of a Gastric Ulcer?
Back: Pain that worsens with food (leads to weight loss).

Front: Most common location for a Gastric Cancer?
Back: Lesser curvature of the antrum.

Part 8: Malabsorption & Small Bowel
Front: Pathophysiology of Celiac Disease?
Back: Immune-mediated damage to small bowel villi due to gluten exposure (gliadin).

Front: Skin manifestation of Celiac Disease?
Back: Dermatitis Herpetiformis (IgA deposits at dermal papillae).

Front: Definitive diagnosis for Celiac Disease?
Back: Small bowel biopsy showing villous atrophy.

Front: Findings in Tropical Sprue compared to Celiac?
Back: Similar villous atrophy but affects the entire small bowel (Celiac is distal duo/proximal jejunum).

Front: Diagnostic test for Lactose Intolerance?
Back: Hydrogen Breath Test.

Front: Stool findings in Malabsorption/Steatorrhea?
Back: Increased fecal fat (>7g/day) and decreased Sudam III stain.

Front: Vitamin deficiency common after ileal resection?
Back: Vitamin B12 and Bile Acid deficiency.

Front: Classic presentation of Whipple’s Disease?
Back: Diarrhea, Weight loss, Arthralgia, and Neurological symptoms.

Front: Characteristic of Small Intestinal Bacterial Overgrowth (SIBO)?
Back: Premature deconjugation of bile acids leading to fat malabsorption.

Part 9: Vascular & Structural Disorders
Front: Definition of "Angiodysplasia"?
Back: Tortuous, dilated submucosal veins; common cause of painless hematochezia in elderly.

Front: Risk factors for Ischemic Colitis?
Back: Hypotension (shock), vasculitis, or extreme exercise (marathons).

Front: Most common site for Ischemic Colitis?
Back: Splenic flexure (Griffith’s point) and Rectosigmoid junction (Sudek’s point).

Front: Presentation of Acute Mesenteric Ischemia?
Back: "Pain out of proportion to exam" in a patient with Atrial Fibrillation.

Front: Management of a Sigmoid Volvulus?
Back: Sigmoidoscopic decompression (detorsion).

Front: Radiographic appearance of Sigmoid Volvulus?
Back: "Coffee bean" sign on X-ray.

Front: Definitive treatment for recurrent Sigmoid Volvulus?
Back: Elective sigmoid resection.

Front: Most common site for an indirect inguinal hernia?
Back: Through the deep inguinal ring (lateral to inferior epigastric vessels).

Part 10: Advanced Liver & Biliary (Sleisenger/Harrison level)
Front: Pathophysiology of Hepatorenal Syndrome?
Back: Splanchnic vasodilation leading to renal vasoconstriction and failure.

Front: Treatment for Hepatorenal Syndrome?
Back: Midodrine, Octreotide, and Albumin.

Front: Best initial step for a patient with jaundice and dilated ducts on US?
Back: ERCP or MRCP.

Front: Primary risk factor for Gallbladder Adenocarcinoma?
Back: Chronic cholelithiasis and Porcelain Gallbladder.

Front: Management of symptomatic cholelithiasis in a pregnant patient?
Back: Conservative management first, then cholecystectomy in 2nd trimester if needed.

Front: Expected lab findings in Extrahepatic Biliary Obstruction?
Back: Elevated Conjugated Bilirubin, High Alk Phos, and High GGT.

Front: What does an isolated elevation of Alk Phos (normal GGT) suggest?
Back: Bone disease or pregnancy, not liver/biliary disease.

Front: Cause of "Pre-hepatic" jaundice?
Back: Hemolysis (excess Unconjugated Bilirubin).

Front: Definition of Gilbert Syndrome?
Back: Mildly decreased UGT activity; asymptomatic jaundice during stress/fasting.

Part 11: Miscellaneous High-Yield GI
Front: Most common cause of lower GI bleed in children?
Back: Meckel’s Diverticulum.

Front: Cause of a "Corkscrew Esophagus" on barium swallow?
Back: Diffuse Esophageal Spasm (DES).

Front: First-line treatment for Diffuse Esophageal Spasm?
Back: Calcium channel blockers or Nitrates.

Front: Criteria for diagnosing Irritable Bowel Syndrome (IBS)?
Back: Rome IV Criteria (recurrent abdominal pain 1 day/week for 3 months).

Front: Alarm symptoms in IBS that warrant further workup?
Back: Weight loss, nocturnal diarrhea, blood in stool, or age >50.

Front: Management of Ogilvie Syndrome (Colonic Pseudo-obstruction)?
Back: Neostigmine or colonic decompression.

Front: Most common cause of Boerhaave Syndrome?
Back: latrogenic (endoscopy) or forceful retching.

Front: Characteristic finding in Intussusception?
Back: "Currant jelly" stools and "Target sign" on ultrasound.

Front: Primary management of Intussusception in children?
Back: Air or Contrast enema (both diagnostic and therapeutic).

Part 12: Pancreatic & Biliary Complications
Front: Lab marker to differentiate Pancreatitis from other causes of RUQ pain?
Back: Serum Lipase >3x upper limit of normal.

Front: Management of infected pancreatic necrosis?
Back: Antibiotics (Meropenem) and delayed necrosectomy.

Front: Most common location of a Gastrinoma?
Back: Duodenum (60-90%).

Front: Association of Gastrinomas with which genetic syndrome?
Back: MEN1 (Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 1).

Front: Best test to visualize the "Onion Skinning" of PSC?
Back: Liver biopsy (though MRCP/ERCP are often used for diagnosis).

Front: Drug that can cause drug-induced pancreatitis?
Back: Valproic acid, Diuretics (Furosemide, Thiazides), Azathioprine.

Front: Classic electrolyte complication of Acute Pancreatitis?
Back: Hypocalcemia (due to saponification in fat necrosis).

Part 13: Colorectal & Anorectal
Front: Definition of Internal Hemorrhoids?
Back: Above the dentate line; painless bleeding.

Front: Definition of External Hemorrhoids?
Back: Below the dentate line; painful (especially if thrombosed).

Front: First-line treatment for Anal Fissures?
Back: Fiber, stool softeners, and topical nitroglycerin or diltiazem.

Front: Most common location for an Anal Fissure?
Back: Posterior midline.

Front: Lynch Syndrome (HNPCC) genetic defect?
Back: DNA Mismatch Repair (MMR) genes (MLH1, MSH2).

Front: Extracolonic cancers associated with Lynch Syndrome?
Back: Endometrial, Ovarian, and Gastric cancer.

Front: Gardner Syndrome presentation?
Back: FAP + Osteomas + Soft tissue tumors + Impacted teeth.

Front: Peutz-Jeghers Syndrome features?
Back: Hamartomatous polyps + Hyperpigmented macules on lips/buccal mucosa.

Section: Quick-Fire Integration
Front: Jaundice + Weight Loss + Palpable Gallbladder?
Back: Pancreatic Cancer (Courvoisier sign).

Front: RUQ Pain + Jaundice + Fever?
Back: Acute Cholangitis.

Front: Cirrhosis + New onset Fever + Abdominal Pain?
Back: Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis (SBP).

Front: Heartburn + Dysphagia to solids + History of asthma/atopy?
Back: Eosinophilic Esophagitis.

Front: Endoscopic finding in Eosinophilic Esophagitis?
Back: Stacked rings or linear furrows ("feline esophagus").

Front: Treatment for Eosinophilic Esophagitis?
Back: Dietary modification or swallowed aerosolized steroids (Fluticasone).

Front: Primary mechanism of Ascites in Cirrhosis?
Back: Portal hypertension leading to splanchnic vasodilation and decreased effective arterial volume.

Front: Most common cause of death in patients with Hemochromatosis?
Back: Hepatocellular Carcinoma (HCC).

Front: Defining characteristic of Type 1 Hepatorenal Syndrome (HRS)?
Back: Rapidly progressive renal failure (doubling of Cr >2.5 in <2 weeks).

Front: SAAG < 1.1 g/dL with high protein (>2.5) in ascitic fluid suggests?
Back: Malignancy, Nephrotic syndrome, or TB peritonitis.

Front: Treatment for symptomatic Grade II/III Hepatic Encephalopathy?
Back: Lactulose titrated to 2-3 soft bowel movements per day.

Front: Indication for adding Rifaximin in Hepatic Encephalopathy?
Back: Prevention of recurrence after a second episode.

Front: Most common organism causing Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis (SBP)?
Back: E. coli.

Front: Long-term prophylaxis for SBP after the first episode?
Back: Daily Ciprofloxacin or Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole.

Front: MELD score components?
Back: Bilirubin, Creatinine, INR, and Sodium.

Front: Use of the MELD score?
Back: Predicting 3-month mortality and prioritizing liver transplant candidates.

Part 15: IBD — Nuanced Management & Complications
Front: IBD type associated with "Creeping Fat" on gross specimen?
Back: Crohn’s Disease.

Front: Highest risk factor for Toxic Megacolon?
Back: Ulcerative Colitis (though it can occur in Crohn's or C. diff).

Front: Initial imaging for suspected Toxic Megacolon?
Back: Abdominal X-ray (showing colonic diameter >6 cm).

Front: Contraindicated procedures in acute Toxic Megacolon?
Back: Colonoscopy and Barium enema (due to perforation risk).

Front: Maintenance therapy for Crohn’s Disease if 5-ASA fails?
Back: Azathioprine, 6-Mercaptopurine, or Anti-TNF (Infliximab).

Front: IBD medication requiring TPMT enzyme activity testing before start?
Back: Azathioprine / 6-Mercaptopurine.

Front: Most common site of stricture formation in Crohn’s?
Back: Terminal Ileum.

Front: Surveillance colonoscopy timing for UC patients?
Back: Starting 8 years after diagnosis, then every 1-2 years.

Front: Extraintestinal manifestation of IBD that does NOT correlate with bowel activity?
Back: Ankylosing spondylitis and Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis.

Part 16: Pancreatitis — Advanced Management
Front: Most common cause of death in the first week of Acute Pancreatitis?
Back: Systemic Inflammatory Response Syndrome (SIRS) and multiorgan failure.

Front: Most common cause of death AFTER the first week of Acute Pancreatitis?
Back: Pancreatic infection (Infected Necrosis).

Front: Preferred fluid for resuscitation in Acute Pancreatitis?
Back: Lactated Ringer’s (associated with lower SIRS risk than Normal Saline).

Front: "Sentinel Loop" on abdominal X-ray indicates?
Back: Localized ileus of the jejunum associated with nearby Pancreatitis.

Front: Definition of "Chronic" Pancreatitis?
Back: Irreversible structural damage leading to exocrine and endocrine insufficiency.

Front: Gold standard test for Pancreatic Exocrine Insufficiency?
Back: 72-hour fecal fat collection (though Fecal Elastase-1 is the common initial test).

Front: Classic pain description for Pancreatic Cancer?
Back: Dull epigastric pain radiating to the back, worse at night.

Front: Trousseau’s Syndrome in Pancreatic Cancer?
Back: Migratory superficial thrombophlebitis.

Part 17: GI Bleeding — Special Scenarios
Front: Management of a "Dieulafoy Lesion"?
Back: Endoscopic clipping or thermal coagulation (it is a large submucosal artery).

Front: Presentation of "Aortoenteric Fistula"?
Back: Brisk "herald bleed" followed by massive hematochezia in a patient with a prior aortic graft.

Front: Primary location of an Aortoenteric Fistula?
Back: Third part of the duodenum.

Front: Scintigraphy (Tagged RBC Scan) is used for what GIB scenario?
Back: Detecting slow or intermittent LGIB when colonoscopy is inconclusive.

Front: Most common cause of UGIB in a patient with chronic NSAID use?
Back: Gastric ulcer (NSAIDs inhibit prostaglandin synthesis).

Front: Cameron Lesions are found in what structural abnormality?
Back: Hiatal hernia (linear erosions in the gastric folds).

Front: Key management for bleeding secondary to Mallory-Weiss tear?
Back: Most stop spontaneously; if not, epinephrine injection or clipping.

Part 18: Liver Disease — Immunology & Metabolism
Front: High Serum Ceruloplasmin levels are found in?
Back: Actually, Ceruloplasmin is low in Wilson's Disease.

Front: Kayser-Fleischer rings are found in which layer of the cornea?
Back: Descemet’s membrane.

Front: Most common liver disease in the Western world?
Back: Nonalcoholic Fatty Liver Disease (NAFLD).

Front: Histological hallmark of Alcoholic Hepatitis?
Back: Mallory-Denk bodies (intracytoplasmic eosinophilic inclusions).

Front: Medical treatment for severe Alcoholic Hepatitis (Maddrey Score >32)?
Back: Prednisolone (Corticosteroids).

Front: Drug used in Alcoholic Hepatitis if steroids are contraindicated?
Back: Pentoxifylline.

Front: Difference between PBC and PSC location?
Back: PBC is intrahepatic ducts only; PSC is both intra- and extrahepatic.

Part 19: Structural & Functional Integration
Front: Diagnosis for "Doughnut sign" or "Target sign" on CT?
Back: Intussusception.

Front: Most common site for Volvulus in the elderly?
Back: Sigmoid colon.

Front: Most common site for Volvulus in children?
Back: Midgut (associated with malrotation).

Front: "Whirl sign" on CT abdomen indicates?
Back: Volvulus (torsion of the mesentery).

Front: Pathophysiology of Zenker’s Diverticulum?
Back: Herniation through Killian’s triangle (weakness in the pharyngeal constrictor).

Final Section: Step 2 CK "Best Next Step" Rapid Fire
Front: Best next step: Patient with cirrhosis, ascites, and altered mental status?
Back: Diagnostic paracentesis (rule out SBP).

Front: Best next step: Hematochezia in a patient over 50 with stable vitals?
Back: Colonoscopy.

Front: Best next step: Suspected Peptic Ulcer perforation?
Back: Upright Chest X-ray (look for free air under the diaphragm).

Front: Best next step: Post-ERCP patient with severe epigastric pain?
Back: Check serum Amylase/Lipase (Post-ERCP pancreatitis).

Front: Best next step: Jaundice, pruritus, and elevated Alk Phos?
Back: Right upper quadrant ultrasound.

Front: IBD-associated skin lesion that shows pathergy (worsens with trauma)?
Back: Pyoderma gangrenosum.

Front: Most common vitamin deficiency in Crohn's disease patients with ileal involvement?
Back: Vitamin B12 (leading to macrocytic anemia).

Front: Type of kidney stone most common in Crohn’s disease?
Back: Calcium Oxalate stones (due to increased oxalate absorption).

Front: Gallstone type associated with Crohn’s disease?
Back: Cholesterol stones (due to decreased bile acid resorption in the ileum).

Front: Primary site of inflammation in microscopic colitis?
Back: Colon (appears endoscopically normal but shows inflammation on biopsy).

Front: Common trigger for Microscopic Colitis?
Back: NSAIDs, PPIs, or Sertraline.

Front: Drug of choice for treating Microscopic Colitis?
Back: Oral Budesonide.

Front: Most common complication of an ileal pouch-anal anastomosis (IPAA)?
Back: Pouchitis.

Front: Initial treatment for Pouchitis?
Back: Ciprofloxacin or Metronidazole.

Part 21: GI Bleeding — Nuance & Management
Front: Standard of care for preventing re-bleeding in non-variceal peptic ulcers?
Back: High-dose IV Proton Pump Inhibitors (PPI) for 72 hours.

Front: Most common cause of a "brisk" Upper GI Bleed in a patient with Cirrhosis?
Back: Esophageal varices.

Front: Mechanism of Octreotide in variceal bleeding?
Back: Causes splanchnic vasoconstriction, reducing portal venous pressure.

Front: Next step if endoscopic therapy fails twice to control a variceal bleed?
Back: TIPS (Transjugular Intrahepatic Portosystemic Shunt).

Front: Contraindication for TIPS procedure?
Back: Severe Congestive Heart Failure or Hepatic Encephalopathy.

Front: Appearance of Gastric Antral Vascular Ectasia (GAVE) on endoscopy?
Back: "Watermelon stomach" (streaky redness in the antrum).

Front: Presentation of Blue Rubber Bleb Nevus Syndrome?
Back: Multiple cavernous hemangiomas of the skin and GI tract leading to chronic GIB.

Part 22: Liver Disease — Advanced Diagnostics
Front: Mnemonic for the causes of Acute Liver Failure?
Back: A-B-C-D-E-F (Acetaminophen/Amanita, Hep B, Hep C, Delta/Drugs, Eschemia, Fatty liver).

Front: Definition of "Fulminant Hepatic Failure"?
Back: Acute liver injury with encephalopathy and coagulopathy (INR >1.5) within 8 weeks of jaundice.

Front: Most common cause of jaundice in the first 24 hours of life?
Back: Hemolytic disease (Pathological).

Front: Histological finding in Alpha-1 Antitrypsin deficiency?
Back: PAS-positive, diastase-resistant globules in hepatocytes.

Front: Lab finding that differentiates Alcoholic Hepatitis from Viral Hepatitis?
Back: AST and ALT are rarely >500 U/L in alcoholic disease.

Front: Medication used to reduce pruritus in Primary Biliary Cholangitis?
Back: Cholestyramine (bile acid sequestrant).

Front: Association between HLA-B8/DR3 and which liver disease?
Back: Autoimmune Hepatitis.

Front: Presentation of Hepatic Hydrothorax?
Back: Right-sided pleural effusion in a cirrhotic patient without primary lung disease.

Part 23: Pancreatitis & Biliary "Gray Areas"
Front: Most common cause of Chronic Pancreatitis in children?
Back: Cystic Fibrosis (CFTR mutations).

Front: Serum marker often elevated in Autoimmune Pancreatitis?
Back: IgG4.

Front: First-line treatment for Autoimmune Pancreatitis?
Back: Corticosteroids.

Front: Diagnosis for a "Double Duct Sign" (dilation of both CBD and pancreatic duct)?
Back: Pancreatic Head Cancer or Ampullary Cancer.

Front: Preferred diagnostic test for suspected Choledocholithiasis when US is equivocal?
Back: MRCP (Magnetic Resonance Cholangiopancreatography).

Front: Most common cause of Pyogenic Liver Abscess?
Back: Biliary tract disease (e.g., ascending cholangitis).

Front: Characteristic CT finding of an Amoebic Liver Abscess?
Back: Single lesion in the right lobe with "anchovy paste" aspirate.

Front: Treatment for Amoebic Liver Abscess?
Back: Metronidazole (Paromomycin follows to clear intraluminal cysts).

Part 24: High-Yield Potpourri
Front: Definition of Ogilvie's Syndrome?
Back: Acute pseudo-obstruction of the colon in hospitalized/elderly patients.

Front: Most common complication of a Meckel's Diverticulum in an adult?
Back: Small bowel obstruction.

Front: Cause of "Pneumatosis Intestinalis" (air in the bowel wall) on X-ray?
Back: Mesenteric ischemia or Necrotizing Enterocolitis (NEC).

Front: Diagnostic finding for Sclerosing Mesenteritis?
Back: "Misty mesentery" on CT scan.

Front: Drug of choice for prophylaxis of NSAID-induced ulcers?
Back: Misoprostol (Prostaglandin E1 analog).

Front: Primary risk of long-term PPI use?
Back: C. difficile infection, hip fractures (decreased Ca absorption), and B12 deficiency.

Front: Hallmark of Dubin-Johnson Syndrome on laparoscopy?
Back: Grossly black/dark liver.

Front: Diagnosis for a patient with RUQ pain, jaundice, and palpable gallbladder?
Back: Courvoisier sign (likely distal malignant biliary obstruction).

Front: Most common site for Gastric Carcinoid tumors?
Back: Gastric body (associated with hypergastrinemia).

Front: Diagnosis for a patient with ascites, hepatomegaly, and RUQ pain after starting OCPs?
Back: Budd-Chiari Syndrome (Hepatic vein thrombosis).

Front: "Nutmeg liver" on gross pathology indicates?
Back: Chronic passive congestion due to Right-Sided Heart Failure.

Front: Primary treatment for a Pyogenic Liver Abscess?
Back: Percutaneous drainage and systemic antibiotics.

Front: Causative organism for "Hydatid Cysts" in the liver?
Back: Echinococcus granulosus.

Front: Pathognomonic CT finding for Echinococcus liver cyst?
Back: Eggshell calcification of the cyst wall.

Front: Why is aspiration contraindicated in suspected Echinococcus cysts?
Back: Risk of anaphylactic shock from leakage of cyst contents.

Front: Most common cause of portal hypertension in the absence of cirrhosis?
Back: Schistosomiasis (worldwide) or Portal Vein Thrombosis.

Front: Diagnosis for a patient with jaundice and "Hamman’s Crunch" (mediastinal emphysema)?
Back: Boerhaave Syndrome (esophageal rupture).

Part 26: Biliary Nuances & Gallbladder Disease
Front: Definition of "Acalculous Cholecystitis"?
Back: Gallbladder inflammation without stones, typically in critically ill/ICU patients.

Front: Best initial treatment for Acalculous Cholecystitis in an unstable patient?
Back: Percutaneous cholecystostomy tube.

Front: "Gallstone Ileus" usually results from a fistula between which two organs?
Back: Gallbladder and Duodenum.

Front: Classic X-ray triad (Rigler’s Triad) for Gallstone Ileus?
Back: Small bowel obstruction, gallstone in ectopic location, and pneumobilia (air in biliary tree).

Front: Most common site of impaction in Gallstone Ileus?
Back: Ileocecal valve.

Front: Finding of "Strawberry Gallbladder" (Cholesterolosis) indicates?
Back: Yellow specks of cholesterol esters within the gallbladder wall (usually asymptomatic).

Front: Primary risk factor for Cholangiocarcinoma in the Western world?
Back: Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis (PSC).

Part 27: Chronic Pancreatitis & Functional Disorders
Front: Most common cause of Chronic Pancreatitis in adults (Western world)?
Back: Alcohol abuse.

Front: Diagnosis for a patient with oily, foul-smelling stools and weight loss?
Back: Steatorrhea (Pancreatic Exocrine Insufficiency).

Front: Best initial step for suspected Pancreatic Exocrine Insufficiency?
Back: Fecal Elastase-1 test.

Front: Treatment for abdominal pain in Chronic Pancreatitis?
Back: Alcohol/smoking cessation, small low-fat meals, and pancreatic enzyme supplementation.

Front: Pathophysiology of Gastroparesis?
Back: Delayed gastric emptying in the absence of mechanical obstruction (common in Diabetes).

Front: Prokinetic agent of choice for Gastroparesis?
Back: Metoclopramide (D2 receptor antagonist) or Erythromycin.

Front: Primary side effect of long-term Metoclopramide?
Back: Tardive dyskinesia.

Part 28: Complex GI Bleeding & Small Bowel Pearls
Front: Diagnosis for "watermelon stomach" appearance in a patient with Systemic Sclerosis?
Back: GAVE (Gastric Antral Vascular Ectasia).

Front: First-line management for GAVE?
Back: Endoscopic Argon Plasma Coagulation (APC).

Front: Most common cause of Small Bowel Bleeding in patients <40 years old?
Back: Meckel’s Diverticulum or IBD.

Front: Most common cause of Small Bowel Bleeding in patients >40 years old?
Back: Angiodysplasia or NSAID-induced ulcers.

Front: Initial step for a patient with Hematochezia and hemodynamic instability?
Back: Rule out an Upper GI source with EGD (Upper GI bleed can present with red blood if brisk).

Front: Interpretation of a "High SAAG" (>1.1) with "Low Protein" (<2.5) in ascites?
Back: Cirrhosis.

Front: Interpretation of a "High SAAG" (>1.1) with "High Protein" (>2.5) in ascites?
Back: Heart Failure or Budd-Chiari Syndrome.

Part 29: Pharmacological Pearls in GI
Front: Mechanism of Ursodeoxycholic acid in PBC?
Back: Decreases cholesterol content of bile and reduces immune-mediated duct damage.

Front: Prophylactic medication for esophageal varices in stable cirrhotics?
Back: Non-selective Beta-blockers (Nadolol or Propranolol).

Front: Mechanism of Terlipressin in Variceal Bleeding?
Back: Vasopressin analogue that causes splanchnic vasoconstriction.

Front: Treatment for Pruritus in cholestatic liver disease?
Back: Cholestyramine, Rifampin, or Naltrexone.

Front: Most common electrolyte side effect of PPIs?
Back: Hypomagnesemia.

Front: Medication that causes "black tongue" and "black stools"?
Back: Bismuth subsalicylate.

Part 30: Step 2 CK "Management Next Steps" Final Fire
Front: Next step for a patient with suspected Achalasia and a "Bird’s Beak" on Barium Swallow?
Back: Esophageal Manometry (to confirm absence of peristalsis and failed LES relaxation).

Front: Next step for a 55-year-old with new-onset GERD?
Back: Upper Endoscopy (to rule out Barrett's or malignancy due to age).

Front: Next step for a patient with a positive H. pylori stool antigen?
Back: Initiate Triple or Quadruple therapy.

Front: Next step after a diagnosis of Colon Cancer is made via biopsy?
Back: CT scan of Chest/Abdomen/Pelvis for staging.

Front: Next step for a cirrhotic patient with a new solid liver mass <1cm on US?
Back: Repeat Ultrasound in 3-4 months.

Front: Next step for a cirrhotic patient with a new solid liver mass >1cm on US?
Back: Triple-phase CT or MRI with contrast (looking for arterial enhancement/venous washout).

Front: Diagnosis for a patient with ascites, hepatomegaly, and RUQ pain after starting OCPs?
Back: Budd-Chiari Syndrome (Hepatic vein thrombosis).

Front: "Nutmeg liver" on gross pathology indicates?
Back: Chronic passive congestion due to Right-Sided Heart Failure.

Front: Primary treatment for a Pyogenic Liver Abscess?
Back: Percutaneous drainage and systemic antibiotics.

Front: Causative organism for "Hydatid Cysts" in the liver?
Back: Echinococcus granulosus.

Front: Pathognomonic CT finding for Echinococcus liver cyst?
Back: Eggshell calcification of the cyst wall.

Front: Why is aspiration contraindicated in suspected Echinococcus cysts?
Back: Risk of anaphylactic shock from leakage of cyst contents.

Front: Most common cause of portal hypertension in the absence of cirrhosis?
Back: Schistosomiasis (worldwide) or Portal Vein Thrombosis.

Front: Diagnosis for a patient with jaundice and "Hamman’s Crunch" (mediastinal emphysema)?
Back: Boerhaave Syndrome (esophageal rupture).

Part 26: Biliary Nuances & Gallbladder Disease
Front: Definition of "Acalculous Cholecystitis"?
Back: Gallbladder inflammation without stones, typically in critically ill/ICU patients.

Front: Best initial treatment for Acalculous Cholecystitis in an unstable patient?
Back: Percutaneous cholecystostomy tube.

Front: "Gallstone Ileus" usually results from a fistula between which two organs?
Back: Gallbladder and Duodenum.

Front: Classic X-ray triad (Rigler’s Triad) for Gallstone Ileus?
Back: Small bowel obstruction, gallstone in ectopic location, and pneumobilia (air in biliary tree).

Front: Most common site of impaction in Gallstone Ileus?
Back: Ileocecal valve.

Front: Finding of "Strawberry Gallbladder" (Cholesterolosis) indicates?
Back: Yellow specks of cholesterol esters within the gallbladder wall (usually asymptomatic).

Front: Primary risk factor for Cholangiocarcinoma in the Western world?
Back: Primary Sclerosing Cholangitis (PSC).

Part 27: Chronic Pancreatitis & Functional Disorders
Front: Most common cause of Chronic Pancreatitis in adults (Western world)?
Back: Alcohol abuse.

Front: Diagnosis for a patient with oily, foul-smelling stools and weight loss?
Back: Steatorrhea (Pancreatic Exocrine Insufficiency).

Front: Best initial step for suspected Pancreatic Exocrine Insufficiency?
Back: Fecal Elastase-1 test.

Front: Treatment for abdominal pain in Chronic Pancreatitis?
Back: Alcohol/smoking cessation, small low-fat meals, and pancreatic enzyme supplementation.

Front: Pathophysiology of Gastroparesis?
Back: Delayed gastric emptying in the absence of mechanical obstruction (common in Diabetes).

Front: Prokinetic agent of choice for Gastroparesis?
Back: Metoclopramide (D2 receptor antagonist) or Erythromycin.

Front: Primary side effect of long-term Metoclopramide?
Back: Tardive dyskinesia.

Part 28: Complex GI Bleeding & Small Bowel Pearls
Front: Diagnosis for "watermelon stomach" appearance in a patient with Systemic Sclerosis?
Back: GAVE (Gastric Antral Vascular Ectasia).

Front: First-line management for GAVE?
Back: Endoscopic Argon Plasma Coagulation (APC).

Front: Most common cause of Small Bowel Bleeding in patients <40 years old?
Back: Meckel’s Diverticulum or IBD.

Front: Most common cause of Small Bowel Bleeding in patients >40 years old?
Back: Angiodysplasia or NSAID-induced ulcers.

Front: Initial step for a patient with Hematochezia and hemodynamic instability?
Back: Rule out an Upper GI source with EGD (Upper GI bleed can present with red blood if brisk).

Front: Interpretation of a "High SAAG" (>1.1) with "Low Protein" (<2.5) in ascites?
Back: Cirrhosis.

Front: Interpretation of a "High SAAG" (>1.1) with "High Protein" (>2.5) in ascites?
Back: Heart Failure or Budd-Chiari Syndrome.

Part 29: Pharmacological Pearls in GI
Front: Mechanism of Ursodeoxycholic acid in PBC?
Back: Decreases cholesterol content of bile and reduces immune-mediated duct damage.

Front: Prophylactic medication for esophageal varices in stable cirrhotics?
Back: Non-selective Beta-blockers (Nadolol or Propranolol).

Front: Mechanism of Terlipressin in Variceal Bleeding?
Back: Vasopressin analogue that causes splanchnic vasoconstriction.

Front: Treatment for Pruritus in cholestatic liver disease?
Back: Cholestyramine, Rifampin, or Naltrexone.

Front: Most common electrolyte side effect of PPIs?
Back: Hypomagnesemia.

Front: Medication that causes "black tongue" and "black stools"?
Back: Bismuth subsalicylate.

Part 30: Step 2 CK "Management Next Steps" Final Fire
Front: Next step for a patient with suspected Achalasia and a "Bird’s Beak" on Barium Swallow?
Back: Esophageal Manometry (to confirm absence of peristalsis and failed LES relaxation).

Front: Next step for a 55-year-old with new-onset GERD?
Back: Upper Endoscopy (to rule out Barrett's or malignancy due to age).

Front: Next step for a patient with a positive H. pylori stool antigen?
Back: Initiate Triple or Quadruple therapy.

Front: Next step after a diagnosis of Colon Cancer is made via biopsy?
Back: CT scan of Chest/Abdomen/Pelvis for staging.

Front: Next step for a cirrhotic patient with a new solid liver mass <1cm on US?
Back: Repeat Ultrasound in 3-4 months.

Front: Next step for a cirrhotic patient with a new solid liver mass >1cm on US?
Back: Triple-phase CT or MRI with contrast (looking for arterial enhancement/venous washout).

Section: Internal Medicine
Topic: Nephrology — AKI, CKD, electrolyte & acid-base disorders

Front: Definition of AKI by KDIGO criteria?

Back: Increase in SCr $\ge 0.3$ mg/dL within 48h or $\ge 1.5\times$ baseline within 7 days.

Front: Most common cause of intrinsic AKI in hospitalized patients?

Back: Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN).

Front: Calculation for Fractional Excretion of Sodium (FeNa)?

Back: $FeNa = \frac{U_{Na} \times P_{Cr}}{P_{Na} \times U_{Cr}} \times 100$.

Front: FeNa value suggestive of Pre-renal azotemia?

Back: Less than 1%.

Front: FeNa value suggestive of Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN)?

Back: Greater than 2%.

Front: Urine sediment finding in ATN?

Back: Muddy brown (granular) casts.

Front: Urine sediment finding in Prerenal azotemia?

Back: Hyaline casts.

Front: BUN:Creatinine ratio in Prerenal AKI?

Back: Greater than 20:1.

Front: Two major phases of ATN?

Back: Maintenance phase (oliguric) and Recovery phase (polyuric).

Front: Classic triad of Acute Interstitial Nephritis (AIN)?

Back: Fever, rash, and peripheral eosinophilia.

Front: Common drug triggers for AIN?

Back: NSAIDs, Penicillins, Sulfonamides, and Proton Pump Inhibitors.

Front: Urinary finding diagnostic of AIN?

Back: White blood cell casts and eosinophiluria.

Front: Pathogenesis of Contrast-Induced Nephropathy?

Back: Direct tubular toxicity and renal vasoconstriction.

Front: Preferred prevention for Contrast-Induced Nephropathy?

Back: Isotonic saline hydration before and after the procedure.

Front: Cause of AKI after starting an ACE inhibitor in a patient with hypertension?

Back: Bilateral Renal Artery Stenosis.

Front: Pathognomonic biopsy finding in Ethylene Glycol poisoning?

Back: Envelope-shaped calcium oxalate crystals.

Front: Treatment for Rhabdomyolysis-induced AKI?

Back: Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation and correction of electrolytes.

Front: Formula for Urine Anion Gap (UAG)?

Back: $UAG = Na + K - Cl$.

Front: Absolute indications for urgent dialysis (mnemonic AEIOU)?

Back: Acidosis, Electrolytes (K+), Ingestion (toxins), Overload (volume), Uremia.

Part 2: Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD)

Front: Definition of CKD?

Back: GFR $< 60$ mL/min/1.73m² or markers of kidney damage for $\ge 3$ months.

Front: Most common cause of CKD in the US?

Back: Diabetes Mellitus.

Front: Target Blood Pressure for CKD patients (KDIGO)?

Back: $< 120$ mmHg systolic.

Front: Preferred antihypertensive for CKD with albuminuria?

Back: ACE inhibitors or ARBs.

Front: GFR range for Stage 3 CKD?

Back: $30$ to $59$ mL/min/1.73m².

Front: Definition of Nephrotic-range proteinuria?

Back: $> 3.5$ g/24 hours.

Front: First-line treatment for CKD-associated anemia?

Back: Iron supplementation, then Erythropoiesis-Stimulating Agents (ESAs).

Front: Target Hemoglobin in CKD patients on ESAs?

Back: $10$ to $11.5$ g/dL (avoiding $> 13$ due to stroke risk).

Front: Mechanism of Secondary Hyperparathyroidism in CKD?

Back: Phosphate retention and decreased $1,25(OH)_2D$ (Calcitriol) leading to hypocalcemia.

Front: Classic X-ray finding in Renal Osteodystrophy?

Back: "Rugger-jersey" spine (subperiosteal bone resorption).

Front: First-line management for Hyperphosphatemia in CKD?

Back: Dietary phosphate restriction and phosphate binders (e.g., Sevelamer).

Front: Why is Metformin contraindicated in advanced CKD?

Back: Increased risk of Lactic Acidosis.

Front: Screening test for Diabetic Nephropathy?

Back: Annual urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR).

Part 3: Electrolyte Disorders - Sodium & Water

Front: Primary determinant of Plasma Osmolality?

Back: Sodium concentration.

Front: Formula for Serum Osmolality?

Back: $2[Na] + \frac{Glucose}{18} + \frac{BUN}{2.8}$.

Front: Definition of Hyponatremia?

Back: Serum Sodium $< 135$ mEq/L.

Front: Cause of Hypertonic Hyponatremia?

Back: Hyperglycemia (glucose pulls water into the extracellular space).

Front: Correction factor for Sodium in Hyperglycemia?

Back: Add $1.6$ mEq/L to Na for every $100$ mg/dL glucose above normal.

Front: Lab findings in SIADH?

Back: Hyponatremia, low serum osm, high urine osm ($> 100$), and high urine sodium ($> 40$).

Front: Treatment for severe symptomatic hyponatremia?

Back: Hypertonic ($3\%$) saline.

Front: Maximum rate of Sodium correction to avoid Central Pontine Myelinolysis?

Back: $8$ mEq/L per 24 hours.

Front: Treatment for Hypovolemic Hyponatremia?

Back: Isotonic ($0.9\%$) saline.

Front: Primary symptom of Central Diabetes Insipidus?

Back: Polyuria and polydipsia with low urine osmolality.

Front: Diagnostic test to differentiate Central vs. Nephrogenic DI?

Back: Desmopressin (DDAVP) challenge.

Front: Response to DDAVP in Central Diabetes Insipidus?

Back: Significant increase in urine osmolality ($> 50\%$).

Front: Common medication causing Nephrogenic DI?

Back: Lithium.

Part 4: Electrolyte Disorders - Potassium

Front: Most common ECG change in Hyperkalemia?

Back: Peaked T-waves.

Front: Order of ECG changes in worsening Hyperkalemia?

Back: Peaked T $\rightarrow$ PR prolongation $\rightarrow$ QRS widening $\rightarrow$ Sine wave.

Front: First step in treating Hyperkalemia with ECG changes?

Back: Calcium Gluconate (membrane stabilization).

Front: Medications that shift Potassium intracellularly?

Back: Insulin (+ glucose), $\beta_2$-agonists, and Sodium Bicarbonate.
