Front: Definitive methods for Potassium removal from the body?

Back: Loop diuretics, Cation exchange resins (Patiromer), or Hemodialysis.

Front: ECG findings in Hypokalemia?

Back: U-waves, T-wave flattening, and ST depression.

Front: Electrolyte deficiency that makes Hypokalemia refractory to treatment?

Back: Hypomagnesemia.

Front: Common cause of Hypokalemia with Hypertension and Metabolic Alkalosis?

Back: Primary Hyperaldosteronism (Conn Syndrome).

Part 5: Acid-Base Disorders

Front: Formula for Anion Gap (AG)?

Back: $Na - (Cl + HCO_3)$.

Front: Normal range for Serum Anion Gap?

Back: $8$ to $12$ mEq/L.

Front: Mnemonic for High Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis (HAGMA)?

Back: MUDPILES (Methanol, Uremia, DKA, Propylene glycol, Iron/INH, Lactic acid, Ethylene glycol, Salicylates).

Front: Winter’s Formula for expected $PCO_2$ in Metabolic Acidosis?

Back: $PCO_2 = (1.5 \times [HCO_3]) + 8 \pm 2$.

Front: Significance of a measured $PCO_2$ higher than Winter’s Formula calculation?

Back: Concomitant Respiratory Acidosis.

Front: Two major causes of Normal Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis (NAGMA)?

Back: Diarrhea and Renal Tubular Acidosis (RTA).

Front: Defect in Type 1 (Distal) RTA?

Back: Inability to secrete $H^+$ in the distal tubule.

Front: Urine pH in Type 1 (Distal) RTA?

Back: Abnormally high ($> 5.5$).

Front: Defect in Type 2 (Proximal) RTA?

Back: Inability to reabsorb $HCO_3$ in the proximal tubule.

Front: Association of Type 2 RTA with other proximal transport defects?

Back: Fanconi Syndrome.

Front: Defect in Type 4 RTA?

Back: Hypoaldosteronism or aldosterone resistance.

Front: Distinguishing lab feature of Type 4 RTA?

Back: Hyperkalemia.

Front: Acid-base disturbance in early Salicylate toxicity?

Back: Respiratory Alkalosis (direct stimulation of respiratory center).

Front: Acid-base disturbance in late Salicylate toxicity?

Back: Mixed Respiratory Alkalosis and High Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis.

Front: Next step if Metabolic Alkalosis is "Chloride Responsive"?

Back: Give Isotonic Saline (usually due to vomiting or diuretics).

Front: Examples of "Chloride Resistant" Metabolic Alkalosis?

Back: Hyperaldosteronism and Cushing Syndrome.

Part 6: Glomerular Diseases

Front: Characteristics of Nephrotic Syndrome?

Back: Proteinuria $> 3.5$ g/day, hypoalbuminemia, edema, and hyperlipidemia.

Front: Most common Nephrotic Syndrome in children?

Back: Minimal Change Disease.

Front: Treatment of choice for Minimal Change Disease?

Back: Corticosteroids.

Front: Most common Nephrotic Syndrome in African Americans and HIV patients?

Back: Focal Segmental Glomerulosclerosis (FSGS).

Front: Nephrotic Syndrome associated with Hepatitis B, NSAIDs, and solid tumors?

Back: Membranous Nephropathy.

Front: Appearance of Membranous Nephropathy on Electron Microscopy?

Back: "Spike and dome" subepithelial deposits.

Front: Definition of Nephritic Syndrome?

Back: Hypertension, hematuria (RBC casts), and mild-to-moderate proteinuria.

Front: Most common cause of Glomerulonephritis worldwide?

Back: IgA Nephropathy (Berger Disease).

Front: Timing of IgA Nephropathy hematuria vs. Post-streptococcal GN?

Back: IgA is "synpharyngitic" (days); PSGN is 1-3 weeks post-infection.

Front: Lab finding specific to Post-streptococcal Glomerulonephritis?

Back: Low C3 complement and high Antistreptolysin O (ASO) titers.

Front: Light microscopy finding in Post-streptococcal GN?

Back: "Starry sky" granular appearance (lumpy-bumpy).

Front: Hallmark of Rapidly Progressive Glomerulonephritis (RPGN)?

Back: Crescents in Bowman’s space on light microscopy.

Front: Mechanism of Goodpasture Syndrome?

Back: Anti-GBM antibodies against Type IV collagen.

Front: Organ systems involved in Goodpasture Syndrome?

Back: Lungs (hemoptysis) and Kidneys (hematuria).

Front: Immunofluorescence pattern in Goodpasture Syndrome?

Back: Linear IgG deposits along the GBM.

Front: Diagnosis for Nephritic syndrome + Hemoptysis + c-ANCA positive?

Back: Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (Wegener’s).

Front: Diagnosis for Nephritic syndrome + Asthma + p-ANCA positive?

Back: Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (Churg-Strauss).

Front: Alport Syndrome inheritance and classic triad?

Back: X-linked dominant; Sensorineural deafness, eye abnormalities, and glomerulonephritis.

Part 7: Cystic & Vascular Diseases

Front: Genetics of Adult Polycystic Kidney Disease (ADPKD)?

Back: Autosomal Dominant (PKD1 or PKD2 mutations).

Front: Extra-renal manifestations of ADPKD?

Back: Berry aneurysms (Circle of Willis), hepatic cysts, and Mitral Valve Prolapse.

Front: Common cause of death in ADPKD patients?

Back: Subarachnoid hemorrhage (ruptured Berry aneurysm) or Cardiovascular disease.

Front: Gold standard for diagnosing Renal Artery Stenosis?

Back: Renal Arteriography.

Front: Typical patient demographic for Fibromuscular Dysplasia?

Back: Young females with new-onset hypertension.

Front: Radiologic sign of Fibromuscular Dysplasia?

Back: "String of pearls" appearance of the renal artery.

Front: Cause of AKI after a vascular procedure (e.g., cardiac cath)?

Back: Cholesterol Atheroembolism.

Front: Physical exam finding in Cholesterol Embolization?

Back: Livedo reticularis or "blue toe" syndrome.

Part 8: Calcium, Magnesium, & Phosphate

Front: Most common cause of Hypercalcemia in outpatients?

Back: Primary Hyperparathyroidism.

Front: Most common cause of Hypercalcemia in hospitalized patients?

Back: Malignancy.

Front: ECG change in Hypercalcemia?

Back: Shortened QT interval.

Front: Initial treatment for severe Hypercalcemia?

Back: Aggressive Isotonic Saline hydration.

Front: Signs of Hypocalcemia (Neuromuscular irritability)?

Back: Chvostek sign (facial twitch) and Trousseau sign (carpal spasm).

Front: ECG change in Hypocalcemia?

Back: Prolonged QT interval.

Front: Common cause of Hypomagnesemia?

Back: Chronic alcoholism, diarrhea, and diuretics.

Front: Life-threatening arrhythmia associated with Hypomagnesemia?

Back: Torsades de Pointes.

Part 9: Diuretics

Front: Site of action for Acetazolamide?

Back: Proximal Convoluted Tubule (Carbonic Anhydrase inhibitor).

Front: Mechanism of Loop Diuretics (e.g., Furosemide)?

Back: Inhibition of Na-K-2Cl cotransporter in the thick ascending limb.

Front: Major side effects of Loop Diuretics?

Back: Ototoxicity, Hypokalemia, Hypocalcemia, and Hyperuricemia.

Front: Mechanism of Thiazide Diuretics?

Back: Inhibition of Na-Cl cotransporter in the distal convoluted tubule.

Front: Distinctive electrolyte effect of Thiazides compared to Loops?

Back: Thiazides cause Hypercalcemia (Loops cause Hypocalcemia).

Front: Site of action for Spironolactone?

Back: Cortical collecting duct (Aldosterone receptor antagonist).

Front: Major side effect of Spironolactone in males?

Back: Gynecomastia.

Part 10: Miscellaneous High-Yield

Front: Most common type of kidney stone?

Back: Calcium Oxalate.

Front: Radiologic appearance of Magnesium Ammonium Phosphate (Struvite) stones?

Back: "Staghorn" calculus.

Front: Organism associated with Struvite stones?

Back: Urease-positive bacteria (e.g., Proteus, Klebsiella).

Front: Stones that are radiolucent on X-ray?

Back: Uric Acid stones.

Front: Treatment for Uric Acid stones?

Back: Alkalinization of urine (Potassium Citrate).

Front: Pathogenesis of Nephrogenic Systemic Fibrosis?

Back: Exposure to Gadolinium-based contrast in patients with low GFR.

Front: Definition of the "Delta-Delta" ($\Delta \Delta$)?

Back: The ratio of the change in Anion Gap to the change in Bicarbonate ($\Delta AG / \Delta HCO_3$).

Front: Interpretation of a $\Delta \Delta$ ratio $> 2$?

Back: Concomitant Metabolic Alkalosis underlying the High Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis.

Front: Interpretation of a $\Delta \Delta$ ratio $< 0.4$?

Back: Concomitant Normal Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis (NAGMA).

Front: Formula for the Osmolar Gap?

Back: $Measured\ Osmolality - Calculated\ Osmolality$.

Front: Significance of an Osmolar Gap $> 10$ mOsm/kg?

Back: Presence of unmeasured osmotically active substances (e.g., Ethanol, Methanol, Ethylene Glycol).

Front: Gold standard for diagnosing the specific cause of NAGMA?

Back: Urine Anion Gap (UAG).

Front: Meaning of a Positive Urine Anion Gap in the setting of Metabolic Acidosis?

Back: Low urinary $NH_4^+$ excretion, suggesting a Renal Tubular Acidosis (RTA).

Front: Meaning of a Negative Urine Anion Gap in the setting of Metabolic Acidosis?

Back: High urinary $NH_4^+$ excretion, suggesting gastrointestinal bicarbonate loss (Diarrhea).

Part 12: Nephrotic & Nephritic Deep Dive

Front: Pathogenesis of Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis (MPGN) Type II?

Back: C3 Nephritic Factor (antibody that stabilizes C3 convertase).

Front: Classic EM finding in MPGN Type II (Dense Deposit Disease)?

Back: Ribbon-like deposits within the Glomerular Basement Membrane.

Front: Most common cause of secondary FSGS?

Back: Obesity, Heroin use, or HIV infection.

Front: Primary pathology in Diabetic Nephropathy?

Back: Non-enzymatic glycosylation of the GBM and efferent arteriole hyalinization.

Front: Pathognomonic light microscopy finding in Diabetic Nephropathy?

Back: Kimmelstiel-Wilson nodules (nodular glomerulosclerosis).

Front: Effect of ACE inhibitors on glomerular hemodynamics?

Back: Vasodilation of the efferent arteriole, decreasing intraglomerular pressure.

Front: Renal pathology associated with Hepatitis C virus?

Back: Cryoglobulinemia leading to MPGN.

Front: Characteristic renal biopsy finding in Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (Class IV)?

Back: "Wire-loop" lesions (subendothelial immune complex deposits).

Front: Most common cause of Nephrotic Syndrome in adults in the US?

Back: Focal Segmental Glomerulosclerosis (FSGS).

Front: Only Nephrotic Syndrome that may present with low complement levels?

Back: Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis (MPGN).

Part 13: Complex Electrolyte Management

Front: Definition of "Pseudohyponatremia"?

Back: Factitiously low sodium due to extreme Hyperlipidemia or Hyperproteinemia.

Front: Treatment of choice for Euvolemic Hyponatremia (SIADH) resistant to fluid restriction?

Back: Salt tablets, Urea, or Vaptans (Vasopressin antagonists).

Front: Electrolyte abnormality that increases the risk of Digoxin toxicity?

Back: Hypokalemia.

Front: Common cause of Hypermagnesemia?

Back: Excessive intake of Magnesium-containing antacids/laxatives in CKD patients.

Front: Management of severe Hypermagnesemia with respiratory depression?

Back: IV Calcium Gluconate (antagonizes Magnesium effects).

Front: Definition of "Hungry Bone Syndrome"?

Back: Severe hypocalcemia and hypophosphatemia following parathyroidectomy.

Front: Characteristic lab profile of Vitamin D deficiency?

Back: Low Calcium, Low Phosphate, High PTH.

Front: Characteristic lab profile of Hypoparathyroidism?

Back: Low Calcium, High Phosphate, Low PTH.

Part 14: Genetic & Rare Tubular Disorders

Front: Inheritance and defect in Bartter Syndrome?

Back: Autosomal recessive; defect in the Na-K-2Cl cotransporter (Loop of Henle).

Front: Clinical presentation of Bartter Syndrome?

Back: Mimics chronic Loop Diuretic use (Hypokalemia, Metabolic Alkalosis, Hypercalciuria).

Front: Inheritance and defect in Gitelman Syndrome?

Back: Autosomal recessive; defect in the Na-Cl cotransporter (Distal Tubule).

Front: Clinical presentation of Gitelman Syndrome?

Back: Mimics Thiazide Diuretic use (Hypokalemia, Metabolic Alkalosis, Hypocalciuria, Hypomagnesemia).

Front: Difference in Urinary Calcium between Bartter and Gitelman?

Back: Bartter has High Urine Calcium; Gitelman has Low Urine Calcium.

Front: Inheritance and defect in Liddle Syndrome?

Back: Autosomal dominant; gain-of-function mutation in ENaC (Collecting Duct).

Front: Clinical presentation of Liddle Syndrome?

Back: Hypertension, Hypokalemia, and Metabolic Alkalosis with low Aldosterone/Renin levels.

Front: Treatment for Liddle Syndrome?

Back: Amiloride or Triamterene (blocks ENaC); Spironolactone is ineffective.

Part 15: Acute Kidney Injury Nuances

Front: Indication for starting a "Renal Dose" of Dopamine in AKI?

Back: None; "Renal Dose" Dopamine is no longer recommended and provides no benefit.

Front: Most sensitive indicator of early recovery from ATN?

Back: Increase in urine output (Polyuric phase).

Front: Common cause of AKI in a patient with a "fountain-like" rash and eosinophilia?

Back: Acute Interstitial Nephritis (AIN).

Front: Likely diagnosis for AKI + palpable purpura + abdominal pain?

Back: IgA Vasculitis (Henoch-Schönlein Purpura).

Front: Diagnosis for AKI + Schistocytes on peripheral smear + Thrombocytopenia?

Back: Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS) or Thrombotic Thrombocytopenic Purpura (TTP).

Front: Pathogenesis of HUS in children?

Back: Shiga toxin (E. coli O157:H7) causing endothelial damage.

Front: Why are NSAIDs contraindicated in AKI?

Back: They inhibit prostaglandins, leading to afferent arteriole vasoconstriction and reduced GFR.

Front: Preferred fluid for resuscitation in a patient with severe Metabolic Acidosis and AKI?

Back: Isotonic Sodium Bicarbonate or Lactated Ringer’s (avoiding normal saline to prevent hyperchloremic acidosis).

Part 16: Stones and Obstruction

Front: Management of a kidney stone $< 5$ mm?

Back: Spontaneous passage with hydration and Alpha-blockers (Tamsulosin).

Front: Absolute indication for surgical intervention in Urolithiasis?

Back: Urosepsis, intractable pain, or stone in a solitary kidney.

Front: Diagnostic test of choice for suspected Nephrolithiasis?

Back: Non-contrast CT Abdomen and Pelvis.

Front: Dietary recommendation to prevent Calcium Oxalate stones?

Back: Low sodium, normal calcium intake, and increased fluid intake.

Front: Why is low-calcium diet NOT recommended for Calcium Oxalate stones?

Back: Low calcium increases intestinal oxalate absorption, leading to hyperoxaluria.

Front: Diagnosis for sudden onset flank pain + hematuria + enlarged kidney in a Nephrotic patient?

Back: Renal Vein Thrombosis.

Front: Nephrotic Syndrome most commonly associated with Renal Vein Thrombosis?

Back: Membranous Nephropathy.

Part 17: Dialysis and Transplant

Front: Most common cause of death in Dialysis patients?

Back: Cardiovascular disease (Arrhythmias and Heart Failure).

Front: Most common complication of Peritoneal Dialysis?

Back: Peritonitis (usually Staphylococcus species).

Front: First-line treatment for Hyperacute Transplant Rejection (within minutes)?

Back: Immediate surgical removal of the graft.

Front: Mechanism of Hyperacute Rejection?

Back: Pre-formed recipient antibodies against donor HLA (Type II Hypersensitivity).

Front: Mechanism of Acute Transplant Rejection (weeks to months)?

Back: T-cell mediated response against donor HLA (Type IV Hypersensitivity).

Front: Biopsy finding in Acute Cellular Rejection?

Back: Lymphocytic infiltration of the renal tubules (Tubulitis).

Front: Major side effect of Cyclosporine?

Back: Nephrotoxicity and gingival hyperplasia.

Front: Characteristic toxicity of Tacrolimus?

Back: Nephrotoxicity and new-onset Diabetes Mellitus.

Front: Drug of choice for CMV prophylaxis in renal transplant?

Back: Valganciclovir.

Part 18: Pregnancy and the Kidney

Front: Normal change in GFR during pregnancy?

Back: Increases by approximately $50\%$.

Front: Normal Serum Creatinine range in a healthy pregnant woman?

Back: $0.4$ to $0.8$ mg/dL (values $> 0.8$ are abnormal).

Front: Definition of Preeclampsia?

Back: New-onset Hypertension ($> 140/90$) and Proteinuria ($> 300$ mg/24h) after 20 weeks gestation.

Front: Preferred treatment for Preeclamptic Hypertensive crisis?

Back: IV Hydralazine, Labetalol, or Nifedipine.

Front: Goal of Magnesium Sulfate in Preeclampsia/Eclampsia?

Back: Seizure prophylaxis.

Front: Treatment for Magnesium toxicity in a pregnant patient?

Back: IV Calcium  Gluconate.

Front: Explanation for "Normal Anion Gap" in some cases of Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Cationic IgG paraproteins decrease the measured anion gap toward zero or negative values.

Front: Effect of severe Hypoalbuminemia on the Anion Gap?

Back: Every 1 g/dL drop in Albumin decreases the "normal" AG by 2.5 mEq/L.

Front: Acid-base status in a patient with both persistent vomiting and DKA?

Back: Mixed High Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis and Metabolic Alkalosis (pH may appear normal).

Front: Typical electrolyte profile in Adrenal Insufficiency (Addison's)?

Back: Hyponatremia, Hyperkalemia, and Normal Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis (Type 4 RTA).

Front: Mechanism of "Milk-Alkali Syndrome"?

Back: Excessive intake of Calcium and absorbable alkali leading to Hypercalcemia, Metabolic Alkalosis, and AKI.

Front: Why does Hypokalemia worsen Hepatic Encephalopathy?

Back: It stimulates renal $NH_3$ production (ammoniagenesis), which crosses the blood-brain barrier.

Part 20: Advanced Tubular & Glomerular Physiology

Front: Primary function of the "Principal Cell" in the Collecting Duct?

Back: Sodium reabsorption (via ENaC) and Potassium secretion.

Front: Primary function of the "Alpha-Intercalated Cell"?

Back: Hydrogen ion ($H^+$) secretion and Bicarbonate ($HCO_3^-$) regeneration.

Front: Definition of the "Myogenic Response" in renal autoregulation?

Back: Afferent arterioles constrict in response to high stretch (pressure) to protect the glomerulus.

Front: Mechanism of "Tubuloglomerular Feedback"?

Back: Macula densa senses high $NaCl$ delivery and triggers afferent arteriole constriction via Adenosine.

Front: Impact of Prostaglandins on the Afferent Arteriole?

Back: Vasodilation (keeps the "pipe" open to maintain GFR).

Front: Definition of "Nephron Loss Compensation"?

Back: Remaining nephrons undergo hypertrophy and hyperfiltration to maintain total GFR.

Part 21: Clinical Nephrology & Systemic Disease

Front: Pathogenesis of Hepatorenal Syndrome (HRS)?

Back: Splanchnic vasodilation leading to severe renal vasoconstriction and "functional" AKI.

Front: First-line medical therapy for Hepatorenal Syndrome?

Back: Vasoconstrictors (Terlipressin or Midodrine/Octreotide) plus Albumin.

Front: Classic renal finding in Scleroderma Renal Crisis?

Back: "Onion-skin" thickening of the arterial walls and malignant hypertension.

Front: Treatment of choice for Scleroderma Renal Crisis?

Back: ACE Inhibitors (even if Serum Creatinine is rising).

Front: Significance of "Dismorphic RBCs" in urine microscopy?

Back: Suggests the source of bleeding is glomerular (passed through the basement membrane).

Front: Definition of "Orthostatic Albuminuria"?

Back: Increased protein excretion while upright, but normal while supine (common in adolescents).

Part 22: Toxicology & The Kidney

Front: Treatment for Ethylene Glycol or Methanol poisoning?

Back: Fomepizole (inhibits Alcohol Dehydrogenase) and Hemodialysis.

Front: Major renal side effect of Amphotericin B?

Back: Distal (Type 1) RTA and significant Magnesium/Potassium wasting.

Front: Renal toxicity associated with Acyclovir?

Back: Crystalline nephropathy (obstructive AKI); prevented by aggressive hydration.

Front: Likely toxin in a patient with AKI and "Fanconi-like" symptoms (glycosuria, phosphaturia)?

Back: If old: Expired Tetracyclines; If modern: Ifosfamide or Tenofovir.

Front: Mechanism of Aristolochic Acid nephropathy (Chinese Herb Nephropathy)?

Back: Severe tubulointerstitial fibrosis and high risk of urothelial malignancy.

Part 23: CKD-MBD (Mineral and Bone Disorder)

Front: Why is Calcitriol ($1,25(OH)_2D$) low in CKD?

Back: Loss of 1-alpha-hydroxylase enzyme in the proximal tubule and high FGF-23 levels.

Front: Role of FGF-23 in early CKD?

Back: Increases urinary phosphate excretion to prevent hyperphosphatemia (at the cost of lowering Vitamin D).

Front: Definition of "Calciphylaxis" (Calcific Uremic Arteriolopathy)?

Back: Systemic calcification of arterioles leading to skin ischemia and necrotic ulcers.

Front: Primary risk factor for Calciphylaxis?

Back: High Calcium-Phosphate product and Warfarin use in ESRD patients.

Front: Distinction between "Osteitis Fibrosa Cystica" and "Adynamic Bone Disease"?

Back: Osteitis is high bone turnover (high PTH); Adynamic is low bone turnover (over-suppressed PTH).

Part 24: Practical Management Nuances

Front: Definition of "Resistance" to Loop Diuretics?

Back: Hypertrophy of the distal tubule allows for compensatory $NaCl$ reabsorption.

Front: Strategy to overcome Loop Diuretic resistance?

Back: "Sequential Nephron Blockade" by adding a Thiazide (e.g., Metolazone).

Front: Indication for Sodium Bicarbonate in CKD patients?

Back: Serum $HCO_3 < 22$ mEq/L (to slow progression of CKD and bone loss).

Front: When to initiate Dialysis in CKD (non-urgent)?

Back: Usually when GFR is $< 10$ mL/min or when uremic symptoms (nausea, pericarditis, encephalopathy) appear.

Front: Preferred vascular access for long-term Hemodialysis?

Back: Arteriovenous (AV) Fistula (lowest infection and thrombosis rates).

Front: How long does an AV Fistula typically need to "mature" before use?

Back: 6 to 8 weeks.

Front: Most common cause of EPO-resistant anemia in CKD?

Back: Iron deficiency.

Part 25: Rapid-Fire Differentials

Front: Patient with Nephrotic syndrome + Macroglossia + Heart Failure?

Back: Renal Amyloidosis.

Front: Apple-green birefringence under polarized light (Congo Red stain)?

Back: Amyloidosis.

Front: Hematuria + Flank Pain + Palpable Mass?

Back: Renal Cell Carcinoma triad (though rarely all three appear together).

Front: Most common histologic type of Renal Cell Carcinoma?

Back: Clear Cell Carcinoma.

Front: Paraneoplastic syndrome associated with Renal Cell Carcinoma?

Back: Erythrocytosis (due to EPO production).

Front: Patient with "Muddy Brown Casts" + high FeNa after a long surgery?

Back: Ischemic Acute Tubular Necrosis (ATN).

Front: Sudden AKI + "Humps" on EM + Low C3?

Back: Post-streptococcal Glomerulonephritis.

Front: Definition of Laboratory Tumor Lysis Syndrome (TLS)?

Back: Hyperuricemia, hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, and hypocalcemia.

Front: Mechanism of AKI in Tumor Lysis Syndrome?

Back: Intratubular precipitation of uric acid and calcium phosphate crystals.

Front: Preferred agent to prevent TLS in high-risk patients?

Back: Allopurinol (blocks xanthine oxidase).

Front: Agent used to treat acute, existing hyperuricemia in TLS?

Back: Rasburicase (recombinant urate oxidase that converts uric acid to allantoin).

Front: Common cause of "Bland" urine sediment but rising SCr in a Multiple Myeloma patient?

Back: Myeloma Kidney (Light Chain Cast Nephropathy).

Front: Nephrotic syndrome classic to Hodgkin Lymphoma?

Back: Minimal Change Disease.

Part 27: Diuretic Resistance & Advanced Pharma

Front: The "Braking Phenomenon" in diuretic therapy?

Back: The kidney's compensatory increase in sodium reabsorption in response to chronic loop diuretic use.

Front: Mechanism of Furosemide in treating pulmonary edema (besides diuresis)?

Back: Rapid venous dilation (increasing venous capacitance) before the onset of diuresis.

Front: Electrolyte abnormality caused by Loop Diuretics that can lead to metabolic alkalosis?

Back: Contraction Alkalosis (loss of chloride-rich, bicarbonate-poor fluid).

Front: Why are Thiazides ineffective when GFR is $< 30$ mL/min?

Back: They cannot reach their site of action in the distal tubule (Metolazone is a notable exception).

Front: Mechanism of Acetazolamide-induced Metabolic Acidosis?

Back: Inhibition of proximal tubule $HCO_3^-$ reabsorption leads to increased bicarbonate excretion.

Part 28: Geriatric & Critical Care Nephrology

Front: Why is Serum Creatinine an unreliable marker of GFR in the elderly?

Back: Decreased muscle mass leads to lower creatinine production, masking significant renal impairment.

Front: Most common cause of Hyponatremia in the elderly on new medication?

Back: Thiazide-induced hyponatremia.

Front: Distinguishing feature between Hyponatremia caused by SIADH vs. Salt-Wasting?

Back: Salt-Wasting involves volume depletion (hypovolemia); SIADH is euvolemic.

Front: First-line treatment for "Hepatorenal Syndrome Type 1"?

Back: Albumin and Vasoconstrictors (e.g., Terlipressin or Norepinephrine).

Front: Classic renal biopsy finding in "Myoglobinuric AKI"?

Back: Pigmented granular casts and tubular necrosis.

Part 29: Glomerular Nuances & Rare GN

Front: Serum Complement level (C3/C4) in IgA Nephropathy?

Back: Characteristically Normal.

Front: Most common extra-renal finding in IgA Vasculitis (Henoch-Schönlein)?

Back: Palpable purpura on the lower extremities and buttocks.

Front: Renal syndrome associated with "Anti-Phospholipase A2 Receptor" (PLA2R) antibodies?

Back: Primary Membranous Nephropathy.

Front: Pathogenesis of Alport Syndrome?

Back: Mutation in the alpha-3, 4, or 5 chains of Type IV Collagen.

Front: EM finding in Alport Syndrome?

Back: Irregular thinning and thickening of the GBM ("Basket-weave" appearance).

Front: Classic feature of Thin Basement Membrane Disease?

Back: Persistent, benign microscopic hematuria with a normal family history of renal function.

Part 30: Electrolyte "Double-Checks"

Front: Why does Hypermagnesemia cause Hypocalcemia?

Back: It suppresses Parathyroid Hormone (PTH) secretion.

Front: Most common cause of Hypoxemia during a Hemodialysis session?

Back: Acetate in the dialysate or complement activation on the dialysis membrane leading to pulmonary sequestration of WBCs.

Front: Preferred treatment for Hyperkalemia in a patient with Metabolic Acidosis?

Back: Sodium Bicarbonate (shifts $K^+$ into cells while treating the underlying pH).

Front: ECG finding classic for Hypocalcemia?

Back: Prolonged QT interval (predisposes to arrhythmias).

Front: Corrected Sodium formula for Hyperglycemia (the "1.6" rule)?

Back: $Corrected\ Na = Measured\ Na + 0.016 \times (Glucose - 100)$.

Part 31: Transplant Immunology & Rejection

Front: Timeframe for "Chronic Rejection" of a renal transplant?

Back: Months to years after transplant.

Front: Primary pathology in Chronic Transplant Rejection?

Back: Vascular fibrosis and "Graft Arteriopathy" (intimal thickening).

Front: Role of BK Virus in transplant nephropathy?

Back: Reactivation of the polyomavirus leads to tubulointerstitial inflammation and graft loss.

Front: Most common cause of Post-transplant Erythrocytosis?

Back: Overproduction of EPO by the native kidneys or the graft (treated with ACE inhibitors).

Part 32: Rapid-Fire Differentials (Summary Cards)

Front: Hematuria + Hemoptysis + Anti-GBM (+)?

Back: Goodpasture Syndrome.

Front: Hematuria + Hemoptysis + c-ANCA (+)?

Back: Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (Wegener’s).

Front: Hematuria + Asthma + p-ANCA (+)?

Back: Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (Churg-Strauss).

Front: Hematuria + Deafness + Family History (+)?

Back: Alport Syndrome.

Front: Hematuria + Recent Pharyngitis (1-3 days prior)?

Back: IgA Nephropathy.

Front: Hematuria + Recent Pharyngitis (2 weeks prior) + Low C3?

Back: Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (PSGN).

Front: Hematuria + HIV + Heroin Use + Nephrotic range proteinuria?

Back: Focal Segmental Glomerulosclerosis (FSGS).


Front: Definition of "Renal Reserve"?

Back: The ability of the kidney to increase GFR in response to a protein load or amino acid infusion.

Front: Why does Urea reabsorption increase in states of dehydration?

Back: ADH (Vasopressin) increases the permeability of the medullary collecting duct to urea to maintain the osmotic gradient.

Front: Formula for "Free Water Clearance" ($C_{H2O}$)?

Back: $C_{H2O} = V - \frac{U_{osm} \times V}{P_{osm}}$.

Front: Significance of a negative Free Water Clearance?

Back: The kidney is conserving water (producing concentrated urine), typical in states of ADH excess.

Front: What does "Urine Osmolar Gap" measure?

Back: It estimates ammonium ($NH_4^+$) excretion in Normal Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis.

Front: Calculation for Urine Osmolar Gap?

Back: $U_{osm} (measured) - [2(Na + K) + \frac{Urea}{2.8} + \frac{Glucose}{18}]$.

Part 34: Nephrolithiasis & Crystal Arthropathy

Front: Primary inhibitor of calcium stone formation found in normal urine?

Back: Citrate (it chelates calcium in the urine).

Front: Most common cause of Hypercalciuria with normal serum calcium?

Back: Idiopathic Hypercalciuria.

Front: Why does Crohn’s Disease increase the risk of Calcium Oxalate stones?

Back: Fat malabsorption binds calcium in the gut, leaving oxalate free to be absorbed (Enteric Hyperoxaluria).

Front: Classic urine crystal finding in Ethylene Glycol ingestion?

Back: Monohydrate calcium oxalate (needle/dumbbell shaped) or dihydrate (envelope shaped).

Front: Mechanism of "Indinavir Stones"?

Back: Precipitation of the protease inhibitor medication itself; these stones are radiolucent on CT.

Part 35: Vascular & Ischemic Renal Disease

Front: Definition of "Flash Pulmonary Edema" in Nephrology?

Back: Sudden, severe pulmonary edema often associated with bilateral renal artery stenosis.

Front: Preferred screening test for Renal Artery Stenosis in patients with CKD?

Back: Doppler Ultrasound or MR Angiography (avoiding CT with contrast).

Front: Most common cause of Renal Artery Stenosis in elderly males?

Back: Atherosclerosis.

Front: Renal biopsy finding in Malignant Hypertension?

Back: Hyperplastic arteriolitis ("onion-skinning") and fibrinoid necrosis.

Front: Typical presentation of Renal Artery Embolism?

Back: Acute flank pain, nausea, and high LDH with a normal urine sediment.

Part 36: Pediatric & Congenital Anomalies

Front: Most common cause of bladder outlet obstruction in newborn males?

Back: Posterior Urethral Valves.

Front: Diagnostic test of choice for Posterior Urethral Valves?

Back: Voiding Cystourethrogram (VCUG).

Front: Mnemonic for Potter Sequence (resulting from oligohydramnios)?

Back: Pulmonary hypoplasia, Oligohydramnios, Twisted face, Twisted skin, Extremity defects, Renal failure.

Front: Most common cause of a palpable abdominal mass in a newborn?

Back: Multicystic Dysplastic Kidney (MCDK).

Front: Genetic defect in Autosomal Recessive Polycystic Kidney Disease (ARPKD)?

Back: Mutation in the PKHD1 gene (fibrocystin).

Front: Systemic finding always present in ARPKD?

Back: Congenital Hepatic Fibrosis.

Part 37: Advanced Fluid Management

Front: Definition of "Isotonic saline" ($0.9\% NaCl$) osmolality?

Back: 308 mOsm/L.

Front: Why can $0.9\%$ Saline cause a Normal Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis?

Back: The high chloride load ($154$ mEq/L) drives bicarbonate out of the plasma (Hyperchloremic Acidosis).

Front: Preferred fluid for resuscitation in Hypernatremic Dehydration?

Back: Initial Isotonic Saline (to restore BP), followed by $5\%$ Dextrose ($D5W$) or $0.45\%$ Saline.

Front: Formula for "Free Water Deficit" in Hypernatremia?

Back: $0.6 \times Weight \times (\frac{Current\ Na}{140} - 1)$.

Part 38: Kidney in Systemic Disease (The "Connectors")

Front: Most common renal lesion in patients with Rheumatoid Arthritis?

Back: AA Amyloidosis (secondary to chronic inflammation).

Front: Effect of NSAIDs on Renal Blood Flow in a "low-effective-circulating-volume" state?

Back: They block vasodilatory prostaglandins, leading to acute prerenal failure.

Front: Distinguishing feature of Lithium-induced Nephrogenic DI vs. others?

Back: It can persist even after the drug is discontinued due to permanent collecting duct damage.

Front: Classic renal finding in Sarcoidosis?

Back: Hypercalciuria and Hypercalcemia (due to extra-renal 1-alpha-hydroxylase in granulomas).

Front: Renal biopsy finding in Sjogren’s Syndrome?

Back: Chronic Interstitial Nephritis, often presenting as Type 1 RTA.

Part 39: End-Stage Renal Disease (ESRD) Management

Front: Leading cause of death in the first year of starting Dialysis?

Back: Infection (specifically catheter-related sepsis).

Front: Target "Urea Reduction Ratio" (URR) for adequate dialysis?

Back: Greater than $65\%$.

Front: Definition of "Disequilibrium Syndrome" during dialysis?

Back: Cerebral edema caused by the rapid removal of urea from the blood while it remains high in the brain.

Front: Primary treatment for Uremic Pericarditis?

Back: Immediate intensification of Hemodialysis.

Front: Why is Heparin used during Hemodialysis?

Back: To prevent clotting of the blood within the extracorporeal circuit/dialyzer.

Part 40: Acid-Base "Must-Knows"

Front: Most common cause of Respiratory Alkalosis in a clinical setting?

Back: Hyperventilation (Anxiety, Pain, or early Sepsis).

Front: Expected compensation for Acute Respiratory Acidosis?

Back: Bicarbonate increases by $1$ mEq/L for every $10$ mmHg increase in $PCO_2$.

Front: Expected compensation for Chronic Respiratory Acidosis?

Back: Bicarbonate increases by $3.5$ to $4$ mEq/L for every $10$ mmHg increase in $PCO_2$.

Front: Definition of "Metabolic Alkalosis, Saline-Resistant"?

Back: Urinary Chloride $> 20$ mEq/L; usually due to mineralocorticoid excess.

Front: Definition of "Metabolic Alkalosis, Saline-Responsive"?

Back: Urinary Chloride $< 15$ mEq/L; usually due to vomiting or nasogastric suction.

=====================================================================
=====================================================================

Section: Internal Medicine

Topic: Endocrinology — Diabetes, thyroid, adrenal, pituitary


Front: Diagnostic criteria for DM based on Fasting Plasma Glucose (FPG)?

Back: $\ge 126$ mg/dL (7.0 mmol/L).

Front: Diagnostic criteria for DM based on HbA1c?

Back: $\ge 6.5\%$.

Front: Diagnostic criteria for DM based on 2-hour Oral Glucose Tolerance Test (OGTT)?

Back: $\ge 200$ mg/dL (11.1 mmol/L).

Front: Definition of "Prediabetes" by HbA1c range?

Back: $5.7\%$ to $6.4\%$.

Front: Primary pathology in Type 1 DM?

Back: Autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells leading to absolute insulin deficiency.

Front: Most common autoantibody checked in Type 1 DM?

Back: Glutamic Acid Decarboxylase (GAD-65) antibodies.

Front: Primary pathology in Type 2 DM?

Back: Insulin resistance combined with relative insulin deficiency.

Front: First-line pharmacological treatment for Type 2 DM?

Back: Metformin.

Front: Major contraindication for Metformin use?

Back: Estimated GFR < 30 mL/min (risk of lactic acidosis).

Front: Mechanism of action of Sulfonylureas (e.g., Glipizide)?

Back: Closes ATP-sensitive K+ channels in beta cells to stimulate insulin release.

Front: Primary side effect of Sulfonylureas?

Back: Hypoglycemia and weight gain.

Front: Mechanism of SGLT-2 inhibitors (e.g., Empagliflozin)?

Back: Inhibits glucose reabsorption in the proximal convoluted tubule, causing glucosuria.

Front: Specific benefit of SGLT-2 inhibitors in comorbid patients?

Back: Reduction in heart failure hospitalizations and progression of CKD.

Front: Common side effect of SGLT-2 inhibitors?

Back: Euglycemic DKA and Genitourinary yeast infections.

Front: Mechanism of GLP-1 agonists (e.g., Liraglutide, Semaglutide)?

Back: Increases glucose-dependent insulin secretion, slows gastric emptying, increases satiety.

Front: GLP-1 agonists are contraindicated in patients with a history of?

Back: Medullary Thyroid Cancer or MEN 2 (due to C-cell hyperplasia risk).

Front: Clinical triad of Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA)?

Back: Hyperglycemia, Ketosis (Anion Gap Metabolic Acidosis), and Dehydration.

Front: Best initial step in management of DKA?

Back: Aggressive IV fluid resuscitation (Normal Saline).

Front: When should Potassium be added to IV fluids in DKA management?

Back: When serum K+ is < 5.2 mEq/L (insulin will shift K+ intracellularly).

Front: Distinctive feature of Hyperosmolar Hyperglycemic State (HHS) compared to DKA?

Back: Extreme hyperglycemia (> 600 mg/dL), high serum osmolality, and absence of significant ketoacidosis.

Front: Screening frequency for diabetic retinopathy and nephropathy?

Back: Annually (starting at diagnosis for T2DM; 5 years after diagnosis for T1DM).

Front: First sign of diabetic nephropathy on labs?

Back: Moderately increased albuminuria (Microalbuminuria, 30–300 mg/day).

Section 2: Thyroid Disorders

Front: Most common cause of Hypothyroidism in iodine-sufficient regions?

Back: Hashimoto Thyroiditis (Chronic Autoimmune Thyroiditis).

Front: Best initial screening test for thyroid function?

Back: Serum TSH.

Front: Serum TSH and Free T4 levels in Subclinical Hypothyroidism?

Back: Elevated TSH with normal Free T4.

Front: Serum TSH and Free T4 levels in Secondary (Central) Hypothyroidism?

Back: Low or inappropriately normal TSH with low Free T4.

Front: Most common cause of Hyperthyroidism?

Back: Graves Disease.

Front: Pathognomonic physical exam findings in Graves Disease?

Back: Exophthalmos (proptosis) and Pretibial Myxedema.

Front: Antibody associated with Graves Disease?

Back: Thyroid Stimulating Immunoglobulin (TSI) or TSH-receptor antibodies.

Front: Radioactive Iodine Uptake (RAIU) pattern in Graves Disease?

Back: Diffuse, increased uptake.

Front: RAIU pattern in Toxic Multinodular Goiter?

Back: Patchy, focal areas of increased uptake ("Hot" nodules).

Front: RAIU pattern in Subacute (de Quervain) Thyroiditis?

Back: Decreased or low uptake (due to release of preformed hormone/inflammation).

Front: Clinical presentation of de Quervain Thyroiditis?

Back: Painful/tender thyroid gland, often following a viral URI.

Front: Management of de Quervain Thyroiditis?

Back: NSAIDs and Beta-blockers (self-limiting).

Front: Serious side effect of Thionamides (PTU/Methimazole)?

Back: Agranulocytosis.

Front: Preferred Thionamide in the first trimester of pregnancy?

Back: Propylthiouracil (PTU).

Front: Preferred Thionamide for most non-pregnant patients and 2nd/3rd trimester?

Back: Methimazole (due to lower hepatotoxicity).

Front: Characteristic EKG finding in Myxedema Coma?

Back: Bradycardia and low voltage complexes.

Front: Treatment for Thyroid Storm?

Back: Propranolol, PTU, IV Iodine (1 hour after PTU), and Glucocorticoids.

Front: Most common type of Thyroid Cancer?

Back: Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma.

Front: Histological hallmarks of Papillary Thyroid Carcinoma?

Back: Orphan Annie eye nuclei and Psammoma bodies.

Front: Thyroid cancer associated with MEN 2A and 2B?

Back: Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma (derived from C-cells; produces Calcitonin).

Front: Management of a thyroid nodule with low TSH?

Back: Perform Thyroid Scintigraphy (RAIU scan).

Front: Management of a thyroid nodule > 1cm with normal/high TSH?

Back: Fine Needle Aspiration (FNA).

Section 3: Adrenal Gland Disorders

Front: Most common cause of Primary Adrenal Insufficiency (Addison's) worldwide?

Back: Tuberculosis (Autoimmune in developed countries).

Front: Electrolyte abnormalities in Primary Adrenal Insufficiency?

Back: Hyponatremia, Hyperkalemia, and Hyperchloremic Metabolic Acidosis.

Front: Why does skin hyperpigmentation occur in Addison's Disease?

Back: High ACTH levels lead to increased Melanocyte-Stimulating Hormone (MSH) production.

Front: Best initial test for suspected Adrenal Insufficiency?

Back: 8 AM Serum Cortisol and ACTH.

Front: Definitive diagnostic test for Adrenal Insufficiency?

Back: Cosyntropin (ACTH) Stimulation Test.

Front: Management of Acute Adrenal Crisis?

Back: High-dose IV Hydrocortisone and aggressive IV fluids.

Front: Difference between Cushing Syndrome and Cushing Disease?

Back: Syndrome is any cause of hypercortisolism; Disease is specifically a pituitary ACTH-secreting adenoma.

Front: Most common overall cause of Cushing Syndrome?

Back: Exogenous glucocorticoid administration.

Front: First-line screening tests for Cushing Syndrome (choose one)?

Back: 24-hour urinary free cortisol, late-night salivary cortisol, or Low-dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test.

Front: Results of High-dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test in Cushing Disease?

Back: Cortisol levels suppress by > 50%.

Front: Results of High-dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test in Ectopic ACTH (e.g., Small Cell Lung Cancer)?

Back: No suppression of cortisol.

Front: Classic triad of Pheochromocytoma?

Back: Episodic headache, diaphoresis, and tachycardia (with hypertension).

Front: Best initial screening test for Pheochromocytoma?

Back: Plasma or 24-hour urinary metanephrines.

Front: Pre-operative management order for Pheochromocytoma?

Back: Alpha-blockade (Phenoxybenzamine) first, then Beta-blockade.

Front: Primary Hyperaldosteronism (Conn Syndrome) triad?

Back: Hypertension, Hypokalemia, and Metabolic Alkalosis.

Front: Screening test for Primary Hyperaldosteronism?

Back: Plasma Aldosterone to Plasma Renin Activity (PAC/PRA) ratio > 20.

Front: Most common cause of Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH)?

Back: 21-hydroxylase deficiency.

Front: Lab finding in 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

Back: Elevated 17-hydroxyprogesterone.

Section 4: Pituitary and Hypothalamus

Front: Most common functional (secretory) pituitary adenoma?

Back: Prolactinoma.

Front: Clinical presentation of Prolactinoma in women?

Back: Galactorrhea, amenorrhea, and infertility.

Front: First-line treatment for Prolactinomas?

Back: Dopamine agonists (Cabergoline or Bromocriptine).

Front: Gold standard test for diagnosing Acromegaly?

Back: Oral Glucose Tolerance Test (failure to suppress Growth Hormone).

Front: Screening test for Acromegaly?

Back: Serum Insulin-like Growth Factor-1 (IGF-1).

Front: Clinical features of Acromegaly?

Back: Enlarged hands/feet, frontal bossing, macroglossia, and new-onset Sleep Apnea or DM.

Front: Definition of Diabetes Insipidus (DI)?

Back: Passage of large volumes (> 3L/day) of dilute urine (Osmolality < 300).

Front: Central vs. Nephrogenic DI response to Desmopressin (DDAVP)?

Back: Central DI shows > 50% increase in urine osmolality; Nephrogenic DI shows little to no response.

Front: Common medication cause of Nephrogenic DI?

Back: Lithium.

Front: Electrolyte hallmark of SIADH?

Back: Euvolemic Hyponatremia.

Front: Management of mild SIADH?

Back: Fluid restriction.

Front: Dangerous complication of correcting hyponatremia too rapidly?

Back: Osmotic Demyelination Syndrome (Central Pontine Myelinolysis).

Front: Safe rate of sodium correction in chronic hyponatremia?

Back: < 8-10 mEq/L in 24 hours.

Front: Definition of Sheehan Syndrome?

Back: Ischemic necrosis of the pituitary gland following severe postpartum hemorrhage.

Front: Early sign of Sheehan Syndrome?

Back: Failure to lactate (loss of prolactin).

Section 5: Calcium and Bone Metabolism

Front: Most common cause of Primary Hyperparathyroidism?

Back: Solitary Parathyroid Adenoma.

Front: Lab findings in Primary Hyperparathyroidism?

Back: Elevated Calcium, Low Phosphorus, Elevated PTH, Elevated Vitamin D.

Front: Radiographic finding classic for Hyperparathyroidism?

Back: Osteitis Fibrosa Cystica (salt-and-pepper skull, subperiosteal bone resorption).

Front: Primary cause of Secondary Hyperparathyroidism?

Back: Chronic Kidney Disease (low Vitamin D/High Phos leading to high PTH).

Front: Definition of Tertiary Hyperparathyroidism?

Back: Autonomous PTH secretion following long-standing secondary hyperparathyroidism.

Front: Chvostek and Trousseau signs indicate?

Back: Hypocalcemia.

Front: EKG finding in Hypocalcemia?

Back: Prolonged QT interval.

Front: EKG finding in Hypercalcemia?

Back: Shortened QT interval.

Front: First-line treatment for symptomatic or severe Hypercalcemia (>14)?

Back: Aggressive IV hydration (Normal Saline).

Front: T-score range for Osteopenia?

Back: -1.0 to -2.5.

Front: T-score for Osteoporosis?

Back: $\le -2.5$.

Front: First-line pharmacological treatment for Osteoporosis?

Back: Bisphosphonates (e.g., Alendronate).

Section 6: Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia (MEN)

Front: Components of MEN 1 (3 Ps)?

Back: Pituitary adenoma, Parathyroid hyperplasia, Pancreatic tumors (Zollinger-Ellison).

Front: Components of MEN 2A (1 M, 2 Ps)?

Back: Medullary thyroid cancer, Pheochromocytoma, Parathyroid hyperplasia.

Front: Components of MEN 2B (2 Ms, 1 P)?

Back: Medullary thyroid cancer, Marfanoid habitus/Mucosal neuromas, Pheochromocytoma.

Front: Gene mutation associated with MEN 2A and 2B?

Back: RET proto-oncogene.

Section 7: Reproductive Endocrinology (Internal Medicine context)

Front: Most common cause of hirsutism and infertility in women?

Back: Polycystic Ovary Syndrome (PCOS).

Front: Lab ratio suggestive of PCOS?

Back: LH:FSH ratio > 2:1 (though not diagnostic).

Front: Primary risk associated with untreated PCOS?

Back: Endometrial Hyperplasia/Cancer (due to unopposed estrogen).

Front: Definition of Klinefelter Syndrome karyotype?

Back: 47, XXY.

Front: Physical findings in Klinefelter Syndrome?

Back: Small firm testes, gynecomastia, tall stature, and primary hypogonadism.

Front: Definition of Kallmann Syndrome?

Back: Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism with anosmia (failed migration of GnRH neurons).

Section 8: Miscellaneous High-Yield

Front: Serum marker for monitoring Medullary Thyroid Cancer recurrence?

Back: Calcitonin.

Front: Serum marker for monitoring Papillary/Follicular Thyroid Cancer recurrence?

Back: Thyroglobulin.

Front: Diagnosis of VIPoma (WDHA Syndrome)?

Back: Watery Diarrhea, Hypokalemia, Achlorhydria.

Front: Diagnosis of Glucagonoma?

Back: Necrolytic Migratory Erythema, Weight loss, and Diabetes.

Front: Primary hormone deficiency in Polyglandular Autoimmune Syndrome Type 1?

Back: Hypoparathyroidism, Adrenal Insufficiency, and Mucocutaneous Candidiasis.

Front: "Rule of 10s" for Pheochromocytoma?

Back: 10% bilateral, 10% malignant, 10% extra-adrenal, 10% familial.

Front: Effect of Estrogen (Pregnancy/OCPs) on thyroid lab values?

Back: Increases Thyroid Binding Globulin (TBG), increasing Total T4, but Free T4 remains normal.

Front: Most common cause of death in patients with Acromegaly?

Back: Heart Failure (dilated cardiomyopathy).

Front: Medical treatment to reduce GH in Acromegaly?

Back: Octreotide or Lanreotide (Somatostatin analogs).

Front: Definition of Nelson Syndrome?

Back: Rapid enlargement of an ACTH-secreting pituitary adenoma after bilateral adrenalectomy.

Section 9: Advanced Diabetes Management & Complications
Front: What is the "Somogyi Effect"?
Back: Rebound hyperglycemia in the morning caused by a response to hypoglycemia during the night.

Front: What is the "Dawn Phenomenon"?
Back: Morning hyperglycemia due to the natural early-morning surge of Growth Hormone and Cortisol.

Front: Preferred screening test for Diabetic Gastroparesis?
Back: Gastric emptying study (scintigraphy) with a solid meal.

Front: First-line medical treatment for Diabetic Gastroparesis?
Back: Metoclopramide or Erythromycin.

Front: Management of Diabetic Neuropathy pain?
Back: Gabapentin, Pregabalin, or Duloxetine.

Front: Most common cause of death in patients with Diabetes Mellitus?
Back: Cardiovascular disease (Myocardial Infarction).

Front: Diagnostic criteria for Metabolic Syndrome?
Back: 3 of 5: Large waist, High TG, Low HDL, HTN, High fasting glucose.

Front: Mechanism of Thiazolidinediones (e.g., Pioglitazone)?
Back: Activates PPAR-gamma to increase peripheral insulin sensitivity.

Front: Major side effects of Pioglitazone?
Back: Weight gain, edema, and worsening of Heart Failure.

Front: Mechanism of Alpha-glucosidase inhibitors (e.g., Acarbose)?
Back: Inhibits intestinal enzymes to delay carbohydrate absorption.

Section 10: Specific Thyroid Scenarios
Front: Management of Amiodarone-induced Thyrotoxicosis Type 1 (Jod-Basedow)?
Back: Thionamides (Methimazole/PTU).

Front: Management of Amiodarone-induced Thyrotoxicosis Type 2 (Destructive)?
Back: Glucocorticoids.

Front: Thyroid finding in Riedel Thyroiditis?
Back: "Rock-hard," fixed, painless goiter (fibrosis may extend to adjacent structures).

Front: Definition of "Euthyroid Sick Syndrome"?
Back: Low T3, normal/low T4, and normal/low TSH in the setting of severe non-thyroidal illness.

Front: Most common cause of Euthyroid Sick Syndrome?
Back: Critical illness or starvation (decreased peripheral conversion of T4 to T3).

Front: Management of Euthyroid Sick Syndrome?
Back: Observation and treatment of the underlying illness (do not give levothyroxine).

Front: Risk of untreated Subclinical Hyperthyroidism in the elderly?
Back: Atrial Fibrillation and Osteoporosis.

Front: Thyroid cancer that spreads hematogenously?
Back: Follicular Thyroid Carcinoma.

Front: Why can't FNA distinguish Follicular Adenoma from Follicular Carcinoma?
Back: Carcinoma requires visual evidence of capsular or vascular invasion (not seen on cytology).

Section 11: Adrenal Nuances & Surgery
Front: Most common cause of Secondary Adrenal Insufficiency?
Back: Chronic glucocorticoid therapy followed by abrupt withdrawal.

Front: Differentiator between Primary and Secondary Adrenal Insufficiency on labs?
Back: Secondary has normal Mineralocorticoid (Aldosterone) levels and no hyperpigmentation.

Front: Management of an incidentally discovered adrenal mass (Incidentaloma)?
Back: Screen for function (24hr catecholamines, cortisol, and renin/aldosterone).

Front: When is surgery indicated for an adrenal incidentaloma?
Back: If it is functional (hormone-secreting) or > 4cm in size.

Front: Management of Primary Hyperaldosteronism due to bilateral adrenal hyperplasia?
Back: Aldosterone antagonists (Spironolactone or Eplerenone).

Front: Mechanism of Metyrapone?
Back: Inhibits 11-beta-hydroxylase, blocking cortisol synthesis (used in Cushing's testing).

Front: Electrolyte finding in Waterhouse-Friderichsen Syndrome?
Back: Acute adrenal insufficiency (low Na+, high K+) due to adrenal hemorrhage.

Section 12: Hypothalamic & Pituitary Details
Front: Triad of McCune-Albright Syndrome?
Back: Precocious puberty, Café-au-lait spots (irregular borders), and Polyostotic fibrous dysplasia.

Front: Most common cause of Hyperprolactinemia besides Prolactinoma?
Back: Medications (especially Antipsychotics/Dopamine antagonists).

Front: Mechanism of Pituitary Apoplexy?
Back: Sudden hemorrhage or infarction of a pituitary adenoma.

Front: First step in suspected Pituitary Apoplexy with visual loss?
Back: Urgent surgical decompression and high-dose glucocorticoids.

Front: Difference between Psychogenic Polydipsia and DI?
Back: In Psychogenic Polydipsia, serum sodium is typically low (dilutional).

Front: Lab finding in Empty Sella Syndrome?
Back: Often incidental and asymptomatic; pituitary function is usually normal.

Front: Most common tumor of the pituitary region in children?
Back: Craniopharyngioma.

Front: Imaging characteristic of Craniopharyngioma?
Back: Suprasellar mass with calcifications on CT.

Section 13: Lipid Metabolism (Harrison's Highlights)
Front: First-line therapy for isolated Hypertriglyceridemia (> 500 mg/dL)?
Back: Fibrates (to prevent Pancreatitis).

Front: Mechanism of Statins?
Back: HMG-CoA Reductase inhibitors (upregulate LDL receptors).

Front: Most common side effect of Statins?
Back: Myalgia and (rarely) Rhabdomyolysis.

Front: Mechanism of Ezetimibe?
Back: Inhibits cholesterol absorption at the brush border of the small intestine.

Front: Primary side effect of Niacin?
Back: Skin flushing (mediated by prostaglandins; treat with Aspirin).

Front: Type I Dyslipidemia (Hyperchylomicronemia) deficiency?
Back: Lipoprotein Lipase (LPL) or ApoC-II deficiency.

Front: Physical finding in Type II Hypercholesterolemia?
Back: Tendon Xanthomas (Achilles) and Xanthelasma.

Front: Physical finding in Hypertriglyceridemia?
Back: Eruptive Xanthomas (yellow papules on back/buttocks).

Section 14: Bone & Mineral (Williams Textbook Focus)
Front: Definitive treatment for Vitamin D deficiency (Osteomalacia)?
Back: Ergocalciferol or Cholecalciferol (Vitamin D2 or D3) supplementation.

Front: Lab values in Vitamin D deficiency?
Back: Low Ca, Low Phosphorus, High PTH.

Front: Hallmark of Paget’s Disease of Bone on labs?
Back: Isolated, severely elevated Alkaline Phosphatase with normal Ca/Phos/PTH.

Front: First-line treatment for Paget’s Disease of Bone?
Back: Bisphosphonates (to inhibit osteoclast activity).

Front: Management of Hungry Bone Syndrome (post-parathyroidectomy)?
Back: Aggressive Calcium and Vitamin D supplementation.

Front: Cause of Pseudohypoparathyroidism?
Back: End-organ resistance to PTH (Albright Hereditary Osteodystrophy).

Front: Lab profile of Pseudohypoparathyroidism?
Back: Low Calcium, High Phosphorus, High PTH.

Section 15: Metabolic Emergency & "Must-Knows"
Front: Best fluid choice for a patient in Hypercalcemic Crisis?
Back: 0.9% Normal Saline (aggressive rate).

Front: Why is Loop Diuretic (Furosemide) use restricted in Hypercalcemia?
Back: Only used after volume repletion to prevent fluid overload.

Front: Standard medical management for Symptomatic Hypocalcemia?
Back: IV Calcium Gluconate.

Front: Most common cause of hypercalcemia in the outpatient setting?
Back: Primary Hyperparathyroidism.

Front: Most common cause of hypercalcemia in the inpatient setting?
Back: Malignancy (PTHrP or bone metastases).

Front: How to calculate Corrected Calcium?
Back: Corrected Ca = [Measured Ca] + 0.8 * (4 - Albumin).

Front: Immediate treatment for severe Hyperkalemia with EKG changes?
Back: IV Calcium Gluconate (to stabilize the cardiac membrane).

Front: Hormonal cause of secondary Hypertension in a young patient with abdominal bruit?
Back: Renal Artery Stenosis (Secondary Hyperaldosteronism).

Front: Characteristic of Glucagonoma rash?
Back: Necrolytic Migratory Erythema (presents in intertriginous areas).

Front: Hormone elevated in Carcinoid Syndrome?
Back: Serotonin (5-HIAA in urine).

Front: Clinical symptoms of Carcinoid Syndrome?
Back: Flushing, diarrhea, and right-sided heart valve lesions.

Front: Why does Carcinoid Syndrome usually require liver metastasis?
Back: Primary tumor secretions are normally metabolized by the liver (first-pass).

Section 16: Endocrine Hypertension & Renovascular
Front: Screening test for Renal Artery Stenosis?
Back: Renal Duplex Ultrasound or CT/MR Angiography.

Front: Effect of Renal Artery Stenosis on Renin and Aldosterone?
Back: High Renin, High Aldosterone (Secondary Hyperaldosteronism).

Front: Most common cause of Renal Artery Stenosis in young women?
Back: Fibromuscular Dysplasia.

Front: Most common cause of Renal Artery Stenosis in elderly men?
Back: Atherosclerosis.

Front: Classic finding in Fibromuscular Dysplasia on angiography?
Back: "String of beads" appearance of the renal artery.

Front: Why are ACE inhibitors contraindicated in bilateral Renal Artery Stenosis?
Back: They can cause acute renal failure by reducing efferent arteriolar tone/filtration pressure.

Section 17: Rare Endocrine Tumors & Syndromes
Front: Clinical presentation of Insulinoma?
Back: Whipple’s Triad: Symptoms of hypoglycemia, low blood glucose, and relief of symptoms with glucose.

Front: C-peptide levels in Insulinoma vs. Exogenous Insulin use?
Back: Elevated in Insulinoma; Low/Suppressed in Exogenous Insulin use.

Front: Diagnosis of Somatostatinoma?
Back: Triad of Diabetes, Cholelithiasis (gallstones), and Steatorrhea.

Front: Primary symptom of a Gastrinoma (Zollinger-Ellison Syndrome)?
Back: Multiple, refractory, or distal peptic ulcers and chronic diarrhea.

Front: Best initial test for Gastrinoma?
Back: Fasting serum gastrin level (>1000 pg/mL is diagnostic).

Front: Secretin Stimulation Test result in Gastrinoma?
Back: Paradoxical increase in serum gastrin levels.

Section 18: Pediatric & Pubertal Transitions (Internal Medicine Prep)
Front: Definition of Delayed Puberty in boys?
Back: Absence of testicular enlargement (volume < 4mL) by age 14.

Front: Definition of Delayed Puberty in girls?
Back: Absence of breast development (Thelarche) by age 13.

Front: Most common cause of Constitutional Delay of Growth and Puberty?
Back: Genetic/Familial (delayed "bone age" matches height age rather than chronological age).

Section 19: Advanced Electrolytes & Hormone Interactions
Front: Effect of Hypomagnesemia on Potassium levels?
Back: Refractory Hypokalemia (K+ cannot be corrected until Mg2+ is replaced).

Front: Effect of Hypomagnesemia on PTH secretion?
Back: Inhibits PTH secretion and causes end-organ resistance to PTH, leading to hypocalcemia.

Front: What is "Hungry Bone Syndrome"?
Back: Severe hypocalcemia and hypophosphatemia following parathyroidectomy in patients with high preoperative bone turnover.

Front: Medical management of Hypercalcemia of Malignancy (refractory to fluids)?
Back: IV Bisphosphonates (Zoledronic acid) or Denosumab.

Section 20: Rapid-Fire Management "Next Steps"
Front: Next step for a patient with a "Cold" nodule on Thyroid Scan?
Back: Fine Needle Aspiration (FNA).

Front: Next step for a patient with a "Hot" nodule on Thyroid Scan?
Back: Evaluate for hyperthyroidism; rarely malignant.

Front: Management of a pregnant patient with Hyperthyroidism?
Back: PTU in 1st trimester; Methimazole in 2nd and 3rd trimesters.

Front: Preferred imaging for localized Pheochromocytoma?
Back: CT or MRI of the abdomen/pelvis.

Front: Imaging used if CT/MRI fails to find a suspected Pheochromocytoma?
Back: MIBG (Metaiodobenzylguanidine) scan.

Front: When to screen for Gestational Diabetes?
Back: Between 24 and 28 weeks of gestation.

Front: Initial treatment for Gestational Diabetes?
Back: Dietary modification and exercise.

Front: Preferred pharmacotherapy for Gestational Diabetes if lifestyle fails?
Back: Insulin (Metformin/Glyburide are second-line in some guidelines).

Front: Lab finding that distinguishes 21-Hydroxylase deficiency from 11-beta-Hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: 11-beta-Hydroxylase deficiency presents with Hypertension (due to 11-deoxycorticosterone).

Front: Major risk of over-replacing Levothyroxine in the elderly?
Back: Atrial fibrillation and bone mineral density loss.

Final Synthesis: Clinical "Gems"
Front: Common cause of falsely low HbA1c?
Back: Conditions with high red blood cell turnover (e.g., Hemolytic Anemia, EPO therapy).

Front: Common cause of falsely high HbA1c?
Back: Iron deficiency anemia (longer RBC lifespan).

Front: What is the "Rule of 1s" for Addison's crisis?
Back: 1 liter of NS every 1 hour, plus 100mg Hydrocortisone.

Front: Primary hormonal deficiency in a patient with Pituitary Stalk Transection?
Back: All pituitary hormones decrease EXCEPT Prolactin (which increases due to loss of dopamine inhibition).

Front: What is "Apoplectic" presentation?
Back: Sudden onset of "worst headache of life," visual field defects, and ophthalmoplegia.

Front: Target HbA1c for most non-pregnant adults with DM2?
Back: < 7.0%.

Front: Primary reason to use Eplerenone over Spironolactone?
Back: Avoidance of anti-androgenic side effects like gynecomastia.

Front: Diagnostic test for Acromegaly after IGF-1 is found elevated?
Back: Glucose suppression test (GH should drop below 1 ng/mL after glucose load).

Section 21: Complex Metabolic & Electrolyte Nuances

Front: What is the "Hooks Effect" in Prolactinoma testing?

Back: Extremely high prolactin levels can saturate the lab assay, leading to a falsely low report; requires sample dilution.

Front: Serum Calcium and Phosphorus levels in Vitamin D-Resistant Rickets (X-linked hypophosphatemia)?

Back: Normal Calcium, Low Phosphorus, and Low/Normal Vitamin D ($1,25,OH_2D$).

Front: Primary hormonal abnormality in Gitelman Syndrome?

Back: Mimics Thiazide diuretic use (Hypokalemia, Metabolic Alkalosis, Hypomagnesemia, and Hypocalciuria).

Front: Primary hormonal abnormality in Bartter Syndrome?

Back: Mimics Loop diuretic use (Hypokalemia, Metabolic Alkalosis, and Hypercalciuria).

Front: Definition of "Schmidt Syndrome" (APS Type 2)?

Back: Adrenal insufficiency + Autoimmune thyroid disease + Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus.

Section 22: Advanced Pharmacology & Interactions

Front: Effect of Cholestyramine on Levothyroxine absorption?

Back: Decreases absorption; Levothyroxine should be taken 4 hours apart from bile acid sequestrants.

Front: Mechanism of Denosumab?

Back: Monoclonal antibody against RANKL, inhibiting osteoclast maturation.

Front: Mechanism of Cinacalcet?

Back: Calcimimetic that increases the sensitivity of calcium-sensing receptors on the parathyroid gland to suppress PTH.

Front: Why is Pioglitazone avoided in patients with a history of bladder cancer?

Back: Potential increased risk of bladder malignancy.

Front: Major side effect of Mitotane?

Back: Adrenolytic effect used to treat adrenal cortex carcinoma; causes severe GI distress and CNS depression.

Section 23: Rare Syndromes & Pathophysiology

Front: Clinical presentation of Klinefelter Syndrome at puberty?

Back: Gynecomastia, sparse body hair, and "firm" small testes (testicular dysgenesis).

Front: Hormonal profile of Turner Syndrome (45, X)?

Back: Primary ovarian failure leading to high FSH and LH (Hypergonadotropic Hypogonadism).

Front: Most common cause of "Precocious Puberty" in boys?

Back: CNS tumors (e.g., hypothalamic hamartoma).

Front: Most common cause of "Precocious Puberty" in girls?

Back: Idiopathic (early activation of the HPO axis).

Front: What is "Liddle Syndrome"?

Back: Genetic mutation causing overactive ENaC channels; presents as hypertension, hypokalemia, and suppressed renin/aldosterone.

Section 24: Critical "Next Best Step" Scenarios

Front: Next step in a patient with suspected Pheochromocytoma and a family history of Medullary Thyroid Cancer?

Back: Genetic testing for RET proto-oncogene (MEN 2).

Front: Next step for a patient with symptomatic Hypercalcemia and a suppressed PTH?

Back: Measure PTHrP, 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D, and SPEP (screen for malignancy/sarcoid).

Front: Next step for a patient with new-onset Diabetes and high Ferritin levels?

Back: Genetic testing for HFE gene (Hereditary Hemochromatosis/Bronze Diabetes).

Front: Management of a patient with an LDL > 190 mg/dL?

Back: Immediate initiation of High-intensity Statin therapy regardless of 10-year risk score.

Front: Preferred treatment for a pregnant patient with Prolactinoma?

Back: Discontinue Dopamine agonists; monitor for visual field changes.

Section 25: The "Ridiculously Simple" Review

Front: Classic "Stone, Moans, Groans, and Psychic Overtones" refers to?

Back: Clinical symptoms of Hypercalcemia.

Front: Mnemonic for the causes of a "High Anion Gap" in Endocrinology?

Back: MUDPILES (specifically "D" for Diabetic Ketoacidosis).

Front: Why does Primary Hypothyroidism sometimes cause Hyperprolactinemia?

Back: High TRH acts as a weak prolactin-releasing factor.

Front: Characteristic of "Watery Diarrhea" in VIPoma?

Back: Persists even during fasting (secretory diarrhea).

Front: What is "Ertl’s sign"?

Back: A rare physical sign (thyroid bruits) sometimes heard in Graves disease.

Section 26: Diagnostic Thresholds Recap

Front: Minimum diameter for a thyroid nodule to warrant FNA if it has "high-risk" ultrasound features?

Back: $\ge 1$ cm.

Front: Fasting glucose level defining "Impaired Fasting Glucose"?

Back: 100 mg/dL to 125 mg/dL.

Front: Random blood glucose level + symptoms required for DM diagnosis?

Back: $\ge 200$ mg/dL.

Front: Normal range for 24-hour urinary free cortisol?

Back: < 90 mcg/day (varies slightly by lab, but high-yield for "normal" cutoffs).

Front: Urine osmolality in a patient with Primary Polydipsia?

Back: Low (< 100 mOsm/kg), as the kidneys are maximally diluting urine to excrete water.

Section 27: Hormone Resistance & Genetic Variants
Front: Cause of Pseudohypoparathyroidism Type 1a?
Back: Defect in the Gs-protein alpha subunit (maternal inheritance).

Front: Phenotype of Albright Hereditary Osteodystrophy?
Back: Short stature, round face, short 4th and 5th metacarpals, and subcutaneous calcifications.

Front: Lab profile of Vitamin D-dependent Rickets Type II?
Back: High 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D levels due to vitamin D receptor resistance.

Front: Clinical sign of Severe Insulin Resistance (Type A)?
Back: Severe Acanthosis Nigricans and virilization in young women.

Front: Difference between MODY (Maturity-Onset Diabetes of the Young) and Type 2 DM?
Back: MODY is autosomal dominant, occurs in non-obese young patients, and is due to a single gene defect in beta-cell function.

Section 28: Paraneoplastic & Ectopic Endocrine Syndromes
Front: Most common cause of Ectopic ACTH production?
Back: Small Cell Lung Cancer.

Front: Paraneoplastic cause of Hypercalcemia in Squamous Cell Lung Cancer?
Back: PTH-related protein (PTHrP) production.

Front: Endocrine cause of Erythrocytosis (high Hgb/Hct)?
Back: Pheochromocytoma or Cushing’s Syndrome (due to EPO-like effects or cortisol's marrow stimulation).

Front: Syndrome associated with Primary Adrenal Lymphoma?
Back: Bilateral adrenal enlargement and primary adrenal insufficiency.

Section 29: Thyroid & Metabolic Nuances
Front: Result of a Perchlorate Discharge Test?
Back: Identifies defects in thyroid iodine organification.

Front: Definition of "Pemberton’s Sign"?
Back: Facial flushing and neck vein distension when raising arms, indicating a large substernal goiter.

Front: Serum marker that is elevated in Thyroid Lymphoma?
Back: Anti-TPO antibodies (often arises in the background of Hashimoto's).

Front: Preferred management for a "toxic" (hyperfunctioning) thyroid nodule?
Back: Radioactive Iodine Ablation or Surgery (Methimazole is usually bridge therapy).

Section 30: Advanced Lipidology & Metabolism
Front: Characteristic finding in Type III Dysbetalipoproteinemia?
Back: Palmar xanthomas and elevations in IDL (Intermediate-Density Lipoprotein).

Front: Genetic defect in Type III Dysbetalipoproteinemia?
Back: ApoE-2 homozygosity (results in decreased clearance of chylomicron remnants).

Front: Effect of high-dose Glucocorticoids on Calcium metabolism?
Back: Decreases intestinal calcium absorption and increases renal calcium excretion (leads to osteoporosis).

Front: Primary cause of death in untreated Primary Hyperparathyroidism?
Back: Actually usually unrelated to the PTH; however, severe "Hypercalcemic Crisis" can lead to cardiac arrest.

Section 31: Specific Drug-Induced Endocrine Changes
Front: Effect of Spironolactone on Testosterone?
Back: Competitively inhibits the androgen receptor and decreases testosterone synthesis (causes gynecomastia).

Front: Medication that can cause both Hypothyroidism and Hyperthyroidism?
Back: Amiodarone (due to high iodine content).

Front: Effect of Phenytoin/Rifampin on Vitamin D metabolism?
Back: Induces P450 enzymes, leading to increased catabolism of Vitamin D and potential osteomalacia.

Front: Why is Radioiodine therapy contraindicated in Graves' patients with severe Ophthalmopathy?
Back: It can acutely worsen the eye disease by increasing TSH-receptor antibody titers.

Section 32: The "Last Look" USMLE Pearls
Front: Most common cause of Primary Hyperparathyroidism in a patient with MEN 1?
Back: Parathyroid Hyperplasia (multiglandular) rather than a single adenoma.

Front: Diagnostic test for suspected Cushing's in a patient with Renal Failure?
Back: Late-night salivary cortisol (24-hour urine cortisol is unreliable in renal failure).

Front: Preferred steroid for replacement in Addison’s to mimic natural rhythm?
Back: Hydrocortisone (divided doses, 2/3 in morning, 1/3 in afternoon).

Front: Requirement for adding Fludrocortisone in Adrenal Insufficiency?
Back: Only needed in Primary Adrenal Insufficiency (to replace Mineralocorticoids).

Front: Clinical presentation of "Apathetic Hyperthyroidism"?
Back: Depression, lethargy, and weight loss in elderly patients with thyrotoxicosis (missing the usual "hyper" symptoms).

Front: Definition of "Wolff-Chaikoff Effect"?
Back: Reduction in thyroid hormone levels caused by the ingestion of a large amount of iodine.

Front: Definition of "Jod-Basedow Phenomenon"?
Back: Iodine-induced hyperthyroidism in a patient with a pre-existing multinodular goiter.

Section 33: Enzymatic Deficiencies (CAH Variations)
Front: Characteristic finding in 17-alpha-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: Hypertension and Hypokalemia with female phenotype (or undescended testes in males).

Front: Primary steroid excess in 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: 11-deoxycorticosterone (leads to hypertension).

Front: Distinguishing feature between 21-hydroxylase and 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency?
Back: 21-hydroxylase deficiency causes hypotension; 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency causes hypertension.

Front: Primary hormone deficiency in 3-beta-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase deficiency?
Back: Deficiency of all adrenal and gonadal steroid hormones.

Section 34: Hypoglycemia & Non-Diabetes Pancreatic Issues
Front: Diagnosis of Non-insulinoma Pancreatogenous Hypoglycemia Syndrome (NIPHS)?
Back: Postprandial hypoglycemia caused by beta-cell hypertrophy (not a tumor).

Front: Mechanism of Factitious Hypoglycemia via Sulfonylurea?
Back: Elevated Insulin and elevated C-peptide (mimics Insulinoma; requires urine drug screen).

Front: What is the "72-hour fast" used for?
Back: The gold standard for diagnosing Insulinoma in a controlled inpatient setting.

Front: Treatment for symptomatic hypoglycemia in a patient with an Inoperable Insulinoma?
Back: Diazoxide (inhibits insulin secretion) or Octreotide.

Section 35: Metabolic Bone Disease Fine-Tuning
Front: Effect of Hyperthyroidism on bone metabolism?
Back: Increased bone resorption leading to hypercalciuria and potentially osteoporosis.

Front: Lab finding in Hypophosphatemic Rickets?
Back: Low serum phosphorus with inappropriately low or normal 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D.

Front: Why is Vitamin D supplementation essential in Hypoparathyroidism?
Back: Lack of PTH prevents the conversion of 25-OH Vitamin D to its active 1,25-OH form.

Front: Radiographic sign of Scurvy (Vitamin C deficiency) in children?
Back: Wimberger’s ring sign (circular calcification around the epiphyseal center).

Section 36: Neuroendocrinology & Sleep
Front: Effect of Sleep Apnea on the endocrine system?
Back: Can cause secondary polycythemia and is highly associated with insulin resistance/Type 2 DM.

Front: Hormone secreted by the Pineal Gland?
Back: Melatonin.

Front: Effect of Craniopharyngioma on growth in children?
Back: Growth failure due to Growth Hormone Deficiency (pituitary compression).

Section 37: Endocrine Changes in Systemic Disease
Front: Endocrine manifestation of Sarcoidosis?
Back: Hypercalcemia (due to 1-alpha-hydroxylase activity in macrophages) and Central Diabetes Insipidus.

Front: Endocrine manifestation of Hemochromatosis?
Back: Central Hypogonadism (iron deposition in pituitary) and "Bronze" Diabetes (pancreatic deposition).

Front: Common endocrine side effect of Checkpoint Inhibitors (Immunotherapy)?
Back: Hypophysitis (inflammation of the pituitary gland).

Section 38: Pregnancy & Reproductive Nuances
Front: Effect of Pregnancy on Total T4 and T3?
Back: Increases due to high Estrogen-induced Thyroid Binding Globulin (TBG).

Front: Why is hCG able to stimulate the Thyroid gland?
Back: The alpha-subunit of hCG is identical to TSH.

Front: Definition of "Postpartum Thyroiditis"?
Back: Autoimmune thyroiditis occurring within 1 year of delivery; usually transient hyperthyroid followed by hypothyroid.

Front: Management of Gestational Hypothyroidism?
Back: Aggressive Levothyroxine replacement; requirements often increase by 30–50% during pregnancy.

Section 39: The "Differential" Master List
Front: Differential for a "Low TSH, Low Free T4"?
Back: Central (Secondary) Hypothyroidism or Euthyroid Sick Syndrome.

Front: Differential for "High PTH, High Calcium"?
Back: Primary Hyperparathyroidism or Familial Hypocalciuric Hypercalcemia (FHH).

Front: How to distinguish FHH from Primary Hyperparathyroidism?
Back: FHH has a low 24-hour urinary calcium excretion (UCCE < 0.01).

Front: Differential for "Low PTH, High Calcium"?
Back: Malignancy, Vitamin D toxicity, or Sarcoidosis.

Front: Differential for "High PTH, Low Calcium"?
Back: Secondary Hyperparathyroidism (usually CKD or Vitamin D deficiency) or Pseudohypoparathyroidism.

Section 40: "Ridiculously Simple" Clinical Logic
Front: Why do we treat Hyperkalemia with Insulin and Glucose?
Back: Insulin drives potassium into the cells by stimulating the Na+/K+ ATPase pump.

Front: Mnemonic for MEN 1?
Back: The 3 Ps: Pituitary, Parathyroid, Pancreas.

Front: Mnemonic for MEN 2A?
Back: The 1 M, 2 Ps: Medullary Thyroid, Pheochromocytoma, Parathyroid.

Front: Mnemonic for MEN 2B?
Back: The 2 Ms, 1 P: Medullary Thyroid, Marfanoid Habitus/Mucosal Neuromas, Pheochromocytoma.

Section 41: Endocrine Oncology & Genetic Markers

Front: Screening test for family members of a patient with Medullary Thyroid Cancer?

Back: DNA testing for RET proto-oncogene mutations.

Front: Serum marker used to monitor treatment response in Carcinoid tumors?

Back: Chromogranin A.

Front: Most common site for an extra-adrenal Pheochromocytoma (Paraganglioma)?

Back: The Organ of Zuckerkandl (near the aortic bifurcation).

Front: Diagnostic test for suspected Cushing's when initial screening is equivocal?

Back: Inferior Petrosal Sinus Sampling (IPSS) to distinguish pituitary vs. ectopic source.

Front: Hormone typically secreted by a "non-functioning" pituitary adenoma?

Back: Gonadotropins (specifically the alpha-subunit), though they rarely cause a clinical syndrome.

Section 42: Specialized Management Nuances

Front: When is surgery (Parathyroidectomy) indicated for asymptomatic Primary Hyperparathyroidism?

Back: Age < 50, Calcium > 1.0 mg/dL above normal, T-score < -2.5, or GFR < 60.

Front: Initial medical management for a patient with a known VIPoma?

Back: Octreotide (to control diarrhea) and aggressive fluid/electrolyte replacement.

Front: Preferred treatment for "Toxic Multinodular Goiter"?

Back: Radioactive Iodine (131-I) or surgery (methimazole is not a definitive cure).

Front: First-line management for Central Diabetes Insipidus?

Back: Desmopressin (DDAVP), available in oral, nasal, or parenteral forms.

Front: First-line management for Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus?

Back: HCTZ (Thiazide diuretics), NSAIDs, and a low-salt diet.

Section 43: Endocrine Emergencies (Detailed)

Front: Serum Glucose level diagnostic of "Hypoglycemia" in a non-diabetic patient?

Back: < 55 mg/dL with associated symptoms.

Front: Calculation for Serum Osmolality?

Back: $2 \times [Na^+] + \frac{Glucose}{18} + \frac{BUN}{2.8}$.

Front: First step in managing a patient with suspected Thyroid Storm?

Back: Propranolol (to control sympathetic overactivity).

Front: Management of Myxedema Coma?

Back: IV Levothyroxine ($T_4$), IV Liothyronine ($T_3$), and stress-dose Glucocorticoids.

Front: Why must Glucocorticoids be given before $T_4$ in Myxedema Coma?

Back: To prevent precipitating an adrenal crisis if the patient has co-existing adrenal insufficiency.

Section 44: Reproductive & Gonadal Nuances

Front: Definition of "Primary Amenorrhea"?

Back: No menses by age 15 with secondary sex characteristics, or by age 13 without them.

Front: Most common cause of Primary Amenorrhea with a present uterus?

Back: Turner Syndrome (45, X).

Front: Most common cause of Primary Amenorrhea with an absent uterus?

Back: Müllerian Agenesis (Mayer-Rokitansky-Küster-Hauser syndrome).

Front: Distinguishing feature of Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome (AIS) from Müllerian Agenesis?

Back: AIS has male-level testosterone and absent pubic/axillary hair.

Front: Hormone elevated in 11-beta-hydroxylase deficiency but low in 21-hydroxylase deficiency?

Back: 11-deoxycorticosterone (DOC).

Section 45: "Ridiculously Simple" Electrolyte Memory Hooks

Front: How does "Acidosis" affect ionized Calcium levels?

Back: Increases ionized Calcium (hydrogen ions displace calcium from albumin).

Front: How does "Alkalosis" affect ionized Calcium levels?

Back: Decreases ionized Calcium (leads to increased binding to albumin; can cause tetany).

Front: Mnemonic for the effects of Glucagon?

Back: GREAT: Glycogenolysis, Releases Insulin, Energy (fatty acid oxidation), Acetone (ketogenesis), Tops gluconeogenesis.

Section 46: Chronic Disease & Endocrine Overlap

Front: Most common endocrine abnormality in patients with Thalassemia Major?

Back: Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (due to iron overload in the pituitary).

Front: Endocrine association with Celiac Disease?

Back: Type 1 Diabetes and Autoimmune Thyroiditis (shared genetic predisposition).

Front: Why do patients with Chronic Kidney Disease (CKD) develop osteomalacia?

Back: Inability to perform 1-alpha-hydroxylation of Vitamin D.

=====================================================================
=====================================================================


Section: Internal Medicine
Topic: Hematology/Oncology — Anemias, leukemia, lymphoma, coagulopathies



Front: Most common cause of microcytic hypochromic anemia worldwide?

Back: Iron deficiency anemia.

Front: Initial and most sensitive lab test for diagnosing iron deficiency anemia?

Back: Serum ferritin level.

Front: Formula for Total Iron Binding Capacity (TIBC) relation to transferrin?

Back: TIBC is a direct measurement of serum transferrin levels.

Front: Pattern of serum iron and TIBC in iron deficiency anemia?

Back: Low serum iron and elevated TIBC.

Front: Pattern of serum iron and TIBC in Anemia of Chronic Disease (ACD)?

Back: Low serum iron and low or normal TIBC.

Front: Key inflammatory cytokine that mediates Anemia of Chronic Disease?

Back: Hepcidin.

Front: Mechanism by which hepcidin causes anemia of chronic disease?

Back: Binds to and degrades ferroportin, trapping iron inside macrophages and enterocytes.

Front: Microcytic anemia presenting with basophilic stippling and ringed sideroblasts?

Back: Lead poisoning.

Front: Enzyme inhibited by lead that leads to accumulation of protoporphyrin?

Back: Ferrochelatase (and ALA dehydratase).

Front: Definitve diagnostic test to distinguish Thalassemia trait from Iron Deficiency Anemia?

Back: Hemoglobin electrophoresis.

Front: Hemoglobin electrophoresis profile characteristic of Beta-Thalassemia minor?

Back: Elevated $HbA_2$ ($>3.5\%$) and slightly elevated HbF.

Front: Chromosome location of the alpha-globin genes?

Back: Chromosome 16.

Front: Chromosome location of the beta-globin genes?

Back: Chromosome 11.

Front: Clinical manifestation of a 3-alpha-globin gene deletion alpha-thalassemia?

Back: Hemoglobin H (HbH) disease, causing chronic hemolytic anemia.

Front: Composition of Hemoglobin H (HbH)?

Back: Tetramers of beta-globin ($\beta_4$).

Front: Clinical manifestation of a 4-alpha-globin gene deletion alpha-thalassemia?

Back: Hydrops fetalis (Hemoglobin Barts).

Front: Composition of Hemoglobin Barts?

Back: Tetramers of gamma-globin ($\gamma_4$).

Front: Classic skull X-ray finding in Beta-Thalassemia major due to extramedullary hematopoiesis?

Back: "Crew-cut" appearance (hair-on-end).

Macrocytic and Normocytic Anemias

Front: Distinctive peripheral blood smear finding that differentiates megaloblastic from non-megaloblastic macrocytic anemia?

Back: Hypersegmented neutrophils ($>5$ lobes).

Front: Metabolic product that builds up in Vitamin B12 deficiency but NOT in folate deficiency?

Back: Methylmalonic acid (MMA).

Front: Spinal cord pathology associated with severe Vitamin B12 deficiency?

Back: Subacute Combined Degeneration (SCD) of the spinal cord.

Front: Specific anatomical site of dietary Vitamin B12 absorption?

Back: Terminal ileum.

Front: Glycoprotein required for Vitamin B12 absorption that is deficient in pernicious anemia?

Back: Intrinsic Factor (IF).

Front: Primary site of dietary folate absorption?

Back: Jejunum.

Front: Classic triad of Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria (PNH)?

Back: Hemolytic anemia, pancytopenia, and venous thrombosis.
