Front: Flow cytometry findings diagnostic for Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria (PNH)?

Back: Absence of CD55 and CD59 on RBC surfaces.

Front: Mutated gene responsible for Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria?

Back: PIGA gene (phosphatidylinositol glycan anchor biosynthesis class A).

Front: Mode of inheritance for Hereditary Spherocytosis?

Back: Autosomal dominant.

Front: Most common molecular/protein defects in Hereditary Spherocytosis?

Back: Defects in ankyrin, spectrin, or band 3.

Front: Screening test of choice for diagnosing Hereditary Spherocytosis?

Back: Eosin-5-maleimide (EMA) binding test or osmotic fragility test.

Front: Definitive treatment to control hemolytic symptoms in Hereditary Spherocytosis?

Back: Splenectomy.

Front: Exact amino acid substitution that causes Sickle Cell Anemia?

Back: Glutamic acid to valine at position 6 of the beta-globin chain.

Front: Most common precipitating factor for a sickle cell vaso-occlusive crisis?

Back: Hypoxia, dehydration, or infection.

Front: Encapsulated organisms that autosplenectomized sickle cell patients are highly susceptible to?

Back: Streptococcus pneumoniae, Neisseria meningitidis, and Haemophilus influenzae.

Front: Maintenance medication used in Sickle Cell Anemia to increase Hemoglobin F production?

Back: Hydroxyurea.

Front: Most common organism causing osteomyelitis in patients with Sickle Cell Disease?

Back: Salmonella species.

Front: Mode of inheritance for Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency?

Back: X-linked recessive.

Front: Two classic structures visualized on a peripheral blood smear during a G6PD crisis?

Back: Heinz bodies and bite cells.

Front: Diagnostic test used to differentiate Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (AIHA) from hereditary hemolytic anemias?

Back: Direct Antiglobulin Test (DAT / Direct Coombs).

Front: Immunoglobulin type associated with Warm Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia?

Back: IgG.

Front: Immunoglobulin type associated with Cold Agglutinin Disease?

Back: IgM.

Front: Classic physical exam finding that differentiates Aplastic Anemia from leukemia or lymphoma?

Back: Absence of splenomegaly or lymphadenopathy.

Front: Bone marrow biopsy finding diagnostic for Aplastic Anemia?

Back: Hypocellular marrow replaced by fat cells.

Acute Leukemias

Front: Minimum percentage of bone marrow blasts required to diagnose Acute Myeloid Leukemia (AML)?

Back: $20\%$ or greater.

Front: Pathognomonic crystalline cytoplasmic inclusion bodies found in AML blasts?

Back: Auer rods.

Front: Chromosomal translocation pathognomonic for Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia (APL)?

Back: $\text{t}(15;17)$.

Front: Fusion gene product created by the $\text{t}(15;17)$ translocation?

Back: PML-RARA.

Front: Life-threatening coagulopathy frequently precipitated by Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia?

Back: Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation (DIC).

Front: Targeted medical therapy used to induce differentiation of blasts in APL?

Back: All-trans retinoic acid (ATRA) and arsenic trioxide.

Front: Histochemical stain used to differentiate AML blasts from ALL blasts?

Back: Myeloperoxidase (MPO) stain.

Front: Most common pediatric malignancy?

Back: Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia (ALL).

Front: Cell surface markers/enzymes characteristic of lymphoblasts in ALL?

Back: Terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase (TdT).

Front: Chromosomal translocation associated with a poor prognosis in adult B-cell ALL?

Back: $\text{t}(9;22)$ (Philadelphia chromosome).

Front: Prophylactic therapy required in all ALL treatment regimens to prevent relapse?

Back: Central Nervous System (CNS) prophylaxis (intrathecal chemotherapy).

Chronic Myeloproliferative and Lymphoproliferative Disorders

Front: Chromosomal translocation present in $>95\%$ of Chronic Myeloid Leukemia (CML) cases?

Back: $\text{t}(9;22)$.

Front: Constitutively active tyrosine kinase fusion protein produced in CML?

Back: BCR-ABL1.

Front: First-line targeted therapy for Chronic Myeloid Leukemia?

Back: Tyrosine kinase inhibitors (e.g., Imatinib, Dasatinib).

Front: Leukocyte Alkaline Phosphatase (LAP) score trend in CML vs. Leukemoid Reaction?

Back: Decreased in CML; elevated in Leukemoid Reaction.

Front: Terminal phase of CML characterized by $>20\%$ blasts in the blood or marrow?

Back: Blast crisis.

Front: Driver mutation found in $>95\%$ of cases of Polycythemia Vera?

Back: JAK2 V617F mutation.

Front: Classic pruritus symptom unique to Polycythemia Vera?

Back: Aquagenic pruritus (itching after a warm bath/shower).

Front: Primary therapeutic intervention to maintain hematocrit $<45\%$ in Polycythemia Vera?

Back: Therapeutic phlebotomy.

Front: Bone marrow biopsy feature hallmark of Primary Myelofibrosis?

Back: Extensive collagen fibrosis and megakaryocyte hyperplasia.

Front: Classic erythrocyte morphology seen on peripheral smear in Primary Myelofibrosis?

Back: Teardrop poikilocytes (dacrocytes).

Front: Most common leukemia of adults in Western countries?

Back: Chronic Lymphocytic Leukemia (CLL).

Front: Characteristic artifactual cell configuration seen on peripheral smear in CLL?

Back: Smudge cells.

Front: Immunophenotype co-expression pattern characteristic of CLL B-cells?

Back: CD5 and CD19/CD20/CD23.

Front: Name given to the transformation of CLL into an aggressive Diffuse Large B-cell Lymphoma?

Back: Richter transformation.

Lymphomas

Front: Pathognomonic giant, multinucleated cell type seen in Hodgkin Lymphoma?

Back: Reed-Sternberg (RS) cell.

Front: Immunophenotype markers typically expressed by classic Reed-Sternberg cells?

Back: CD15 and CD30.

Front: Most common histological subtype of Hodgkin Lymphoma?

Back: Nodular sclerosis subtype.

Front: Constitutional symptoms designated as "B symptoms" in lymphoma staging?

Back: Fever, drenching night sweats, and unexplained weight loss ($>10\%$ in 6 months).

Front: Name of the staging system used for Hodgkin and Non-Hodgkin Lymphomas?

Back: Ann Arbor Staging System (or Lugano classification).

Front: Most common subtype of Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma worldwide?

Back: Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma (DLBCL).

Front: Chromosomal translocation and gene associated with Burkitt Lymphoma?

Back: $\text{t}(8;14)$ causing c-myc overexpression.

Front: Characteristic histological description of a Burkitt Lymphoma biopsy?

Back: "Starry sky" appearance.

Front: Infection highly associated with endemic (African) Burkitt Lymphoma?

Back: Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV).

Front: Chromosomal translocation and gene associated with Follicular Lymphoma?

Back: $\text{t}(14;18)$ causing BCL-2 overexpression.

Front: Pathophysiological effect of BCL-2 overexpression in Follicular Lymphoma?

Back: Inhibition of apoptosis in B-cells.

Front: Chromosomal translocation and gene associated with Mantle Cell Lymphoma?

Back: $\text{t}(11;14)$ causing Cyclin D1 overexpression.

Plasma Cell Dyscrasias

Front: Diagnostic criteria that differentiates Multiple Myeloma from MGUS?

Back: Clonal bone marrow plasma cells $\ge 10\%$ OR biopsy-proven plasmacytoma, plus CRAB features.

Front: Components of the acronym CRAB used in Multiple Myeloma diagnosis?

Back: HyperCalcemia, Renal insufficiency, Anemia, and Bone lytic lesions.

Front: Monoclonal protein production description in MGUS without end-organ damage?

Back: Serum M-protein $<3\text{ g/dL}$ and bone marrow plasma cells $<10\%$.

Front: RBC stacking phenomenon seen on peripheral smear in Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Rouleaux formation.

Front: Free light chains cleared by the kidney and found in the urine of Myeloma patients?

Back: Bence-Jones proteins.

Front: Classic skull X-ray finding in Multiple Myeloma?

Back: "Punched-out" lytic bone lesions.

Front: Triad defining Waldenström Macroglobulinemia?

Back: Lymphoplasmacytic lymphoma in marrow, monoclonal IgM spike, and hyperviscosity.

Front: Primary clinical presentation unique to Waldenström Macroglobulinemia due to IgM size?

Back: Hyperviscosity syndrome (visual changes, neurological symptoms, bleeding).

Platelet Disorders and Coagulopathies

Front: Primary target of autoantibodies in Immune Thrombocytopenic Purpura (ITP)?

Back: Platelet GP IIb/IIIa or GP Ib/IX receptors.

Front: First-line medical therapy for an adult with symptomatic ITP and platelets $<30,000$?

Back: Corticosteroids (or IVIG).

Front: Pentad of clinical findings in Thrombotic Thrombocytopenic Purpura (TTP)?

Back: Thrombocytopenia, microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, fever, renal failure, neurological signs.

Front: Enzyme deficiency or autoantibody target responsible for TTP?

Back: ADAMTS13.

Front: Pathophysiological function of the ADAMTS13 enzyme?

Back: Cleaves ultra-large von Willebrand factor (vWF) multimers.

Front: Contraindicated treatment modality in TTP that can worsen thrombosis?

Back: Platelet transfusion.

Front: Essential first-line treatment for Thrombotic Thrombocytopenic Purpura (TTP)?

Back: Therapeutic plasma exchange (plasmapheresis).

Front: Triad characterizing Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS)?

Back: Microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury.

Front: Most common infectious trigger for typical HUS in children?

Back: Shiga toxin-producing Escherichia coli (STEC, O157:H7).

Front: Most common inherited bleeding disorder?

Back: von Willebrand Disease (vWD).

Front: Dual function of von Willebrand Factor in hemostasis?

Back: Mediates platelet adhesion to subendothelium and stabilizes Factor VIII.

Front: Pattern of coagulation studies (PT/aPTT/Bleeding Time) in von Willebrand Disease?

Back: Normal PT, normal or prolonged aPTT, prolonged bleeding time (or abnormal PFA-100).

Front: Initial medical treatment used to elevate endogenous vWF and Factor VIII levels?

Back: Desmopressin (DDAVP).

Front: Coagulation factor deficiency in Hemophilia A?

Back: Factor VIII deficiency.

Front: Coagulation factor deficiency in Hemophilia B?

Back: Factor IX deficiency.

Front: Mode of inheritance for Hemophilia A and B?

Back: X-linked recessive.

Front: Classic clinical bleeding manifestation of severe hemophilia?

Back: Hemarthrosis (bleeding into joints).

Front: Coagulation profile (PT, aPTT, Platelets) for Hemophilia A or B?

Back: Prolonged aPTT, normal PT, normal platelet count.

Front: Diagnostic mixing study result if prolonged aPTT is due to factor deficiency?

Back: The aPTT corrects to normal when mixed 1:1 with normal plasma.

Front: Diagnostic mixing study result if prolonged aPTT is due to a factor inhibitor?

Back: The aPTT does not correct when mixed 1:1 with normal plasma.

Front: Classic lab profile of Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation (DIC)?

Back: Prolonged PT, prolonged aPTT, decreased platelets, decreased fibrinogen, elevated D-dimer.

Hypercoagulable States (Thrombophilia)

Front: Most common inherited hypercoagulable state among Caucasians?

Back: Factor V Leiden.

Front: Molecular mechanism of Factor V Leiden mutation?

Back: Single nucleotide mutation rendering Factor V resistant to inactivation by Activated Protein C (APC).

Front: Molecular mechanism of the Prothrombin G20210A mutation?

Back: Mutation in the 3' untranslated region leading to increased prothrombin levels.

Front: Dermatological complication that can occur when initiating Warfarin without heparin bridging?

Back: Warfarin-induced skin necrosis.

Front: Inherited thrombophilias that increase risk for warfarin-induced skin necrosis?

Back: Protein C or Protein S deficiency.

Front: Target of autoantibodies in Antiphospholipid Syndrome (APS)?

Back: Lupus anticoagulant, Anticardiolipin antibodies, or Anti-beta2-glycoprotein I antibodies.

Front: Paradoxical laboratory finding in patients with Antiphospholipid Syndrome?

Back: Prolonged in vitro aPTT that does not correct with mixing studies, despite in vivo thrombosis.

Transfusion Medicine & Complications

Front: Acute transfusion reaction characterized by fever, chills, and flank pain due to ABO incompatibility?

Back: Acute Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction.

Front: Cause of Febrile Non-Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction?

Back: Cytokines accumulated in the stored blood product during storage.

Front: Primary preventive strategy to avoid Febrile Non-Hemolytic Transfusion Reactions?

Back: Leukoreduction of blood products.

Front: Pulmonary transfusion complication presenting with hypoxemia and non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema within 6 hours?

Back: Transfusion-Related Acute Lung Injury (TRALI).

Front: Pathophysiology driving Transfusion-Related Acute Lung Injury (TRALI)?

Back: Donor antibodies targeting recipient neutrophils in the pulmonary vasculature.

Front: Pulmonary transfusion complication presenting with hypoxemia, hypertension, and jugular venous distention?

Back: Transfusion-Associated Circulatory Overload (TACO).

Front: Delayed transfusion reaction occurring days to weeks later, presenting with mild jaundice and falling hemoglobin?

Back:状况 Delayed Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction.

Front: Primary mechanism behind Delayed Hemolytic Transfusion Reactions?

Back: Anamnestic antibody response against minor RBC antigens (e.g., Kidd, Kell).

Myelodysplastic Syndromes and Bone Marrow Failure

Front: Pathognomonic bone marrow finding in Myelodysplastic Syndrome (MDS) showing a ring of iron deposits around the nucleus?

Back: Ringed sideroblast.

Front: Distinctive bi-lobed neutrophil morphology ("sunglasses appearance") classic for Myelodysplastic Syndrome?

Back: Pelger-Huët anomaly.

Front: Most common cytogenetic deletion associated with a specific, favorable-prognosis subtype of MDS?

Back: 5q deletion (5q- syndrome).

Front: Targeted medical therapy of choice for transfusion-dependent patients with 5q- Myelodysplastic Syndrome?

Back: Lenalidomide.

Front: Inherited bone marrow failure syndrome presenting with aplastic anemia, short stature, absent thumbs, and café-au-lait spots?

Back: Fanconi Anemia.

Front: Definitive diagnostic screening test for Fanconi Anemia?

Back: Chromosome breakage analysis (using diepoxybutane or mitomycin C).

Front: Inherited pure red cell aplasia presenting in infancy with craniofacial abnormalities and triphalangeal thumbs?

Back: Diamond-Blackfan Anemia.

Hemolytic and Hemoglobinopathic Nuances

Front: Mechanism of RBC destruction in Warm Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia?

Back: Splenic macrophages pit the IgG-coated membrane, causing extravascular hemolysis and spherocyte formation.

Front: Mechanism of RBC destruction in Cold Agglutinin Disease?

Back: IgM binds RBCs in the cold, fixes complement, and C3b-coated RBCs are destroyed by hepatic Kupffer cells.

Front: Two classic infections that characteristically precipitate Cold Agglutinin Disease?

Back: Mycoplasma pneumoniae and Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV).

Front: Primary therapeutic choice for severe, symptomatic Warm Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia?

Back: Corticosteroids (or Rituximab).

Front: First-line management strategy for Cold Agglutinin Disease that avoids immunosuppressants?

Back: Avoidance of cold temperatures.

Front: Most common presentation of Sickle Cell Trait (heterozygous HbAS) on routine urinalysis?

Back: Microscopic hematuria (due to focal sickling in the renal medulla).

Front: Phenomenon causing acute, profound drop in hemoglobin in sickle cell patients, associated with a reticulocytopenia?

Back: Aplastic crisis.

Front: Most common viral pathogen responsible for triggering an aplastic crisis in sickle cell or hereditary spherocytosis patients?

Back: Parvovirus B19.

Front: Phenomenon causing an acute drop in hemoglobin in sickle cell patients, associated with splenomegaly and reticulocytosis?

Back: Splenic sequestration crisis.

Front: Leading cause of death in adult patients with Sickle Cell Disease?

Back: Acute Chest Syndrome.

Front: Diagnostic criteria for Acute Chest Syndrome in a sickle cell patient?

Back: New pulmonary infiltrate on chest X-ray plus fever, chest pain, tachypnea, or hypoxia.

Advanced Leukemia and Lymphoma Concepts

Front: Genetic mutation found in nearly all cases of Hairy Cell Leukemia?

Back: BRAF V600E mutation.

Front: Key histochemical stain historically used to identify Hairy Cell Leukemia cells?

Back: Tartrate-Resistant Acid Phosphatase (TRAP) stain.

Front: Preferred highly effective first-line purine analog therapy for Hairy Cell Leukemia?

Back: Cladribine (2-chlorodeoxyadenosine).

Front: Classic physical exam finding characteristically ABSENT in Hairy Cell Leukemia despite massive splenomegaly?

Back: Lymphadenopathy.

Front: The single most important prognostic factor for patients diagnosed with Hodgkin Lymphoma?

Back: Stage of the disease at the time of diagnosis.

Front: Unique physical symptom where lymph nodes become painful specifically after drinking alcohol?

Back: Alcohol-induced nodal pain in Hodgkin Lymphoma.

Front: Lymphoma subtype characterized by a "waxy," painless lymphadenopathy that fluctuates in size over years?

Back: Follicular Lymphoma.

Front: Histological finding that differentiates Follicular Lymphoma from benign reactive follicular hyperplasia?

Back: Monoclonal B-cell population and absence of tingible body macrophages in the follicles.

Front: Aggressive T-cell lymphoma presenting with lytic bone lesions, hypercalcemia, skin lesions, and "cloverleaf" T-cells?

Back: Adult T-Cell Leukemia/Lymphoma (ATLL).

Front: Causative viral pathogen direct linked to Adult T-Cell Leukemia/Lymphoma?

Back: Human T-Cell Leukemia Virus type 1 (HTLV-1).

Front: Cutaneous T-cell lymphoma characterized by cerebriform nuclei and skin plaques?

Back: Mycosis Fungoides.

Front: Term used when Mycosis Fungoides leukemizes, presenting with erythroderma and malignant T-cells in the peripheral blood?

Back: Sézary Syndrome.

Coagulation, Anticoagulation, and Platelet Pathology

Front: Laboratory marker used to monitor the therapeutic efficacy and dosing of Unfractionated Heparin?

Back: Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time (aPTT).

Front: Laboratory marker used to monitor the therapeutic efficacy and dosing of Warfarin?

Back: Prothrombin Time (PT) / International Normalized Ratio (INR).

Front: Antidote used for the immediate reversal of Unfractionated Heparin?

Back: Protamine sulfate.

Front: Specific reversal agent for the Direct Oral Anticoagulant (DOAC) Dabigatran?

Back: Idarucizumab.

Front: Specific reversal agent for Factor Xa inhibitors (Rivaroxaban, Apixaban)?

Back: Andexanet alfa.

Front: Pathophysiological mechanism behind Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT)?

Back: IgG antibodies form against the Heparin-Platelet Factor 4 (PF4) complex, activating platelets.

Front: Standard alternative anticoagulant of choice when HIT is confirmed or highly suspected?

Back: Direct thrombin inhibitors (e.g., Argatroban, Bivalirudin).

Front: Most accurate confirmatory laboratory assay for Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia?

Back: Serotonin Release Assay (SRA).

Front: Inherited condition presenting with prolonged bleeding time, normal platelet count, and giant platelets due to GP Ib-IX-V defect?

Back: Bernard-Soulier Syndrome.

Front: Inherited condition presenting with prolonged bleeding time, normal platelet count, and defective platelet aggregation due to GP IIb/IIIa defect?

Back: Glanzmann Thrombasthenia.

Front: Distinguishing aggregation assay result between Bernard-Soulier and Glanzmann Thrombasthenia?

Back: Ristocetin agglutination fixes Bernard-Soulier but does not correct Glanzmann; normal plasma corrects neither.

Front: Rare, life-threatening manifestation of severe DIC or meningococcemia presenting with bilateral adrenal hemorrhage and shock?

Back: Waterhouse-Friderichsen syndrome.

Oncological Emergencies and Support

Front: Metabolic triad and secondary complication defining Tumor Lysis Syndrome (TLS)?

Back: Hyperuricemia, hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, and hypocalcemia.

Front: Mechanism by which Tumor Lysis Syndrome induces acute kidney injury?

Back: Precipitation of uric acid or calcium phosphate crystals in the renal tubules.

Front: Prophylactic medication used to prevent Tumor Lysis Syndrome by inhibiting xanthine oxidase?

Back: Allopurinol.

Front: Recombinant uric acid oxidase enzyme used to treat established, severe hyperuricemia in TLS?

Back: Rasburicase.

Front: Absolute Neutrophil Count (ANC) threshold used to define severe neutropenia?

Back: $\text{ANC} < 500/\mu\text{L}$.

Front: Empiric monotherapy antibiotic class required immediately in a patient with febrile neutropenia?

Back: Antipseudomonal beta-lactam (e.g., Cefepime, Meropenem, Piperacillin/Tazobactam).

Front: Immediate medical management for a cancer patient presenting with thoracic back pain, hyperreflexia, and lower extremity weakness?

Back: Intravenous high-dose corticosteroids (followed by emergent MRI for spinal cord compression).

Front: Pathophysiology behind Leukostasis Syndrome in patients with ultra-high WBC counts ($>100,000/\mu\text{L}$)?

Back: Increased blood viscosity and blast plugs causing tissue hypoxia, particularly in the brain and lungs.

Iron Overload and Storage Disorders

Front: Classical triad of Hemochromatosis?

Back: Cirrhosis, diabetes mellitus ("bronze diabetes"), and skin hyperpigmentation.

Front: Most common genetic mutation associated with Hereditary Hemochromatosis?

Back: HFE gene mutation (C282Y or H63D).

Front: Transmembrane protein that is mutated or non-functional in Juvenile Hemochromatosis?

Back: Hemojuvelin or Hepcidin.

Front: Best screening lab evaluation for Hereditary Hemochromatosis?

Back: Transferrin saturation ($>45\%$) and serum ferritin.

Front: First-line management strategy for symptomatic Hereditary Hemochromatosis?

Back: Therapeutic phlebotomy.

Front: Preferred iron chelation therapy used for secondary iron overload due to chronic transfusions?

Back: Deferasirox (oral) or Deferoxamine (IV/SC).

Front: Quantitative organ assessment method considered the non-invasive gold standard for tracking tissue iron overload?

Back: T2* MRI of the liver and heart.

Porphyrias and Heme Synthesis Defects

Front: Enzyme deficiency responsible for Acute Intermittent Porphyria (AIP)?

Back: Porphobilinogen (PBG) deaminase.

Front: Classic clinical presentation pentad of Acute Intermittent Porphyria?

Back: Painful abdomen, Polyneuropathy, Psychiatic disturbances, Purple-red urine, and Precipitated by drugs/alcohol.

Front: Two primary compounds that accumulate in the urine during an Acute Intermittent Porphyria crisis?

Back: ALA (aminolevulinic acid) and PBG (porphobilinogen).

Front: Primary therapeutic intervention to abort an acute attack of AIP by suppressing ALA synthase?

Back: Intravenous Hemin (or high-dose glucose).

Front: Most common porphyria overall, presenting with blistering skin lesions on sun-exposed areas?

Back: Porphyria Cutanea Tarda (PCT).

Front: Enzyme deficiency responsible for Porphyria Cutanea Tarda?

Back: Uroporphyrinogen decarboxylase (UROD).

Front: Common chronic infectious disease strongly associated with the development of Porphyria Cutanea Tarda?

Back: Hepatitis C virus (HCV).

Myeloproliferative and Eosinophilic Variations

Front: Mutation strongly associated with Essential Thrombocythemia (ET) and Primary Myelofibrosis outside of JAK2?

Back: CALR (calreticulin) or MPL mutation.

Front: Platelet count threshold typically used to prompt evaluation for Essential Thrombocythemia?

Back: Platelets $>450,000/\mu\text{L}$ sustained on repeat checks.

Front: Phenomenon where severe thrombocytosis causes bleeding due to functional loss of high-molecular-weight vWF multimers?

Back: Acquired von Willebrand Syndrome.

Front: First-line cytoreductive therapy of choice for high-risk Essential Thrombocythemia?

Back: Hydroxyurea.

Front: Variant of Chronic Myelomonocytic Leukemia (CMML) requires what absolute monocyte count for diagnosis?

Back: Persistent peripheral blood monocytosis $\ge 1,000/\mu\text{L}$.

Front: Distinctive genetic fusion product seen in Chronic Eosinophilic Leukemia that responds exceptionally well to Imatinib?

Back: FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion gene.

Rare and Complex Lymphoma Entities

Front: Rare, highly aggressive Non-Hodgkin B-cell lymphoma with features intermediate between DLBCL and Burkitt lymphoma?

Back: High-grade B-cell lymphoma (often called "double-hit" or "triple-hit" lymphoma if MYC, BCL2, and/or BCL6 are rearranged).

Front: T-cell lymphoma subtype characterized by a "necklace" of malignant T-cells around subcutaneous fat cells, mimicking panniculitis?

Back: Subcutaneous Panniculitis-like T-cell Lymphoma.

Front: Extranodal lymphoma subtype arising in chronic inflammatory states, strongly associated with H. pylori infection in the stomach?

Back: MALT lymphoma (Mucosa-Associated Lymphoid Tissue lymphoma).

Front: Chromosomal translocation frequently driving MALT lymphoma that predicts resistance to H. pylori eradication therapy?

Back: $\text{t}(11;18)$.

Front: Aggressive extranodal T/NK-cell lymphoma presenting as a destructive midline facial mass, strongly associated with EBV?

Back: Extranodal NK/T-cell lymphoma, nasal type.

Front: Rare B-cell lymphoproliferative disorder characteristically localized to the pleural or pericardial cavities in HIV/AIDS patients, driven by HHV-8?

Back: Primary Effusion Lymphoma.

Complex Coagulation and Thrombophilia Concepts

Front: Autosomal dominant condition presenting with telangiectasias, recurrent epistaxis, and arteriovenous malformations (AVMs)?

Back: Hereditary Hemorrhagic Telangiectasia (Osler-Weber-Rendu syndrome).

Front: Primary autoantibody target responsible for Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia type II?

Back: Platelet Factor 4 (PF4)-heparin complexes.

Front: Name of the clinical pre-test probability scoring system used to risk-stratify suspected HIT?

Back: The 4Ts Score.

Front: Mechanistic explanation for why an isolated Factor XII deficiency prolongs aPTT but does NOT cause clinical bleeding?

Back: Factor XII is crucial for in vitro contact activation but redundant for in vivo coagulation initiation.

Front: Rare bleeding disorder characterized by normal PT, aPTT, and platelet counts, but presents with delayed wound healing and severe bleeding?

Back: Factor XIII deficiency.

Front: Definitive laboratory test used to screen for a suspected Factor XIII deficiency?

Back: 5M urea clot solubility test (or quantitative Factor XIII activity assay).

Front: Mechanism by which Dysfibrinogenemia can present simultaneously with both bleeding and thrombotic risks?

Back: Structural fibrinogen mutations alter either its cleavage by thrombin or its ability to bind tissue plasminogen activator (tPA).

Transfusion Reactions and Complications (Extended)

Front: Expected incremental rise in hemoglobin and platelet count after 1 unit of packed RBCs and 1 unit of apheresis platelets respectively?

Back: Hemoglobin rises by $1\text{ g/dL}$; platelet count rises by $30,000\text{ to }50,000/\mu\text{L}$.

Front: Transfusion reaction presenting with severe rigors and hyperthermia, caused by bacterial endotoxins contaminating the blood product?

Back: Septic transfusion reaction (most common with platelet transfusions due to room temperature storage).

Front: Fatal complication of transfusion occurring in immunocompromised hosts where donor T-cells attack recipient tissues?

Back: Transfusion-Associated Graft-Versus-Host Disease (TA-GVHD).

Front: Standard processing technique used to completely eliminate the risk of Transfusion-Associated Graft-Versus-Host Disease?

Back: Gamma or X-ray irradiation of cellular blood products.

Front: Phenomenon where severe, life-threatening thrombocytopenia develops 5-10 days after a transfusion due to alloantibodies against human platelet antigen-1a (HPA-1a)?

Back: Post-Transfusion Purpura (PTP).

Front: Preferred initial management strategy for Post-Transfusion Purpura?

Back: Intravenous Immunoglobulin (IVIG) or plasmapheresis.

Amyloidosis and Heavy Chain Diseases

Front: Protein precursor mismatch driving Primary Systemic Amyloidosis (AL)?

Back: Monoclonal immunoglobulin light chains (typically lambda).

Front: Characteristic organ biopsy finding when examining systemic amyloid deposits under polarized light microscopy?

Back: Apple-green birefringence under Congo red staining.

Front: Classic soft-tissue finding in AL amyloidosis characterized by macroglossia and purple periorbital discoloration?

Back: "Racoon eyes" (periorbital purpura).

Front: Most common protein precursor responsible for Secondary Systemic Amyloidosis (AA)?

Back: Serum Amyloid A (an acute-phase reactant driven by chronic inflammation).

Front: Plasma cell dyscrasia characterized by the production of truncated heavy chains without attached light chains?

Back: Heavy Chain Disease (e.g., Franklin disease, Seligmann disease).

Front: Rare systemic disorder presenting with Polyneuropathy, Organomegaly, Endocrinology abnormalities, Monoclonal plasma cell proliferative disorder, and Skin changes?

Back: POEMS syndrome.


Coagulation Cascade and Lab Interpretations

Front: Coagulation factor that initiates the extrinsic pathway of coagulation following vascular injury?

Back: Tissue Factor (Factor III).

Front: Coagulation factors unique to the intrinsic pathway?

Back: Factors XII, XI, IX, and VIII.

Front: Coagulation factors comprising the common pathway?

Back: Factors X, V, II (prothrombin), and I (fibrinogen).

Front: Only coagulation factor that does not circulate as a serine protease zymogen?

Back: Factor V (and Factor VIII; they act as cofactors).

Front: Coagulation factor responsible for cross-linking fibrin polymers to stabilize a clot?

Back: Factor XIII (Fibrin-stabilizing factor).

Front: Expected coagulation profile (PT and aPTT) in a patient with a severe Vitamin K deficiency?

Back: Both PT and aPTT are prolonged (PT typically prolongs first due to short half-life of Factor VII).

Front: Exact mechanism by which Warfarin acts as an anticoagulant?

Back: Inhibits Vitamin K Epoxide Reductase Complex 1 (VKORC1), preventing gamma-carboxylation of Factors II, VII, IX, X, and Proteins C/S.

Front: Mechanistic reason why Unfractionated Heparin prolongs aPTT more significantly than Low-Molecular-Weight Heparin (LMWH)?

Back: Unfractionated heparin binds antithrombin to inhibit both Thrombin and Factor Xa, whereas LMWH principally inhibits Factor Xa.

Front: Laboratory assay used to accurately monitor therapeutic levels of Low-Molecular-Weight Heparin when clinically indicated?

Back: Anti-Factor Xa assay.

Myelodysplastic/Myeloproliferative Overlap and Rare Anemias

Front: Distinctive bone marrow feature that distinguishes Chronic Myelomonocytic Leukemia (CMML) from CML?

Back: Absence of the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene and presence of persistent peripheral monocytosis.

Front: Diagnostic genetic deletion confirming Secondary Acute Myeloid Leukemia arising from prior MDS or chemotherapy?

Back: Deletion of chromosome 5 (del(5q)) or chromosome 7 (del(7q)).

Front: Rare congenital normocytic anemia presenting with triphalangeal thumbs and a striking increased risk of osteosarcoma and AML?

Back: Diamond-Blackfan Anemia.

Front: Autosomal recessive disorder presenting with progressive pancytopenia, café-au-lait spots, microcephaly, and a high risk of squamous cell carcinomas?

Back: Fanconi Anemia.

Front: Mechanistic cause of anemia in Lead Poisoning outside of enzyme inhibition?

Back: Inhibition of pyrimidine 5'-nucleotidase, causing ribosomal RNA degradation failure and subsequent basophilic stippling.

Front: Classic clinical triad of Plummer-Vinson Syndrome?

Back: Iron deficiency anemia, esophageal webs, and dysphagia.

Lymphoma and Leukemia Molecular Pathogenesis

Front: Immunophenotypic cell surface marker used to definitively identify classic Hodgkin Lymphoma Reed-Sternberg cells via immunohistochemistry?

Back: CD15 and CD30 positive (CD45 negative).

Front: Histological variant of Hodgkin Lymphoma characterized by "popcorn cells" that express B-cell markers like CD20?

Back: Nodular Lymphocyte Predominant Hodgkin Lymphoma (NLPHL).

Front: Chromosomal translocation and gene rearrangement characteristic of Anaplastic Large Cell Lymphoma (ALCL)?

Back: $\text{t}(2;5)$ resulting in the NPM-ALK fusion gene.

Front: Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma subtype characterized by primary involvement of the skin, presenting as generalized erythroderma, lymphadenopathy, and atypical T-cells with cerebriform nuclei?

Back: Sézary Syndrome (leukemic phase of Mycosis Fungoides).

Front: Unique oncogenic mechanism driving Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma (DLBCL) in up to $30\%$ of cases?

Back: BCL6 gene rearrangements or dysregulation on chromosome 3q27.

Front: Primary viral driver implicated in Primary Central Nervous System Lymphoma in immunocompromised/HIV patients?

Back: Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV).

Front: Translocation associated with a subset of Acute Myeloid Leukemias that carries a highly favorable prognosis?

Back: $\text{t}(8;21)$ involving the RUNX1-RUNX1T1 fusion.

Front: Cytogenetic abnormality in AML involving chromosome 16 that correlates with abnormal eosinophils in the bone marrow and a favorable prognosis?

Back: $\text{inv}(16)$ or $\text{t}(16;16)$.

Non-Malignant Hematological Emergencies

Front: First line medical therapy for acute, severe bleeding in a patient taking direct oral Factor Xa inhibitors?

Back: Andexanet alfa.

Front: Immediate management step for a sickle cell patient presenting with sudden-onset severe dyspnea, new chest infiltrate, and hypoxia?

Back: Emergent empiric antibiotics, supplemental oxygen, IV fluids, and consideration for exchange transfusion (Acute Chest Syndrome).

Front: Pathophysiological driver of priapism in male pediatric patients with Sickle Cell Disease?

Back: Vaso-occlusion and stasis of sickled erythrocytes within the corpora cavernosa.

Front: Vital intervention required immediately for a neutropenic cancer patient presenting with a single oral temperature reading of $\ge 38.3^\circ\text{C}$ ($101^\circ\text{F}$)?

Back: Draw blood cultures and immediately administer empiric broad-spectrum antipseudomonal IV antibiotics (e.g., Cefepime).

Front: Major electrolyte abnormality that can precipitate life-threatening cardiac arrhythmias during massive blood transfusion protocols?

Back: Hypocalcemia (caused by citrate preservative binding free calcium).

Histiocytic and Dendritic Cell Disorders

Front: Clonal proliferation of dendritic antigen-presenting cells presenting with lytic bone lesions, skin rashes, and diabetes insipidus?

Back: Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis (LCH).

Front: Pathognomonic electron microscopy finding in Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis?

Back: Birbeck granules (tennis-racket shaped structures).

Front: Key immunohistochemical markers used to confirm Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis?

Back: CD1a and Langerin (CD207) positivity.

Front: Highly aggressive, life-threatening syndrome characterized by cytopenias, hepatosplenomegaly, and hyperferritinemia due to overactivation of macrophages?

Back: Hemophagocytic Lymphohistiocytosis (HLH).

Front: Diagnostic ferritin threshold typically used as a major criterion for Hemophagocytic Lymphohistiocytosis?

Back: Serum ferritin $\ge 500\text{ }\mu\text{g/L}$ (often $>3,000\text{ }\mu\text{g/L}$ is highly suggestive).

Front: Common genetic mutations identified in adult presentations of Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis?

Back: BRAF V600E mutation (found in roughly $50\%$ of cases).

Advanced Hypercoagulable and Thrombotic Concepts

Front: Autosomal dominant condition presenting with recurrent venous thromboembolisms where standard therapeutic doses of Heparin fail to prolong the aPTT?

Back: Antithrombin (ATIII) deficiency.

Front: Specific clinical scenario where an Antiphospholipid Syndrome patient requires lifelong therapeutic anticoagulation with Warfarin over DOACs?

Back: Triple-positive Antiphospholipid Syndrome (positive for Lupus Anticoagulant, Anticardiolipin, and Anti-beta2-glycoprotein I).

Front: Rare condition presenting with a combination of arterial/venous thromboses, livedo reticularis, and recurrent ischemic strokes in young adults?

Back: Sneddon Syndrome.

Front: Mechanistic cause of neonatal purpura fulminans immediately after birth?

Back: Homozygous Protein C or Protein S deficiency.

Front: Coagulation test modification required to confirm the presence of a Lupus Anticoagulant?

Back: Demonstration of phospholipid-dependence using the Dilute Russell's Viper Venom Time (dRVVT) or high-phospholipid neutralization.

Front: Most common site of visceral vein thrombosis in patients diagnosed with Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria (PNH)?

Back: Hepatic vein thrombosis (Budd-Chiari syndrome).

Bone Marrow Microenvironment and Specialized Anemias

Front: Key stromal-derived cytokine essential for the survival, proliferation, and differentiation of early hematopoietic stem cells?

Back: Stem Cell Factor (SCF) (or c-Kit ligand).

Front: Specialized macrophage-containing structures in the bone marrow where erythroblast maturation occurs?

Back: Erythroblastic islands.

Front: Autosomal dominant pure red cell aplasia linked to ribosomal protein gene mutations ($RPS19$)?

Back: Diamond-Blackfan Anemia.

Front: Distinctive physical exam sign on the hands that differentiates Diamond-Blackfan Anemia from Fanconi Anemia?

Back: Triphalangeal thumbs in Diamond-Blackfan; absent or hypoplastic thumbs/radii in Fanconi.

Front: Acquired pure red cell aplasia (PRCA) is classically associated with what encapsulated mediastinal tumor?

Back: Thymoma.

Front: Rare, inherited normocytic anemia characterized by multinucleated erythroblasts and a positive acidified serum lysis (Ham) test?

Back: Congenital Dyserythropoietic Anemia (CDA).

Hemoglobinopathies: Structural Variants and Genetics

Front: Structural hemoglobin variant that causes Hemoglobin C disease?

Back: Lysine substitution for glutamic acid at position 6 of the beta-globin chain.

Front: Classic morphology of erythrocytes on a peripheral blood smear from a patient with Hemoglobin CC disease?

Back: HbC crystals (rod-shaped/rectangular structures) and target cells.

Front: Structural variant where glutamic acid is replaced by lysine at position 26 of the beta-globin chain, common in Southeast Asia?

Back: Hemoglobin E ($HbE$).

Front: Underlying genetic mechanism responsible for the vast majority of Alpha-Thalassemia cases?

Back: Gene deletions (large structural deletions via unequal crossing over).

Front: Underlying genetic mechanism responsible for the vast majority of Beta-Thalassemia cases?

Back: Point mutations (splicing, promoter, or nonsense mutations).

Front: Hemoglobin variant formed by two alpha chains and two delta chains ($\alpha_2\delta_2$)?

Back: Hemoglobin $A_2$ ($HbA_2$).

Front: Definitive quantitative assay used to precisely monitor Hemoglobin S levels in sickle cell patients undergoing exchange transfusion?

Back: High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC).

Immune and Non-Immune Hemolytic Pathophysiology

Front: Specific complement regulatory protein missing in PNH that normally degrades C3 convertases?

Back: CD55 (Decay-accelerating factor / DAF).

Front: Specific complement regulatory protein missing in PNH that normally prevents assembly of the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC)?

Back: CD59 (Membrane inhibitor of reactive lysis / MIRL).

Front: Humanized monoclonal antibody targeting complement component C5 used to treat Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria?

Back: Eculizumab (or Ravulizumab).

Front: Classic mechanism of cell destruction in Microangiopathic Hemolytic Anemia (MAHA)?

Back: Mechanical shearing of erythrocytes as they pass through fibrin-platelet meshworks in microvessels.

Front: Physical footprint of intravascular hemolysis measured via a decrease in which specific serum transport protein?

Back: Haptoglobin.

Front: Oxidation product of hemoglobin breakdown that accumulates in the urine during sustained, severe intravascular hemolysis?

Back: Hemosiderin (Hemosiderinuria).

Front: Protozoal intraerythrocytic infection that mimics G6PD deficiency by inducing hemolytic anemia and forming "Maltese cross" tetrads?

Back: Babesiosis.

Advanced Leukemia Classification and Cytogenetics

Front: Cell surface markers used to differentiate early T-cell ALL from B-cell ALL via flow cytometry?

Back: T-ALL expresses CD3, CD7, and CD2; B-ALL expresses CD19, CD10, and CD22.

Front: Chromosomal translocation $\text{t}(12;21)$ involving $ETV6-RUNX1$ confers what type of prognosis in pediatric B-ALL?

Back: Highly favorable prognosis.

Front: Chromosomal translocation $\text{t}(1;19)$ involving $TCF3-PBX1$ confers what type of prognosis in pediatric B-ALL?

Back: Unfavorable/poor prognosis.

Front: Specific WHO classification cytogenetic rearrangement in AML that involves the $RUNX1-RUNX1T1$ fusion?

Back: $\text{t}(8;21)$.

Front: Cytogenetic abnormality in AML that results in the $CBFB-MYH11$ fusion protein?

Back: $\text{inv}(16)$ or $\text{t}(16;16)$.

Front: The presence of NPM1 or CEBPA mutations in the absence of FLT3-ITD mutations in AML signals what prognosis?

Back: Favorable prognosis (in cytogenetically normal AML).

Front: Cell-of-origin classification marker that identifies the chronic phase of CML from leukemoid reactions?

Back: Low or zero Leukocyte Alkaline Phosphatase (LAP) score in CML.

Front: Splenic pathology classic for Chronic Myeloid Leukemia?

Back: Massive splenomegaly due to extensive extramedullary hematopoiesis in the red pulp.

Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma and T-Cell Malignancies

Front: Cell of origin for Follicular Lymphoma?

Back: Germinal center B-cells.

Front: Cell surface marker universally absent on normal B-cells but characteristically aberrant/positive on Chronic Lymphocytic Leukemia (CLL) B-cells?

Back: CD5.

Front: Pathognomonic tissue biopsy layout defining Mantle Cell Lymphoma?

Back: Monomorphic expansion of small-to-medium lymphocytes surrounding germinal centers in the mantle zone.

Front: Unique biological mechanism behind the growth of Gastric MALT lymphoma?

Back: $H.\text{ pylori}$-specific T-cells stimulate the malignant B-cell clone via cytokine signaling.

Front: Chromosomal translocation $\text{t}(11;18)$ in MALT lymphoma fuses which two genes?

Back: $API2$ and $MALT1$.

Front: Most common clinical presentation of Anaplastic Large Cell Lymphoma (ALCL) in young adults?

Back: Lymphadenopathy with skin/soft tissue involvement and systemic B symptoms.

Front: Hallmark cell morphology describing Anaplastic Large Cell Lymphoma?

Back: Large "hallmark" cells with kidney- or horseshoe-shaped nuclei and a prominent perinuclear halo.

Front: Target antigen for the antibody-drug conjugate Brentuximab Vedotin used in Hodgkin Lymphoma and ALCL?

Back: CD30.

Complex Hypercoagulability and Endothelial Pathology

Front: Autoimmune clotting disorder presenting with prolonged aPTT, livedo reticularis, and false-positive VDRL/RPR tests for syphilis?

Back: Antiphospholipid Syndrome (APS).

Front: Primary mechanism by which anti-beta2-glycoprotein I antibodies promote a prothrombotic state?

Back: Activation of endothelial cells, platelets, and monocytes, upregulating tissue factor expression.

Front: Inherited condition caused by a mutation in the prothrombin gene untranslated region that increases prothrombin mRNA stability?

Back: Prothrombin G20210A mutation.

Front: Most accurate timing to check Protein C or Protein S functional activity levels to evaluate for inherited deficiency?

Back: At least 2 weeks after terminating oral Warfarin therapy and when the patient is free from acute thrombosis.

Front: Rare, life-threatening complication of Unfractionated Heparin infusion presenting with a $>50\%$ drop in platelets within 5-10 days?

Back: Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT) Type II.

Front: Specific functional diagnostic confirmation test for HIT Type II that measures radioactive isotope emission?

Back: Serotonin Release Assay (SRA).

Platelet Function Defects and Vascular Purpuras

Front: Inherited qualitative platelet disorder due to a deficiency in the Glycoprotein Ib-IX-V complex?

Back: Bernard-Soulier Syndrome.

Front: Inherited qualitative platelet disorder due to a deficiency in the Glycoprotein IIb/IIIa complex?

Back: Glanzmann Thrombasthenia.

Front: Unique laboratory feature that differentiates Bernard-Soulier Syndrome from Glanzmann Thrombasthenia on a peripheral blood smear?

Back: Presence of giant platelets in Bernard-Soulier Syndrome.

Front: Effect of adding normal plasma to a platelet agglutination test using Ristocetin in a patient with Bernard-Soulier Syndrome?

Back: No correction (because the defect is in the structural platelet GP Ib receptor, not a plasma factor).

Front: Effect of adding normal plasma to a platelet agglutination test using Ristocetin in a patient with von Willebrand Disease?

Back: Correction (because normal plasma replenishes the missing von Willebrand Factor).

Front: Acquired vascular purpura caused by Vitamin C deficiency affecting collagen cross-linking?

Back: Scurvy.

Front: Classic perifollicular skin findings pathognomonic for severe Vitamin C deficiency?

Back: Perifollicular hemorrhages and "corkscrew" hairs.

Myelofibrosis and Secondary Myeloproliferative States

Front: Principal bone marrow cytokine responsible for driving the extensive reactive collagen fibrosis in Primary Myelofibrosis?

Back: Transforming Growth Factor-beta (TGF-$\beta$) (released from clonal megakaryocytes).

Front: Name given to the dry tap bone marrow aspiration attempt classic in Primary Myelofibrosis and Hairy Cell Leukemia?

Back: Punctio sicca.

Front: Most common physiological cause of a secondary polycythemia presenting with elevated erythropoietin (EPO) levels?

Back: Chronic hypoxemia (e.g., severe COPD, sleep apnea, high altitude) or EPO-secreting tumors.

Front: Paraneoplastic syndrome condition where a renal cell carcinoma or cerebellar hemangioblastoma causes erythrocytosis?

Back: Inappropriate secondary polycythemia (via autonomous EPO production).

Front: Pathophysiological driver of erythromelalgia (burning pain and erythema in extremities) in Essential Thrombocythemia?

Back: Platelet microvascular occlusion and subsequent endothelial inflammation.

Special Storage Profiles and Plasma Cell Variants

Front: Diagnostic plasma cell threshold required to classify a patient as Smoldering Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Serum M-protein $\ge 3\text{ g/dL}$ OR bone marrow clonal plasma cells $10\%\text{ to }60\%$ without CRAB features.

Front: Cytogenetic deletion strongly associated with high-risk Multiple Myeloma and short survival times?

Back: Deletion $17\text{p}$ ($del(17p)$ losing the p53 gene).

Front: Primary mode of therapeutic action for Immunomodulatory drugs (IMiDs like Lenalidomide) in Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Binding to Cereblon, leading to the selective degradation of Ikaros and Aiolos transcription factors.

Front: Mechanism of action for Bortezomib in the treatment of Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Reversible inhibition of the $26\text{S}$ proteasome, inducing unfolded protein stress and apoptosis.

Front: Specific organ dysfunction that makes the usage of systemic Bisphosphonates highly risky or contraindicated in Myeloma patients?

Back: Severe Renal Insufficiency ($\text{GFR} < 30\text{ mL/min}$).

Front: Diagnostic criteria for Light Chain Smoldering Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Involved/uninvolved serum free light chain ratio $\ge 100$ plus bone marrow plasma cells $<60\%$.

Coagulation Cascade Regulation and Thrombophilia Pathology

Front: Endothelial cell surface receptor that binds Thrombin to activate the Protein C pathway?

Back: Thrombomodulin.

Front: Co-factor that accelerates the inactivation of Factors Va and VIIIa by Activated Protein C?

Back: Protein S.

Front: Plasma inhibitor that directly neutralizes Tissue Factor-Factor VIIa-Factor Xa complexes?

Back: Tissue Factor Pathway Inhibitor (TFPI).

Front: Specific mechanism by which Antithrombin III neutralizes Thrombin and Factor Xa?

Back: Functions as a suicide substrate protease inhibitor, forming a stable 1:1 complex with the active site of the clotting factors.

Front: Specific structural component of the endothelial surface that naturally accelerates Antithrombin III activity?

Back: Heparan sulfate proteoglycans.

Front: Diagnostic pitfall when testing for Antithrombin III deficiency during an acute thrombotic event?

Back: Acute thrombosis can transiently consume Antithrombin III, leading to a false-positive diagnosis.

Front: Minimum duration a patient must be off oral Warfarin before testing for functional Protein C and S levels?

Back: Two weeks.

Hemolytic Anemias: Enzymatic and Membrane Details

Front: Metabolic pathway in which Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) operates to protect RBCs from oxidative stress?

Back: Hexose monophosphate (HMP) shunt.

Front: Key reducing equivalent generated by G6PD that maintains glutathione in its active state?

Back: NADPH.

Front: Molecular consequence to hemoglobin when NADPH is depleted under oxidative stress?

Back: Sulfhydryl groups on Hb cross-link, causing the protein to denature and precipitate as Heinz bodies.

Front: Specific physiological mechanism by which "bite cells" are formed in G6PD deficiency?

Back: Splenic macrophages pluck out denatured hemoglobin inclusions (Heinz bodies) from the erythrocytes.

Front: Diagnostic pitfall when testing G6PD enzyme activity levels immediately after an acute hemolytic crisis?

Back: False-negative result due to the destruction of older, enzyme-deficient RBCs and a high concentration of young, enzyme-replete reticulocytes.

Front: Transmembrane transport protein mutated in the majority of patients with autosomal dominant Hereditary Spherocytosis?

Back: Ankyrin (encoded by the ANK1 gene).

Front: Primary site of RBC destruction in Hereditary Spherocytosis?

Back: The splenic cords of Billroth (extravascular hemolysis).

Hemoglobinopathies and Quantitative Globin Defects

Front: Normal physiological composition of adult Hemoglobin A ($HbA$)?

Back: Two alpha-globin chains and two beta-globin chains ($\alpha_2\beta_2$).

Front: Normal physiological composition of fetal Hemoglobin F ($HbF$)?

Back: Two alpha-globin chains and two gamma-globin chains ($\alpha_2\gamma_2$).

Front: Exact amino acid substitution at position 6 of the beta-chain that results in Hemoglobin S ($HbS$)?

Back: Substitution of hydrophilic glutamic acid with hydrophobic valine.

Front: Molecular consequence when deoxygenated Hemoglobin S molecules aggregate?

Back: Hydrophobic interactions cause polymerization into long, rigid crystalline polymers that distort the RBC into a sickle shape.

Front: Exact amino acid substitution at position 6 of the beta-chain that results in Hemoglobin C ($HbC$)?

Back: Substitution of glutamic acid with lysine.

Front: Microscopic structural morphology typical of Hemoglobin C crystals seen in homozygous HbCC patients?

Back: Hexagonal, dark-staining, rod-like intracellular crystals.

Front: Pathophysiological mechanism behind the development of Beta-Thalassemia major?

Back: Severe reduction or complete absence of beta-globin synthesis leading to an excess of unpaired, unstable alpha-globin chains that precipitate and cause ineffective erythropoiesis.

Acute Leukemia Cytogenetics and Molecular Drivers

Front: Cytogenetic translocation $\text{t}(15;17)(\text{q}24;\text{q}21)$ fuses which two genes?

Back: PML (promyelocytic leukemia) and RARA (retinoic acid receptor alpha).

Front: Biological function disrupted by the PML-RARA fusion protein in Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia?

Back: Blocks myeloid differentiation at the promyelocytic stage by recruiting histone deacetylases to repress transcription.

Front: Cytogenetic translocation $\text{t}(8;21)(\text{q}22;\text{q}22)$ in AML fuses which two genes?

Back: RUNX1 (AML1) and RUNX1T1 (ETO).

Front: Cytogenetic inversion $\text{inv}(16)(\text{p}13.1\text{q}22)$ in Acute Myeloid Leukemia fuses which two genes?

Back: CBFB (core-binding factor subunit beta) and MYH11.

Front: Prognostic significance of $\text{t}(8;21)$ or $\text{inv}(16)$ in a patient with de novo Acute Myeloid Leukemia?

Back: Confers a favorable prognosis (classified as Core Binding Factor AML).

Front: Internal tandem duplication mutation in which gene confers a poor prognosis in cytogenetically normal AML?

Back: FLT3 (Fms-like tyrosine kinase 3).

Front: Cell surface protein configuration that characterizes the immunophenotype of standard Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia lymphoblasts?

Back: TdT positive, CD19 positive, and CD10 positive (Common ALL Antigen / CALLA).

Chronic Lymphoproliferative and Plasma Cell Disorders

Front: Clonal cell surface marker expression pattern that definitively differentiates CLL from Mantle Cell Lymphoma via flow cytometry?

Back: Both express CD5, but CLL is CD23 positive and Mantle Cell Lymphoma is CD23 negative.

Front: Histological pattern within lymph nodes when CLL manifests primarily as a solid tumor (Small Lymphocytic Lymphoma)?

Back: Diffuse effacement of nodal architecture with proliferation centers containing prolymphocytes and paraimmunoblasts.

Front: Laboratory parameter used in the Rai Staging System to classify Stage IV Chronic Lymphocytic Leukemia?

Back: Thrombocytopenia (platelets $<100,000/\mu\text{L}$).

Front: Diagnostic evaluation required to differentiate Smoldering Multiple Myeloma from Monoclonal Gammopathy of Undetermined Significance (MGUS)?

Back: Quantifying clonal bone marrow plasma cells ($10\text{ to }60\%$ in Smoldering vs. $<10\%$ in MGUS).

Front: Most common light-chain configuration found in systemic AL amyloidosis secondary to plasma cell dyscrasias?

Back: Lambda ($\lambda$) light chains.

Front: Mechanism by which Multiple Myeloma plasma cells induce lytic bone lesions?

Back: Secretion of RANKL and DKK1, which activates osteoclasts and suppresses osteoblasts.

Chronic Myeloproliferative Neoplasms

Front: Translocation $\text{t}(9;22)(\text{q}34.1;\text{q}11.2)$ relocates the c-ABL proto-oncogene to which specific gene site on chromosome 22?

Back: BCR (breakpoint cluster region).

Front: Intracellular signaling pathways constitutively activated by the BCR-ABL1 fusion tyrosine kinase?

Back: JAK/STAT, RAS/MAPK, and PI3K/AKT pathways.

Front: Specific JAK2 mutation found in nearly all cases of Polycythemia Vera?

Back: A valine-to-phenylalanine substitution at codon 617 (JAK2 V617F) in the pseudokinase domain.

Front: Pathophysiological effect of the JAK2 V617F mutation on hematopoietic growth factor receptors?

Back: Confers hypersensitivity to erythropoietin, thrombopoietin, and G-CSF, allowing cytokine-independent signaling.

Front: Major bone marrow biopsy feature that distinguishes Essential Thrombocythemia from Primary Myelofibrosis in early stages?

Back: ET shows loose clusters of giant, mature megakaryocytes without significant reticulin fibrosis.

Front: Serum erythropoietin (EPO) level expected in a patient with Polycythemia Vera vs. Secondary Polycythemia?

Back: Suppressed/low in Polycythemia Vera; elevated in Secondary Polycythemia.

Advanced Coagulopathies and Platelet Dysfunctions

Front: Target antigen of the autoantibodies driving Immune Thrombocytopenic Purpura (ITP)?

Back: Platelet membrane glycoproteins GPIIb/IIIa or GPIb/IX.

Front: Plasma metalloprotease responsible for cleaving ultra-large von Willebrand factor (vWF) multimers?

Back: ADAMTS13.

Front: Pathophysiological mechanism behind the microvascular thrombosis in Thrombotic Thrombocytopenic Purpura (TTP)?

Back: Accumulation of uncleaved ultra-large vWF multimers causes spontaneous platelet aggregation and microvascular shear stress.

Front: Crucial laboratory parameter that differentiates TTP/HUS from Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation (DIC)?

Back: Coagulation profiles (PT and aPTT) are normal in TTP/HUS but prolonged in DIC.

Front: Inherited structural defect responsible for Bernard-Soulier Syndrome?

Back: Deficiency or dysfunction of the GPIb-IX-V receptor complex on the platelet membrane.

Front: Inherited structural defect responsible for Glanzmann Thrombasthenia?

Back: Deficiency or dysfunction of the GPIIb/IIIa (integrin $\alpha_{\text{IIb}}\beta_3$) receptor complex on the platelet membrane.

Front: Laboratory aggregation pattern diagnostic for von Willebrand Disease when evaluating platelet-rich plasma?

Back: Impaired platelet agglutination to Ristocetin, which normalizes upon the addition of normal plasma.

Lymphoma Pathogenesis and Immunophenotypical Nuances

Front: Chromosomal translocation t(8;14)(q24;q32) characteristically moves which proto-oncogene to the immunoglobulin heavy chain locus?

Back: MYC.

Front: Chromosomal translocation t(14;18)(q32;q21) characteristically fuses the BCL2 gene to which locus?

Back: Immunoglobulin heavy chain (IGH) locus.

Front: Molecular mechanism by which BCL2 overexpression promotes cell survival in Follicular Lymphoma?

Back: It stabilizes the outer mitochondrial membrane, preventing the release of cytochrome c and inhibiting apoptosis.

Front: Chromosomal translocation t(11;14)(q13;q32) overexpresses which protein?

Back: Cyclin D1 (encoded by the CCND1 gene).

Front: Primary physiological function of Cyclin D1 that is dysregulated in Mantle Cell Lymphoma?

Back: Promotes transition from G1 to S phase of the cell cycle by phosphorylating and inactivating the Retinoblastoma (Rb) protein.

Front: Specific translocation t(11;18)(q21;q21) seen in gastric MALT lymphoma fuses which two genes?

Back: BIRC3 (API2) and MALT1.

Front: Immunophenotypic profile that characterizes classic Reed-Sternberg cells?

Back: CD15 positive, CD30 positive, and CD45 negative.

Front: Immunophenotypic profile that distinguishes Nodular Lymphocyte Predominant Hodgkin Lymphoma (NLPHL) from classic Hodgkin Lymphoma?

Back: Popcorn cells in NLPHL are CD20 positive, CD45 positive, CD15 negative, and CD30 negative.

Porphyrias and Disorders of Heme Biosynthesis

Front: Enzyme deficiency responsible for the clinical presentation of Acute Intermittent Porphyria (AIP)?

Back: Porphobilinogen deaminase (also known as hydroxymethylbilane synthase).

Front: Major neurotoxic precursors that accumulate in plasma and urine during an acute flare of AIP?

Back: Aminolevulinic acid (ALA) and porphobilinogen (PBG).

Front: Primary enzymatic step inhibited by lead poisoning in the heme synthesis pathway?

Back: Aminolevulinic acid dehydratase (ALA dehydratase) and ferrochelatase.

Front: Intermediate product that accumulates in erythrocytes as a direct consequence of lead poisoning?

Back: Zinc protoporphyrin.

Front: Enzyme deficiency responsible for Porphyria Cutanea Tarda (PCT)?

Back: Uroporphyrinogen decarboxylase.

Front: Classic skin pathology seen on sun-exposed areas of patients with Porphyria Cutanea Tarda?

Back: Subepidermal blistering, fragility, and hyperpigmentation due to porphyrin activation by UV light.

Front: First-line medical therapies used to downregulate heme synthesis during an acute attack of AIP?

Back: Intravenous hemin or high-dose glucose infusions (which repress hepatic ALA synthase-1).

Bone Marrow Failure and Myelodysplastic Pathology

Front: Pathognomonic bone marrow erythroid finding in myelodysplastic syndrome characterized by a ring of iron granules around the nucleus?

Back: Ring sideroblast.

Front: Exact organelle where iron accumulates abnormally to form the morphology of a ring sideroblast?

Back: Mitochondria.

Front: Genetic mutation strongly associated with the presence of ring sideroblasts in Myelodysplastic Syndrome?

Back: SF3B1 mutation (a spliceosome gene).

Front: Nuclear morphology change in neutrophils ("pseudo-Pelger-Huët anomaly") classic for Myelodysplastic Syndrome?

Back: Hyposegmented (bilobed or monolobed) neutrophilic nuclei.

Front: Chromosomal deletion associated with a specific myelodysplastic syndrome variant that responds dramatically to lenalidomide?

Back: Isolated deletion of the long arm of chromosome 5 (del(5q)).

Front: Molecular defect driving Fanconi Anemia?

Back: Defect in the multiprotein FANCA-complex responsible for DNA interstrand cross-link repair.

Front: Classic developmental phenotype that distinguishes Diamond-Blackfan Anemia from Fanconi Anemia?

Back: Diamond-Blackfan shows pure red cell aplasia and triphalangeal thumbs; Fanconi shows pancytopenia and absent or hypoplastic thumbs.

Specialized Hypercoagulable Conditions

Front: Structural alteration caused by the Factor V Leiden mutation?

Back: A single point mutation (Arg506Gln) at the cleavage site of Factor V.

Front: Functional consequence of the Arg506Gln mutation in Factor V Leiden?

Back: Renders Factor Va resistant to cleavage and inactivation by Activated Protein C (APC).

Front: Molecular basis of the Prothrombin G20210A mutation?

Back: A point mutation in the 3' untranslated region of the prothrombin gene that increases mRNA stability and prothrombin expression.

Front: Mechanism of hypercoagulability in a patient with an Antithrombin III deficiency?

Back: Failure to neutralize serine protease clotting factors (Thrombin, Factor Xa, Factor IXa).

Front: Mechanistic reason why initiating Warfarin therapy can induce temporary hypercoagulability?

Back: Warfarin depletes Protein C and Protein S (short half-lives) faster than it depletes clotting factors II, IX, and X.

Front: Diagnostic test used to confirm a Lupus Anticoagulant that utilizes viper venom?

Back: Dilute Russell's Viper Venom Time (dRVVT).

Front: True underlying pathophysiology behind the false-positive VDRL/RPR test in Antiphospholipid Syndrome?

Back: Antibodies cross-react with the cardiolipin antigen used in the syphilis screening assay.

Transfusion Complications and Precision Management

Front: Transfusion reaction presenting with fever, chills, and mild dyspnea within 1 to 6 hours, driven by pre-formed cytokines in the blood product bag?

Back: Febrile Non-Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction.

Front: Processing step utilized by blood banks to drastically reduce the incidence of Febrile Non-Hemolytic Transfusion Reactions?

Back: Leukoreduction (filtering out donor white blood cells prior to storage).

Front: Transfusion reaction presenting as acute hypoxemia and non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema within 6 hours of a transfusion?

Back: Transfusion-Related Acute Lung Injury (TRALI).

Front: Primary immunological driver behind Transfusion-Related Acute Lung Injury (TRALI)?

Back: Donor HLA or neutrophil-specific antibodies targeting and activating recipient neutrophils within the pulmonary vasculature.

Front: Clinical feature that distinguishes Transfusion-Associated Circulatory Overload (TACO) from TRALI?

Back: TACO presents with signs of volume overload, such as hypertension, elevated JVP, and response to diuretics.

Front: Storage requirement used to prevent Transfusion-Associated Graft-Versus-Host Disease (TA-GVHD) in immunocompromised recipients?

Back: Gamma or X-ray irradiation of cellular blood products to inactivate donor T-lymphocytes.

Front: Phenomenon where profound thrombocytopenia occurs 5 to 10 days post-transfusion due to alloantibodies against human platelet antigens?

Back: Post-Transfusion Purpura (PTP).

Histiocytic and Histological Borderlands

Front: Clonal neoplastic proliferation of dendritic cells presenting with lytic bone lesions, skin rash, and diabetes insipidus?

Back: Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis (LCH).

Front: Pathognomonic electron microscopy finding inside Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis cells?

Back: Birbeck granules (tennis-racket shaped cytoplasmic structures).

Front: Immunohistochemical cell surface markers positive in Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis?

Back: CD1a and Langerin (CD207).

Front: Aggressive, life-threatening syndrome characterized by cytopenias, massive hepatosplenomegaly, and hyperferritinemia driven by uninhibited macrophage activation?

Back: Hemophagocytic Lymphohistiocytosis (HLH).

Front: Principal cytokine that drives the hyperactivation of macrophages and subsequent hemophagocytosis in HLH?

Back: Interferon-gamma (IFN-γ).

Front: Histological pattern in lymph nodes showing an expansion of marginal zone B-cells that form a halo around reactive germinal centers?

Back: Marginal Zone Lymphoma.

Front: Rare plasma cell variant disorder presenting with polyneuropathy, organomegaly, endocrinopathy, monoclonal protein, and skin changes?

Back: POEMS syndrome.

Direct Oral Anticoagulants (DOACs) and Specific Reversal Agents

Front: Specific mechanism of action of Dabigatran?

Back: Direct, competitive thrombin (Factor IIa) inhibitor.

Front: Specific mechanism of action of Rivaroxaban, Apixaban, and Edoxaban?

Back: Direct, selective inhibitors of free and clot-bound Factor Xa.

Front: Monoclonal antibody fragment used for the immediate reversal of Dabigatran?

Back: Idarucizumab.

Front: Decoy receptor protein used to reverse direct Factor Xa inhibitors (Rivaroxaban and Apixaban)?

Back: Andexanet alfa.

Front: Standard coagulation screening test most prolonged by therapeutic doses of Dabigatran?

Back: Thrombin Time (TT) (and aPTT).

Front: Primary route of elimination for Dabigatran that requires strict dosage adjustments in renal failure?

Back: Renal clearance ($80\%$).

Secondary and Microangiopathic Thrombocytopenias

Front: Classic drug-induced thrombocytopenia mechanism associated with Quinine, Sulfa drugs, or Gold salts?

Back: Drug-dependent antibodies bind to platelet glycoproteins only in the presence of the drug, causing accelerated clearance.

Front: Pathophysiological mechanism behind Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT) Type I?

Back: Direct non-immunologic pro-aggregatory effect of heparin on platelets, occurring within 1–2 days and presenting with mild, transient thrombocytopenia.

Front: Minimum percentage drop in platelet count from baseline that defines a positive "4Ts" criteria component for HIT Type II?

Back: $>50\%$ drop in platelet count.

Front: Timing window post-heparin exposure during which HIT Type II characteristically manifests?

Back: 5 to 10 days (or within 24 hours if pre-exposed to heparin in the past 100 days).

Front: Standard non-heparin anticoagulant of choice for a patient with confirmed HIT who has concurrent severe hepatic impairment?

Back: Bivalirudin or Fondaparinux (Argatroban is avoided/dose-reduced due to hepatic metabolism).

Front: Pathophysiological driver of diarrhea-associated (typical) Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS)?

Back: Shiga toxin localizes to the renal endothelial cells, causing endothelial damage, microthrombi formation, and mechanical hemolysis.

Front: Alternative (atypical) Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (aHUS) is primarily driven by genetic mutations in what physiological pathway?

Back: Regulatory proteins of the alternative complement pathway (e.g., Factor H, Factor I, or CD46).

Front: Humanized monoclonal antibody targeting complement protein C5 used as first-line therapy for atypical HUS?

Back: Eculizumab.

Platelet Storage Pool and Adhesion Defects

Front: Specific substance stored inside platelet alpha-granules that is deficient in Gray Platelet Syndrome?

Back: Platelet-specific proteins (e.g., vWF, Fibrinogen, Platelet Factor 4, PDGF).

Front: Structural morphology of platelets on a peripheral blood smear in Gray Platelet Syndrome?

Back: Large, pale/gray platelets lacking normal purple azurophilic granulation.

Front: Specific substances stored inside platelet dense ($\delta$) granules that are deficient in Hermansky-Pudlak syndrome?

Back: ADP, ATP, Serotonin, and Calcium.

Front: Oculocutaneous albinism combined with a bleeding diathesis due to dense granule deficiency defines which syndrome?

Back: Hermansky-Pudlak syndrome.

Front: Quantitative functional test used to assess the initial stage of platelet adhesion in clinical practice?

Back: Platelet Function Analysis (PFA-100) closure time.

High-Yield Lymphoma Mimics and Subtypes

Front: Benign, self-limiting lymphadenopathy characterized by follicular hyperplasia and sheets of pale histiocytes with tingible body macrophages?

Back: Reactive follicular hyperplasia.

Front: Lymphoma variant that characteristically presents as an isolated, enlarging mass in the testis of an elderly man?

Back: Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma (DLBCL).

Front: Distinctive immunohistochemical marker used to identify Primary Central Nervous System Lymphoma cells on biopsy?

Back: CD20 positive (as they are almost universally diffuse large B-cell lymphomas).

Front: Rare B-cell variant that arises within the walls of a chronic fluid-filled cavity, universally driven by HHV-8?

Back: Primary Effusion Lymphoma.

Front: Slowly progressive T-cell lymphoma presenting with erythroderma, generalized lymphadenopathy, and circulating atypical T-cells with cerebriform nuclei?

Back: Sézary Syndrome.

Advanced Hemoglobinopathies and Hydrops Fetalis

Front: Quantitative percentage of Hemoglobin A ($HbA$) expected on a hemoglobin electrophoresis of a patient with Beta-Thalassemia Major?

Back: $0\%$ (completely absent in $\beta^0/\beta^0$ genotypes).

Front: Predominant hemoglobin type visualized on electrophoresis in a child with Beta-Thalassemia Major?

Back: Hemoglobin F ($\alpha_2\gamma_2$).

Front: Quantitative hemoglobin profile diagnostic for Alpha-Thalassemia Minor (trait) in an adult?

Back: Normal hemoglobin electrophoresis profile (diagnosed via genetic testing, as no abnormal bands are formed in adults).

Front: Specific name given to the dangerous condition where all four alpha-globin genes are deleted?

Back: Hydrops fetalis (or Alpha-Thalassemia Major).

Front: Cause of tissue hypoxia in a fetus with Hemoglobin Barts ($\gamma_4$)?

Back: Hemoglobin Barts has an extremely high oxygen affinity, refusing to release oxygen to peripheral fetal tissues.

Oncological Emergencies and Intracellular Lysis

Front: Electrocardiogram (ECG) changes expected during acute Tumor Lysis Syndrome due to severe hyperkalemia?

Back: Peaked T waves, PR interval prolongation, and widening of the QRS complex.

Front: Intravenous medication given in Tumor Lysis Syndrome to stabilize the cardiac myocyte membrane against hyperkalemia?

Back: Calcium gluconate (or calcium chloride).

Front: Mechanism of action of Rasburicase in managing hyperuricemia?

Back: Catalyzes the oxidation of uric acid into allantoin, which is highly soluble and easily excreted by the kidneys.

Front: Specific absolute neutrophil count (ANC) calculation formula?

Back: $\text{ANC} = \text{WBC} \times (\% \text{Neutrophils} + \% \text{Bands}) / 100$.

Front: Most common bacterial pathogens isolated from the blood of patients presenting with febrile neutropenia?

Back: Gram-positive cocci (e.g., Staphylococcus epidermidis, Staphylococcus aureus) and Gram-negative bacilli (e.g., Pseudomonas aeruginosa).

Direct Oral Anticoagulants (DOACs) and Specific Reversal Agents

Front: Specific mechanism of action of Dabigatran?

Back: Direct, competitive thrombin (Factor IIa) inhibitor.

Front: Specific mechanism of action of Rivaroxaban, Apixaban, and Edoxaban?

Back: Direct, selective inhibitors of free and clot-bound Factor Xa.

Front: Monoclonal antibody fragment used for the immediate reversal of Dabigatran?

Back: Idarucizumab.

Front: Decoy receptor protein used to reverse direct Factor Xa inhibitors (Rivaroxaban and Apixaban)?

Back: Andexanet alfa.

Front: Standard coagulation screening test most prolonged by therapeutic doses of Dabigatran?

Back: Thrombin Time (TT).

Front: Primary route of elimination for Dabigatran that requires strict dosage adjustments in renal failure?

Back: Renal clearance (approximately 80%).

Secondary and Microangiopathic Thrombocytopenias

Front: Classic drug-induced thrombocytopenia mechanism associated with Quinine, Sulfa drugs, or Gold salts?

Back: Drug-dependent antibodies bind to platelet glycoproteins only in the presence of the drug, causing accelerated clearance.

Front: Pathophysiological mechanism behind Heparin-Induced Thrombocytopenia (HIT) Type I?

Back: Direct non-immunologic pro-aggregatory effect of heparin on platelets, occurring within 1–2 days and presenting with mild, transient thrombocytopenia.

Front: Minimum percentage drop in platelet count from baseline that defines a positive "4Ts" criteria component for HIT Type II?

Back: Greater than 50% drop in platelet count.

Front: Timing window post-heparin exposure during which HIT Type II characteristically manifests?

Back: 5 to 10 days (or within 24 hours if pre-exposed to heparin in the past 100 days).

Front: Standard non-heparin anticoagulant of choice for a patient with confirmed HIT who has concurrent severe hepatic impairment?

Back: Bivalirudin or Fondaparinux (Argatroban is avoided or significantly dose-reduced due to hepatic metabolism).

Front: Pathophysiological driver of diarrhea-associated (typical) Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS)?

Back: Shiga toxin localizes to the renal endothelial cells, causing endothelial damage, microthrombi formation, and mechanical hemolysis.

Front: Alternative (atypical) Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (aHUS) is primarily driven by genetic mutations in what physiological pathway?

Back: Regulatory proteins of the alternative complement pathway (e.g., Factor H, Factor I, or CD46).

Front: Humanized monoclonal antibody targeting complement protein C5 used as first-line therapy for atypical HUS?

Back: Eculizumab.

Platelet Storage Pool and Adhesion Defects

Front: Specific substance stored inside platelet alpha-granules that is deficient in Gray Platelet Syndrome?

Back: Platelet-specific proteins (e.g., vWF, Fibrinogen, Platelet Factor 4, PDGF).

Front: Structural morphology of platelets on a peripheral blood smear in Gray Platelet Syndrome?

Back: Large, pale/gray platelets lacking normal purple azurophilic granulation.

Front: Specific substances stored inside platelet dense ($\delta$) granules that are deficient in Hermansky-Pudlak syndrome?

Back: ADP, ATP, Serotonin, and Calcium.

Front: Oculocutaneous albinism combined with a bleeding diathesis due to dense granule deficiency defines which syndrome?

Back: Hermansky-Pudlak syndrome.

Front: Quantitative functional test used to assess the initial stage of platelet adhesion in clinical practice?

Back: Platelet Function Analysis (PFA-100) closure time.

High-Yield Lymphoma Mimics and Subtypes

Front: Benign, self-limiting lymphadenopathy characterized by follicular hyperplasia and sheets of pale histiocytes with tingible body macrophages?

Back: Reactive follicular hyperplasia.

Front: Lymphoma variant that characteristically presents as an isolated, enlarging mass in the testis of an elderly man?

Back: Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma (DLBCL).

Front: Distinctive immunohistochemical marker used to identify Primary Central Nervous System Lymphoma cells on biopsy?

Back: CD20 positive (as they are almost universally diffuse large B-cell lymphomas).

Front: Rare B-cell variant that arises within the walls of a chronic fluid-filled cavity, universally driven by HHV-8?

Back: Primary Effusion Lymphoma.

Front: Slowly progressive T-cell lymphoma presenting with erythroderma, generalized lymphadenopathy, and circulating atypical T-cells with cerebriform nuclei?

Back: Sézary Syndrome.

Advanced Hemoglobinopathies and Hydrops Fetalis

Front: Quantitative percentage of Hemoglobin A ($HbA$) expected on a hemoglobin electrophoresis of a patient with Beta-Thalassemia Major?

Back: 0% (completely absent in $\beta^0/\beta^0$ genotypes).

Front: Predominant hemoglobin type visualized on electrophoresis in a child with Beta-Thalassemia Major?

Back: Hemoglobin F ($\alpha_2\gamma_2$).

Front: Quantitative hemoglobin profile diagnostic for Alpha-Thalassemia Minor (trait) in an adult?

Back: Normal hemoglobin electrophoresis profile (diagnosed via genetic testing, as no abnormal bands are formed in adults).

Front: Specific name given to the dangerous condition where all four alpha-globin genes are deleted?

Back: Hydrops fetalis (or Alpha-Thalassemia Major).

Front: Cause of tissue hypoxia in a fetus with Hemoglobin Barts ($\gamma_4$)?

Back: Hemoglobin Barts has an extremely high oxygen affinity, refusing to release oxygen to peripheral fetal tissues.

Oncological Emergencies and Intracellular Lysis

Front: Electrocardiogram (ECG) changes expected during acute Tumor Lysis Syndrome due to severe hyperkalemia?

Back: Peaked T waves, PR interval prolongation, and widening of the QRS complex.

Front: Intravenous medication given in Tumor Lysis Syndrome to stabilize the cardiac myocyte membrane against hyperkalemia?

Back: Calcium gluconate (or calcium chloride).

Front: Mechanism of action of Rasburicase in managing hyperuricemia?

Back: Catalyzes the oxidation of uric acid into allantoin, which is highly soluble and easily excreted by the kidneys.

Front: Specific absolute neutrophil count (ANC) calculation formula?

Back: $\text{ANC} = \text{WBC} \times (\% \text{Neutrophils} + \% \text{Bands}) / 100$.

Front: Most common bacterial pathogens isolated from the blood of patients presenting with febrile neutropenia?

Back: Gram-positive cocci (e.g., Staphylococcus epidermidis, Staphylococcus aureus) and Gram-negative bacilli (e.g., Pseudomonas aeruginosa).

Anticoagulant Monitoring Pitfalls & Heparin Resistance

Front: Most common physiological cause of "Heparin Resistance," where exceptionally high doses of heparin fail to prolong the aPTT?

Back: Antithrombin (ATIII) deficiency (inherited or acquired via consumption).

Front: Alternative laboratory assay used to accurately monitor Unfractionated Heparin therapy when a patient has a baseline prolonged aPTT?

Back: Chromogenic anti-Factor Xa assay.

Front: Effect of therapeutic doses of direct oral Factor Xa inhibitors on standard Prothrombin Time (PT) assays?

Back: They prolong the PT in a variable, reagent-dependent manner that is unreliable for quantification.

Front: Coagulation test that remains completely unaffected by the presence of therapeutic direct oral Factor Xa inhibitors?

Back: Thrombin Time (TT) (which only evaluates thrombin-to-fibrin conversion).

Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria (PNH) & Complement Therapeutics

Front: Principal cause of chronic iron deficiency in patients with untreated Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria?

Back: Continuous intravascular hemolysis leading to marked, persistent hemosiderinuria and urinary iron loss.

Front: Serious infectious complication for which patients must be vaccinated prior to initiating Eculizumab or Ravulizumab therapy?

Back: Neisseria meningitidis infection (due to therapeutic blockade of the terminal complement pathway/MAC assembly).

Front: Primary mechanism of breakthrough extravascular hemolysis that can occur in PNH patients treated with C5 inhibitors?

Back: C3b fragments continue to accumulate on the RBC surface upstream of C5, leading to splenic macrophage clearing.

Front: Target of Pegcetacoplan, a newer complement inhibitor used to prevent both intravascular and extravascular hemolysis in PNH?

Back: Complement component C3 (and C3b).

Myelodysplastic/Myeloproliferative Overlap & Histiocytic Subtleties

Front: Minimum percentage of peripheral blood monocytes required over a 3-month period to satisfy WHO diagnostic criteria for CMML?

Back: Persistent monocytosis $\ge 10\%$ of the total white blood cell count (and $\ge 1,000/\mu\text{L}$).

Front: Distinguishing bone marrow feature that separates Chronic Myelomonocytic Leukemia (CMML) from Chronic Myeloid Leukemia (CML)?

Back: CMML lacks the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene and shows dominant monocytic proliferation rather than granulocytic.
