Front: Key diagnostic criteria that separates Juvenile Myelomonocytic Leukemia (JMML) from adult CMML forms?

Back: JMML is a childhood disorder strongly linked to mutations in the RAS pathway (e.g., PTPN11, NF1, KRAS) and exhibits elevated Hemoglobin F.

Front: Classic radiological description of the bone lesions seen in Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis (LCH) of the skull?

Back: "Punched-out" lytic lesions without a reactive sclerotic rim.

Front: Therapeutic backbone for a patient presenting with severe, life-threatening Hemophagocytic Lymphohistiocytosis (HLH)?

Back: The HLH-94 protocol consisting of Etoposide, Dexamethasone, and Cyclosporine A.

Quantitative Platelet Aggregometry Profiles

Front: Platelet aggregometry response to ADP, Epinephrine, and Collagen in a patient with Glanzmann Thrombasthenia?

Back: Severely impaired or completely absent aggregation.

Front: Platelet aggregometry response to Ristocetin in a patient with Glanzmann Thrombasthenia?

Back: Normal agglutination (because the GP Ib-IX-V complex is entirely intact).

Front: Platelet aggregometry response to ADP, Epinephrine, and Collagen in a patient with Bernard-Soulier Syndrome?

Back: Normal aggregation.

Front: Platelet aggregometry response to Ristocetin in a patient with Bernard-Soulier Syndrome?

Back: Absent agglutination, which does not correct when mixed with normal plasma.

Rare T-Cell & Aggressive B-Cell Lymphoma Variants

Front: Highly aggressive T-cell lymphoma arising in the background of long-standing, poorly controlled Celiac Disease?

Back: Enteropathy-Associated T-Cell Lymphoma (EATL).

Front: Primary anatomical location where Enteropathy-Associated T-Cell Lymphoma (EATL) characteristically causes ulceration and perforation?

Back: The proximal small intestine (jejunum and ileum).

Front: Rare B-cell malignancy that characteristically arises within the walls of a chronic fluid-filled cavity, universally driven by HHV-8?

Back: Primary Effusion Lymphoma (PEL).

Front: Aggressive Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma presenting as a rapidly expanding, destructive midline mass in the nasal cavity, strongly linked to EBV?

Back: Extranodal NK/T-cell Lymphoma, nasal type.

Front: Cytogenetic configuration that defines a "Double-Hit" High-Grade B-cell Lymphoma?

Back: Concurrent rearrangements of the MYC gene along with either the BCL2 or BCL6 gene.

Targeted Oncological Mechanisms & Toxicities

Front: Most common long-term, life-threatening organ toxicity associated with the use of Anthracyclines (e.g., Doxorubicin)?

Back: Dilated cardiomyopathy (congestive heart failure) due to cumulative, iron-dependent free radical generation.

Front: Cardioprotective chelating agent administered alongside Doxorubicin to reduce the risk of anthracycline-induced cardiomyopathy?

Back: Dexrazoxane.

Front: Classic chemotherapeutic agent historically associated with the development of secondary Acute Myeloid Leukemia (sAML) with a short latency period?

Back: Etoposide (a Topoisomerase II inhibitor, often associated with $\text{t}(11;23)$ or MLL gene rearrangements).

Front: Class-specific adverse effect of Tyrosine Kinase Inhibitors like Imatinib related to fluid balance?

Back: Fluid retention presenting as periorbital edema, pleural effusions, or peripheral edema.

Front: Distinctive pulmonary toxic side effect characteristically associated with Bleomycin therapy?

Back: Pulmonary fibrosis (interstitial lung disease), which requires serial pulmonary function testing (DLCO monitoring).

Targeted Therapeutics & Precision Mechanisms

Front: Specific molecular target of Rituximab used in B-cell lymphomas and autoimmune cytopenias?

Back: CD20 surface antigen expressed on mature B cells.

Front: Humanized monoclonal antibody targeting CD38 used as a core therapy for relapsed or refractory Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Daratumumab.

Front: Reversible proteasome inhibitor that disrupts protein homeostasis to induce apoptosis in Multiple Myeloma plasma cells?

Back: Bortezomib.

Front: Mechanism of action of Brentuximab Vedotin used in Hodgkin Lymphoma?

Back: Antibody-drug conjugate targeting CD30 that delivers a microtubule-disrupting agent (MMAE) directly to Reed-Sternberg cells.

Front: Chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell therapies primarily target which antigen in refractory B-cell Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia?

Back: CD19.

Front: Unique mechanism of action of Romiplostim used in chronic Immune Thrombocytopenic Purpura (ITP)?

Back: Thrombopoietin (TPO) receptor agonist that stimulates platelet production in the bone marrow.

Microenvironment, Iron Transport, and Secondary Anemias

Front: Major iron-transport protein responsible for moving iron from enterocytes and macrophages into circulation?

Back: Ferroportin.

Front: Plasma transport protein that binds toxic free hemoglobin in the blood to prevent oxidative tissue damage?

Back: Haptoglobin.

Front: Intracellular storage protein that serves as the primary accessible reserve for iron inside hepatocytes and macrophages?

Back: Ferritin.

Front: Specific physiological site of erythropoietin (EPO) production in the adult human body?

Back: Peritubular interstitial cells of the renal cortex.

Front: Primary mechanism behind the development of Anemia of Prematurity?

Back: Impaired erythropoietin production in response to hypoxia combined with a shortened red blood cell lifespan.

Front: Distinctive bone marrow feature that distinguishes Congenital Dyserythropoietic Anemia (CDA) from other causes of ineffective erythropoiesis?

Back: Marked erythroblast multinuclearity and nuclear bridging.

Hypercoagulability Nuances & Endothelial Interactions

Front: Autosomal dominant condition causing a mutation in the thrombomodulin gene results in what clinical phenotype?

Back: Increased risk of early-onset venous thromboembolism due to impaired activation of Protein C.

Front: Impact of severe Hepatic Cirrhosis on a patient's natural anticoagulant systems?

Back: Marked reduction in the synthesis of Protein C, Protein S, and Antithrombin, which can paradoxically balance out the loss of procoagulants.

Front: Most accurate timing to check for a suspected hereditary Protein S deficiency after an acute deep vein thrombosis?

Back: At least 2 weeks after completing acute anticoagulation therapy, as acute thrombosis consumes Protein S.

Front: Mechanism by which active malignancies promote a systemic hypercoagulable state (Trousseau syndrome)?

Back: Tumor cells secrete tissue factor-bearing microvesicles and mucins that directly activate Factor X.

Front: Cardinal clinical presentation that defines May-Thurner Syndrome?

Back: Left-sided deep vein thrombosis caused by compression of the left common iliac vein by the overlying right common iliac artery.

Histiocytic Profiles & Bone Marrow Borderlands

Front: Clonal driver mutation present in approximately 50% of cases of Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis (LCH)?

Back: BRAF V600E mutation.

Front: Classic immunohistochemical marker that identifies Langerhans cells by staining for the component of Birbeck granules?

Back: Langerin (CD207).

Front: Standard first-line systemic therapy for multi-system Langerhans Cell Histiocytosis?

Back: Vinblastine combined with Prednisone.

Front: Life-threatening diagnostic criteria for Hemophagocytic Lymphohistiocytosis (HLH) includes what level of fasting serum triglycerides?

Back: Fasting triglycerides $\ge 265\text{ mg/dL}$ (or $\ge 3\text{ mmol/L}$).

Front: Diagnostic bone marrow hallmark observed on a core biopsy of a patient with advanced Primary Myelofibrosis?

Back: Dense reticulin or collagen fibrosis with a complete absence of normal fat spaces.

Rare Coagulopathies & Mixing Study Logistics

Front: Coagulation profile (PT, aPTT, bleeding time) characteristically observed in an isolated Factor VII deficiency?

Back: Prolonged PT, normal aPTT, and normal bleeding time.

Front: Coagulation factor deficiency that characteristically causes a severely prolonged aPTT in vitro but presents with absolutely no clinical bleeding tendency?

Back: Factor XII (Hageman factor) deficiency.

Front: Diagnostic implication if a patient's prolonged aPTT fails to correct back to normal limits immediately upon a 1:1 mixing study?

Back: Indicates the presence of a coagulation inhibitor (e.g., Lupus Anticoagulant or a specific factor antibody).

Front: Diagnostic implication if a patient's prolonged aPTT corrects immediately upon a 1:1 mixing study, but prolongs again after a 2-hour incubation?

Back: Indicates the presence of a time-dependent, temperature-dependent factor inhibitor (classic for Factor VIII inhibitors).

Front: Rare bleeding disorder characterized by a normal PT, normal aPTT, and normal platelet count, but presents with delayed severe hemorrhage after trauma?

Back: Factor XIII deficiency.

Front: Definitive qualitative screening test used to detect a suspected clinical Factor XIII deficiency?

Back: Urea clot solubility test (Factor XIII-deficient clots dissolve rapidly in a 5-molar urea solution).

Transfusion Immunology & Metabolic Complications

Front: Mechanism behind a Delayed Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction?

Back: Anamnestic antibody response against minor red blood cell antigens (such as Kidd or Kell) previously encountered via transfusion or pregnancy.

Front: Metabolic complication resulting from the rapid transfusion of multiple units of packed red blood cells stored for an extended period?

Back: Hyperkalemia (caused by the gradual leakage of potassium out of aging erythrocytes into the supernatant fluid of the storage bag).

Front: Primary mechanism behind Post-Transfusion Purpura (PTP)?

Back: Production of alloantibodies against human platelet antigen-1a (HPA-1a) that paradoxically destroy both the transfused and autologous platelets.

Front: Processing modification required for blood products given to a patient with a history of severe, IgA-mediated anaphylactic transfusion reactions?

Back: Extensive washing of cellular blood products with saline to remove all traces of donor plasma proteins.

Front: Direct physiological trigger for Transfusion-Associated Circulatory Overload (TACO)?

Back: Rapid infusion of a large volume of blood product exceeding the cardiovascular system's regulatory capacity, increasing hydrostatic pressure.

Myeloma Mimics, Heavy Chain Diseases, and Organ Toxicity

Front: Distinctive clinical triad that differentiates Waldenström Macroglobulinemia from Multiple Myeloma?

Back: Presence of a monoclonal IgM spike, lymphoplasmacytic bone marrow infiltration, and absence of lytic bone lesions.

Front: Primary pathophysiological mechanism driving the hyperviscosity syndrome in Waldenström Macroglobulinemia?

Back: Large IgM pentamers circulate in high concentrations, significantly increasing plasma resistance and shearing forces.

Front: Rare plasma cell dyscrasia characterized by the isolated production of truncated immunoglobulin heavy chains without light chains?

Back: Heavy Chain Disease (such as Franklin disease or Seligmann disease).

Front: Distinctive organ biopsy finding when examining systemic amyloid deposits under polarized light microscopy?

Back: Apple-green birefringence under Congo red staining.

Front: Rare systemic disorder presenting with Polyneuropathy, Organomegaly, Endocrinology abnormalities, Monoclonal plasma cell proliferative disorder, and Skin changes?

Back: POEMS syndrome.

Front: Class-specific, long-term, life-threatening organ toxicity associated with the cumulative use of Anthracyclines like Doxorubicin?

Back: Dilated cardiomyopathy due to iron-dependent free radical generation.

Front: Cardioprotective chelating agent administered alongside Doxorubicin to mitigate the risk of anthracycline-induced cardiomyopathy?

Back: Dexrazoxane.

Front: Distinctive pulmonary toxic side effect characteristically associated with Bleomycin therapy requiring serial DLCO monitoring?

Back: Pulmonary fibrosis.

Bone Marrow Microenvironment and Non-Malignant Pancytopenias

Front: Key stromal-derived cytokine essential for the survival, homing, and differentiation of early hematopoietic stem cells?

Back: Stem Cell Factor (SCF).

Front: Specialized microenvironmental structures in the bone marrow where erythroblast maturation and iron transfer occur around a central macrophage?

Back: Erythroblastic islands.

Front: Autosomal dominant pure red cell aplasia linked to ribosomal protein gene mutations ($RPS19$) presenting in infancy?

Back: Diamond-Blackfan Anemia.

Front: Distinctive physical exam sign on the hands that differentiates Diamond-Blackfan Anemia from Fanconi Anemia?

Back: Triphalangeal thumbs in Diamond-Blackfan; absent or hypoplastic thumbs and radii in Fanconi.

Front: Acquired pure red cell aplasia (PRCA) in adults is classically associated with what encapsulated anterior mediastinal tumor?

Back: Thymoma.

Front: Rare, inherited normocytic anemia characterized by multinucleated erythroblasts, nuclear bridging, and a positive acidified serum lysis (Ham) test?

Back: Congenital Dyserythropoietic Anemia (CDA).

Hemoglobinopathies: Structural Variants and Technical Testing

Front: Structural hemoglobin variant that defines Hemoglobin C disease?

Back: Lysine substitution for glutamic acid at position 6 of the beta-globin chain.

Front: Classic morphology of erythrocytes on a peripheral blood smear from a patient with homozygous Hemoglobin CC disease?

Back: Hexagonal, rod-shaped intracellular HbC crystals and target cells.

Front: Structural variant where glutamic acid is replaced by lysine at position 26 of the beta-globin chain, highly prevalent in Southeast Asia?

Back: Hemoglobin E ($HbE$).

Front: Underlying genetic mechanism responsible for the vast majority of Alpha-Thalassemia cases?

Back: Large structural gene deletions via unequal chromosomal crossing over.

Front: Underlying genetic mechanism responsible for the vast majority of Beta-Thalassemia cases?

Back: Point mutations affecting globin mRNA splicing, promoter regions, or translation termination.

Front: Normal minor adult hemoglobin variant formed by two alpha chains and two delta chains ($\alpha_2\delta_2$)?

Back: Hemoglobin $A_2$ ($HbA_2$).

Front: Definitive quantitative assay used to precisely measure and monitor Hemoglobin S levels in sickle cell patients undergoing exchange transfusion?

Back: High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC).

Immune and Non-Immune Hemolytic Footprints

Front: Humanized monoclonal antibody targeting complement component C5 used to treat Paroxysmal Nocturnal Hemoglobinuria and atypical HUS?

Back: Eculizumab.

Front: Physical footprint of intravascular hemolysis measured via a marked decrease in which specific serum transport protein?

Back: Haptoglobin.

Front: Oxidation product of hemoglobin breakdown that accumulates in the renal tubules and urine during sustained, severe intravascular hemolysis?

Back: Hemosiderin (Hemosiderinuria).

Front: Protozoal intraerythrocytic infection that mimics G6PD deficiency by inducing hemolytic anemia and forming "Maltese cross" tetrads on a thick smear?

Back: Babesiosis.

Front: Metabolic pathway in which Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) operates to protect RBCs from oxidative stress?

Back: Hexose monophosphate (HMP) shunt.

Front: Diagnostic pitfall when testing G6PD enzyme activity levels immediately after an acute hemolytic crisis?

Back: False-negative result due to the destruction of older, enzyme-deficient RBCs and a high concentration of young, enzyme-replete reticulocytes.

Secondary Myeloproliferative States and Reactive Changes

Front: Principal bone marrow cytokine responsible for driving the extensive reactive collagen fibrosis in Primary Myelofibrosis?

Back: Transforming Growth Factor-beta (TGF-$\beta$) released from clonal megakaryocytes.

Front: Name given to the dry tap bone marrow aspiration attempt classic in both Primary Myelofibrosis and Hairy Cell Leukemia?

Back: Punctio sicca.

Front: Most common physiological cause of a secondary polycythemia presenting with elevated erythropoietin (EPO) levels?

Back: Chronic hypoxemia due to severe lung disease, sleep apnea, high altitude, or right-to-left shunts.

Front: Paraneoplastic condition where a renal cell carcinoma or cerebellar hemangioblastoma causes erythrocytosis?

Back: Inappropriate secondary polycythemia via autonomous EPO production.

Front: Pathophysiological driver of erythromelalgia presenting as burning pain and erythema in the extremities of patients with Essential Thrombocythemia?

Back: Platelet microvascular occlusion and subsequent localized endothelial inflammation.

Front: Expected Leukocyte Alkaline Phosphatase (LAP) score trend in Chronic Myeloid Leukemia versus a benign Leukemoid Reaction?

Back: Decreased or absent in CML; significantly elevated in a Leukemoid Reaction.


=====================================================================
=====================================================================

Section: Internal Medicine
Topic: Infectious Disease

General Bacteriology & Pharmacotherapy

Front: First-line empiric treatment for native valve infective endocarditis?

Back: Vancomycin plus Ceftriaxone.

Front: Most common causative organism of acute infective endocarditis in IV drug users?

Back: Staphylococcus aureus.

Front: Classic valvular pathology seen in intravenous drug users with endocarditis?

Back: Tricuspid regurgitation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Vancomycin?

Back: Inhibits bacterial cell wall synthesis by binding to the D-Ala-D-Ala terminus of peptidoglycan precursors.

Front: Mechanism of action of Daptomycin?

Back: Disrupts cell membrane function by creating transmembrane channels causing intracellular ion leakage.

Front: Major adverse effect of Daptomycin that requires weekly monitoring?

Back: Myopathy and elevated Creatine Kinase (CK) levels.

Front: Why is Daptomycin ineffective in treating bacterial pneumonia?

Back: It is inactivated by pulmonary surfactant.

Front: Empiric treatment for a patient with suspected bacterial meningitis aged 2 to 50 years?

Back: Vancomycin plus a third-generation cephalosporin (Ceftriaxone or Cefotaxime).

Front: Crucial antibiotic addition to empiric meningitis treatment for patients over 50 or immunocompromised?

Back: Ampicillin.

Front: Why is Ampicillin added to empiric meningitis regimens in older or vulnerable adults?

Back: To cover Listeria monocytogenes.

Front: Which adjunctive medication should be given prior to or with the first dose of antibiotics in pneumococcal meningitis?

Back: Dexamethasone.

Front: Most common cause of community-acquired pneumonia (CAP) across all age groups?

Back: Streptococcus pneumoniae.

Front: Empirical outpatient treatment for a healthy adult with Community-Acquired Pneumonia and no comorbidities?

Back: Amoxicillin, Doxycycline, or a Macrolide (if local resistance is <25%).

Front: Empirical inpatient (non-ICU) treatment for Community-Acquired Pneumonia?

Back: A respiratory fluoroquinolone (e.g., Levofloxacin) OR a beta-lactam plus a macrolide.

Front: Drug of choice for treating severe Clostridioides difficile infection?

Back: Oral Vancomycin or Fidaxomicin.

HIV, Opportunistic Infections, and Prophylaxis

Front: CD4 count threshold below which prophylaxis for Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) is indicated?

Back: <200 cells/mm³.

Front: First-line agent used for Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) prophylaxis?

Back: Trimethoprim-Sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX).

Front: CD4 count threshold below which prophylaxis for Toxoplasma gondii is indicated in seropositive patients?

Back: <100 cells/mm³.

Front: Medication used for Toxoplasma gondii prophylaxis?

Back: Trimethoprim-Sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX).

Front: Diagnostic finding on a brain MRI for an HIV patient with Toxoplasma gondii encephalitis?

Back: Multiple ring-enhancing lesions with a preference for the basal ganglia.

Front: Preferred treatment for active Toxoplasma encephalitis?

Back: Pyrimethamine plus Sulfadiazine (plus Leucovorin).

Front: Opportunistic infection causing painful, linear esophageal ulcers in an HIV patient?

Back: Cytomegalovirus (CMV).

Front: Opportunistic infection causing round, punched-out esophageal ulcers in an HIV patient?

Back: Herpes Simplex Virus 1 (HSV-1).

Front: Diagnostic finding on a fundoscopic exam for Cytomegalovirus (CMV) retinitis?

Back: Pizzalike retinopathy showing perivascular hemorrhages and fluffy white exudates.

Front: First-line treatment for Cytomegalovirus (CMV) retinitis?

Back: Ganciclovir or Valganciclovir.

Front: CD4 count threshold below which primary prophylaxis for Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC) was historically indicated (no longer routinely required if ART is started immediately)?

Back: <50 cells/mm³.

Front: Preferred treatment for an active Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC) infection?

Back: Azithromycin or Clarithromycin combined with Ethambutol.

Front: Most common central nervous system fungal infection in HIV patients?

Back: Cryptococcal meningitis.

Front: Initial diagnostic test visualizing encapsulated yeasts in cerebrospinal fluid via negative staining?

Back: India ink preparation.

Front: Most sensitive and specific rapid diagnostic test for Cryptococcal meningitis?

Back: Cryptococcal antigen (CrAg) lateral flow assay.

Front: Induction therapy regimen for Cryptococcal meningitis?

Back: Liposomal Amphotericin B plus Flucytosine.

Front: Maintenance therapy agent for Cryptococcal meningitis after induction?

Back: Fluconazole.

Front: Form of skin lesion associated with Human Herpesvirus 8 (HHV-8) in HIV patients?

Back: Kaposi sarcoma.

Tick-Borne and Zoonotic Diseases

Front: Causative organism of Lyme disease?

Back: Borrelia burgdorferi.

Front: Classic initial dermatological presentation of Lyme disease?

Back: Erythema migrans (bull's-eye rash).

Front: Vector responsible for transmitting Lyme disease and Anaplasmosis?

Back: Ixodes tick.

Front: First-line oral antibiotic for early localized Lyme disease in adults?

Back: Doxycycline.

Front: Preferred antibiotic for Lyme disease in pregnant patients or children under 8?

Back: Amoxicillin.

Front: Manifestation of late disseminated Lyme disease involving the joints?

Back: Asymmetric monoarticular or oligoarticular arthritis, usually affecting the knee.

Front: Causative organism of Rocky Mountain Spotted Fever (RMSF)?

Back: Rickettsia rickettsii.

Front: Classic progression of the rash in Rocky Mountain Spotted Fever?

Back: Starts on the wrists and ankles and spreads centripetally to the trunk, palms, and soles.

Front: Drug of choice for Rocky Mountain Spotted Fever in ALL patients, including children and pregnant women?

Back: Doxycycline.

Front: Tick-borne disease characterized by hemolytic anemia, jaundice, and a Maltese cross appearance on blood smear?

Back: Babesiosis.

Front: Vector for Babesia microti?

Back: Ixodes tick.

Front: First-line antimicrobial combination for mild-to-moderate Babesiosis?

Back: Atovaquone plus Azithromycin.

Front: Tick-borne illness characterized by leukopenia, thrombocytopenia, elevated liver enzymes, and morulae inside monocytes?

Back: Human Monocytic Ehrlichiosis (HME).

Tuberculosis & Mycobacterial Diseases

Front: Standard 4-drug regimen for initial treatment of active Tuberculosis (TB)?

Back: Rifampin, Isoniazid, Pyrazinamide, and Ethambutol (RIPE).

Front: Mechanism of action of Isoniazid?

Back: Inhibits the synthesis of mycolic acids, an essential component of the mycobacterial cell wall.

Front: Common adverse effect of Isoniazid preventable by co-administering Vitamin B6 (Pyridoxine)?

Back: Peripheral neuropathy.

Front: Mechanism of action of Rifampin?

Back: Inhibits bacterial DNA-dependent RNA polymerase.

Front: Benign, classic side effect of Rifampin that patients must be counseled on?

Back: Red-orange discoloration of bodily fluids (urine, tears, sweat).

Front: Unique toxic side effect of Ethambutol requiring baseline and periodic visual testing?

Back: Optic neuritis (reversible red-green color blindness and decreased visual acuity).

Front: Major metabolic side effect associated with Pyrazinamide?

Back: Hyperuricemia (which can precipitate acute gouty arthritis).

Front: Screening test cut-off for a positive Tuberculin Skin Test (TST) in a healthcare worker with no other risk factors?

Back: $\ge 10\text{ mm}$ of induration.

Front: Screening test cut-off for a positive Tuberculin Skin Test (TST) in an HIV-infected or severely immunosuppressed patient?

Back: $\ge 5\text{ mm}$ of induration.

Front: Screening test cut-off for a positive Tuberculin Skin Test (TST) in a person with no known risk factors for TB?

Back: $\ge 15\text{ mm}$ of induration.

Front: Preferred preferred regimen for treating Latent Tuberculosis Infection (LTBI) to optimize compliance?

Back: 3 months of weekly Isoniazid plus Rifapentine OR 4 months of daily Rifampin.

Sexually Transmitted Infections (STIs)

Front: Causative organism of Syphilis?

Back: Treponema pallidum.

Front: Classic presentation of primary Syphilis?

Back: A single, painless ulcer (chancre) with clean bases and indurated margins.

Front: Clinical features characteristic of secondary Syphilis?

Back: Maculopapular rash on palms and soles, lymphadenopathy, and condylomata lata.

Front: Screening (nontreponemal) serologic tests for Syphilis?

Back: VDRL (Venercal Disease Research Laboratory) and RPR (Rapid Plasma Reagin).

Front: Confirmatory (treponemal) serologic tests for Syphilis?

Back: FTA-ABS (Fluorescent Treponemal Antibody Absorption) and TP-PA (Treponema pallidum particle agglutination).

Front: Treatment of choice for primary, secondary, or early latent Syphilis?

Back: A single intramuscular dose of Benzathine Penicillin G ($2.4\text{ million units}$).

Front: Acute febrile reaction accompanied by headache and myalgias that can occur within 24 hours of starting penicillin for syphilis?

Back: Jarisch-Herxheimer reaction.

Front: Preferred treatment for Neurosyphilis?

Back: Intravenous Aqueous Crystalline Penicillin G for 10 to 14 days.

Front: Painful, friable genital ulcer accompanied by painful, suppurative inguinal lymphadenopathy (buboes)?

Back: Chancroid (caused by Haemophilus ducreyi).

Front: First-line empirical treatment for uncomplicated urogenital Chlamydia trachomatis infection?

Back: Doxycycline ($100\text{ mg}$ orally twice daily for 7 days).

Front: First-line empirical treatment for uncomplicated Gonococcal urethritis?

Back: A single intramuscular dose of Ceftriaxone ($500\text{ mg}$).

Front: Diagnostic finding on a wet mount for Trichomonas vaginalis?

Back: Motile, flagellated trichomonads.

Front: First-line treatment for both the patient and sexual partner in a Trichomonas vaginalis infection?

Back: Oral Metronidazole.

Systemic Mycoses and Fungal Infections

Front: Fungal infection endemic to the Ohio and Mississippi River valleys, associated with bird or bat droppings?

Back: Histoplasmosis (Histoplasma capsulatum).

Front: Diagnostic tissue finding for Histoplasma capsulatum?

Back: Small intracellular yeasts within macrophages.

Front: Fungal infection endemic to the Southwestern United States, presenting with valley fever (fever, arthralgias, erythema nodosum)?

Back: Coccidioidomycosis (Coccidioides immitis).

Front: Diagnostic tissue finding for Coccidioides immitis?

Back: Large spherules filled with endospores.

Front: Fungal infection endemic to the Great Lakes and Ohio River valley, causing inflammatory cutaneous and osteolytic lesions?

Back: Blastomycosis (Blastomyces dermatitidis).

Front: Diagnostic tissue finding for Blastomyces dermatitidis?

Back: Large, round yeasts with broad-based budding.

Front: First-line treatment for mild-to-moderate localized systemic fungal infections (e.g., Histoplasmosis, Blastomycosis)?

Back: Itraconazole.

Front: First-line treatment for severe, disseminated systemic fungal infections?

Back: Amphotericin B.

Front: Invasive fungal infection causing rapidly progressive paranasal sinus necrosis in a patient with diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA)?

Back: Mucormycosis (caused by Mucor or Rhizopus species).

Front: Microscopic appearance of Mucor or Rhizopus hyphae in tissue biopsy?

Back: Broad, nonseptate hyphae branching at right ($90^\circ$) angles.

Front: Microscopic appearance of Aspergillus fumigatus hyphae?

Back: Septate hyphae branching at acute ($45^\circ$) angles.

Gastrointestinal and Intra-abdominal Infections

Front: Most common bacterial cause of bloody diarrhea (dysentery) in the United States, associated with poultry?

Back: Campylobacter jejuni.

Front: Complication of Campylobacter jejuni infection presenting as ascending paralysis?

Back: Guillain-Barré syndrome.

Front: Gram stain morphology of Campylobacter jejuni?

Back: Comma- or S-shaped Gram-negative rod with polar flagella.

Front: Bacterial diarrheal pathogen associated with undercooked ground beef and Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS)?

Back: Enterohemorrhagic Escherichia coli (EHEC), specifically O157:H7.

Front: Why are antibiotics contraindicated in children suspected of having EHEC O157:H7 infection?

Back: They increase the release of Shiga toxin, raising the risk of Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS).

Front: Triad of signs defining Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS)?

Back: Microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury.

Front: Protozoan parasite causing foul-smelling, fatty diarrhea (steatorrhea) after drinking water from mountain streams?

Back: Giardia lamblia.

Front: Treatment of choice for Giardiasis?

Back: Tinidazole or Metronidazole.

Front: Empiric antibiotic regimen for acute diverticulitis?

Back: Ciprofloxacin plus Metronidazole OR Amoxicillin-Clavulanate monotherapy.

Front: Most common cause of spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP) in cirrhotic patients?

Back: Escherichia coli.

Front: Diagnostic threshold of polymorphonuclear leukocytes (PMNs) in ascitic fluid to confirm spontaneous bacterial peritonitis?

Back: $\ge 250\text{ cells/mm}^3$.

Front: First-line antibiotic choice for Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis?

Back: A third-generation cephalosporin (e.g., Cefotaxime or Ceftriaxone).

Urinary Tract and Soft Tissue Infections

Front: Most common causative organism of uncomplicated Urinary Tract Infections (UTIs)?

Back: Escherichia coli.

Front: First-line antibiotic options for acute uncomplicated cystitis in women?

Back: Nitrofurantoin, Trimethoprim-Sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX), or Fosfomycin.

Front: First-line empiric treatment for outpatient acute uncomplicated pyelonephritis?

Back: Oral Fluoroquinolones (e.g., Ciprofloxacin or Levofloxacin).

Front: Soft tissue infection characterized by painful, well-demarcated, bright red erythema of the superficial dermis, often on the face?

Back: Erysipelas (most commonly caused by Streptococcus pyogenes).

Front: Soft tissue infection presenting as ill-defined, flat, warm erythema of the deeper dermis and subcutaneous tissue?

Back: Cellulitis (commonly caused by Streptococcus pyogenes or Staphylococcus aureus).

Front: Surgical emergency presenting with soft tissue swelling, severe pain out of proportion to exam findings, and gas (crepitus) on X-ray?

Back: Necrotizing fasciitis.

Zoonotic and Miscellaneous Bacterial Infections

Front: Causative organism of Cat Scratch Disease?

Back: Bartonella henselae.

Front: First-line antibiotic therapy to shorten the duration of painful lymphadenopathy in Cat Scratch Disease?

Back: Azithromycin.

Front: Causative agent of Q fever, typically transmitted via inhalation of aerosols from farm animal amniotic fluid?

Back: Coxiella burnetii.

Front: Classic clinical presentation of acute Q fever?

Back: Culture-negative endocarditis, severe flu-like symptoms, and hepatitis or atypical pneumonia.

Front: Causative agent of Brucellosis, acquired via unpasteurized dairy products?

Back: Brucella species.

Front: Standard antibiotic regimen for treating Brucellosis?

Back: Doxycycline plus Rifampin.

Front: Causative organism of Tularemia, associated with handling infected rabbits or tick bites?

Back: Francisella tularensis.

Front: First-line antibiotic treatment for severe Francisella tularensis (Tularemia) infection?

Back: Streptomycin or Gentamicin.

Front: Causative organism of Pasteurellosis, following a domestic dog or cat bite?

Back: Pasteurella multocida.

Front: Prophylactic and definitive first-line oral antibiotic for mammalian bite wounds?

Back: Amoxicillin-Clavulanate.

Viral Pathogens and Vaccines

Front: Causative virus of Infectious Mononucleosis?

Back: Epstein-Barr Virus (EBV).

Front: Diagnostic test that detects heterophile antibodies in patients with suspected EBV?

Back: Monospot test.

Front: Classic adverse drug reaction seen when treating a patient who has EBV mononucleosis with Amoxicillin?

Back: A diffuse maculopapular rash.

Front: Serious splenic complication of Infectious Mononucleosis requiring avoidance of contact sports for at least 3-4 weeks?

Back: Splenic rupture.

Front: Preferred antiviral drug used to treat severe Influenza infections within 48 hours of symptom onset?

Back: Oseltamivir.

Front: Antiviral mechanism of action of Oseltamivir?

Back: Inhibits neuraminidase, preventing the release of new viral particles from host cells.

Front: First-line treatment for chronic Hepatitis C infection across major genotypes?

Back: Direct-acting antivirals (DAAs) such as Sofosbuvir/Velpatasvir.

Front: Post-exposure prophylaxis regimen for an unvaccinated individual exposed to Hepatitis B?

Back: Hepatitis B vaccine series plus Hepatitis B Immune Globulin (HBIG).

Front: Post-exposure prophylaxis protocol for a person bitten by a wild, potentially rabid animal?

Back: Rabies vaccine series (days 0, 3, 7, 14) plus Rabies Immune Globulin (RIG) infiltrated around the wound.

Sepsis and Healthcare-Associated Infections

Front: Definition of Sepsis according to Sepsis-3 guidelines?

Back: Life-threatening organ dysfunction caused by a dysregulated host response to infection.

Front: Clinical scoring system used to identify worsening organ dysfunction in the intensive care unit?

Back: Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA) score.

Front: Definition of Septic Shock according to Sepsis-3 guidelines?

Back: Sepsis requiring vasopressors to maintain a mean arterial pressure (MAP) $\ge 65\text{ mmHg}$ and serum lactate $>2\text{ mmol/L}$ despite adequate fluid resuscitation.

Front: First-line vasopressor of choice for fluid-refractory septic shock?

Back: Norepinephrine.

Front: Initial fluid resuscitation volume recommended for patients with sepsis-induced tissue hypoperfusion?

Back: $30\text{ mL/kg}$ of intravenous crystalloids within the first 3 hours.

Front: Most common source of bacteremia leading to healthcare-associated infections?

Back: Central venous catheters.

Front: Diagnostic action required when a central venous catheter-associated bloodstream infection is suspected?

Back: Draw simultaneous blood cultures from both the catheter lumen and a peripheral vein.

Parasitic and Helminthic Infections

Front: Vector responsible for transmitting Malaria (Plasmodium species)?

Back: Female Anopheles mosquito.

Front: Gold standard diagnostic test for confirming Malaria and determining parasitemia levels?

Back: Thick and thin microscopic blood smears.

Front: First-line agent for treating uncomplicated chloroquine-resistant Plasmodium falciparum malaria?

Back: Artemether-Lumefantrine OR Atovaquone-Proguanil.

Front: Medication added to eradicate hypnozoites in latent hepatic phases of Plasmodium vivax and Plasmodium ovale?

Back: Primaquine.

Front: Vital pre-screening requirement before initiating Primaquine therapy?

Back: Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) activity assay to avoid severe hemolysis.

Front: Parasitic infection causing perianal pruritus, diagnosed via the cellophane tape test?

Back: Enterobiasis (Pinworm infection, caused by Enterobius vermicularis).

Front: Drug of choice for treating Enterobius vermicularis (Pinworm) infections?

Back: Pyrantel pamoate or Albendazole.

Front: Parasitic tissue infection caused by ingesting undercooked pork containing cysts, presenting with periorbital edema, myalgias, and eosinophilia?

Back: Trichinellosis (Trichinella spiralis).

Front: Treatment of choice for tissue microfilariae in Onchocerciasis (River Blindness)?

Back: Ivermectin.

Front: Treatment of choice for Neurocysticercosis?

Back: Albendazole plus corticosteroids (e.g., Dexamethasone).


Bioterrorism & Rare Pathogens

Front: Initial diagnostic test of choice for a patient presenting with a painless cutaneous black eschar surrounded by extensive edema?

Back: Gram stain and culture of the skin lesion to evaluate for Bacillus anthracis.

Front: First-line antimicrobial treatment for naturally acquired or inhalational Anthrax?

Back: Ciprofloxacin or Doxycycline (combined with an antitoxin for systemic disease).

Front: Flaccid, descending paralysis beginning with cranial nerve palsies after consuming home-canned foods?

Back: Botulism (caused by Clostridium botulinum toxin).

Front: Mechanism of action of the Botulinum neurotoxin?

Back: Cleaves SNARE proteins, preventing the presynaptic release of Acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction.

Front: Treatment for foodborne Botulism in adults?

Back: Equine-derived heptavalent botulinum antitoxin.

Front: Treatment for infant Botulism caused by ingestion of Clostridium botulinum spores (e.g., from honey)?

Back: Human-derived botulinum immune globulin (BabyBIG).

Specific Exposure & Environmental Associations

Front: Prophylactic antibiotic regimen for a patient who sustained a puncture wound through the sole of a sneaker?

Back: Ciprofloxacin (to cover Pseudomonas aeruginosa).

Front: Organism responsible for a rapidly progressive, necrotizing soft-tissue infection after exposure to warm seawater or raw oysters in a patient with chronic liver disease?

Back: Vibrio vulnificus.

Front: First-line treatment for a systemic or necrotizing Vibrio vulnificus infection?

Back: Doxycycline plus a third-generation cephalosporin (e.g., Ceftriaxone).

Front: Causative organism of a localized cutaneous ulcer with painful regional lymphadenopathy following a scratch from a wild rabbit?

Back: Francisella tularensis.

Front: Flu-like illness with severe calf muscle tenderness, conjunctival suffusion, and jaundice after swimming in water contaminated with animal urine?

Back: Leptospirosis (caused by Leptospira interrogans).

Front: Classic clinical triad of severe Leptospirosis (Weil disease)?

Back: Jaundice, acute kidney injury, and hemorrhage.

Infection Control & Isolation Precautions

Front: Type of isolation precaution required for a patient with active Pulmonary Tuberculosis?

Back: Airborne precautions (requires an airborne infection isolation room [AIIR] and N95 respirator).

Front: Type of isolation precaution required for a patient with Clostridioides difficile infection?

Back: Contact precautions (requires gowns, gloves, and mandatory handwashing with soap and water instead of alcohol gel).

Front: Type of isolation precaution required for a patient with Neisseria meningitidis meningitis?

Back: Droplet precautions (requires a surgical mask for healthcare providers; can be discontinued after 24 hours of effective antibiotic therapy).

Front: Type of isolation precaution required for a patient with disseminated Herpes Zoster (shingles)?

Back: Airborne and Contact precautions.

Surgical Prophylaxis & Medical Conditions

Front: Standard timing for the administration of pre-operative prophylactic antibiotics to maximize tissue concentration?

Back: Within 60 minutes prior to the surgical incision.

Front: Preferred first-line antibiotic for routine surgical prophylaxis in clean procedures (e.g., orthopedic joint replacement)?

Back: Cefazolin.

Front: Empiric prophylactic antibiotic added to surgical regimens involving the colorectal tract to cover anaerobes?

Back: Cefoxitin, Cefotetan, or Cefazolin plus Metronidazole.

Front: Empiric antimicrobial management for a patient presenting with febrile neutropenia (Absolute Neutrophil Count $<500/\mu\text{L}$)?

Back: Monotherapy with an antipseudomonal beta-lactam (e.g., Piperacillin-Tazobactam, Cefepime, or Meropenem).

Front: Target window to administer post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) after an occupational needle-stick exposure to HIV?

Back: Ideally within 2 hours, and no later than 72 hours after exposure.

Front: Standard duration of antiretroviral therapy for HIV post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP)?

Back: 28 days.

Front: Standard three-drug regimen used for HIV post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP)?

Back: Tenofovir, Emtricitabine, and an integrase strand transfer inhibitor (e.g., Dolutegravir or Raltegravir).

Central Nervous System & Head/Neck Extensions

Front: Organism causing a rapidly fatal meningoencephalitis in a healthy individual with a recent history of swimming in warm, fresh water?

Back: Naegleria fowleri.

Front: Diagnostic finding on a wet mount of cerebrospinal fluid in a patient with Naegleria fowleri infection?

Back: Motile, trophozoite amebae.

Front: Severe, painful otitis externa with granulation tissue at the floor of the ear canal in an elderly diabetic patient?

Back: Malignant Otitis Externa.

Front: Most common causative pathogen of Malignant Otitis Externa?

Back: Pseudomonas aeruginosa.

Front: Preferred first-line intravenous antibiotic therapy for Malignant Otitis Externa?

Back: Ciprofloxacin or Cefepime.

Front: Rapidly progressive, bilateral cellulitis of the submandibular and sublingual spaces, typically originating from an infected mandibular molar?

Back: Ludwig angina.

Front: Primary immediate clinical priority in managing a patient with Ludwig angina?

Back: Secure and maintain a patent airway.

Endocarditis & Vascular Additions

Front: Culture-negative infective endocarditis organism associated with poor dental hygiene or periodontal disease?

Back: Eikenella corrodens (part of the HACEK group).

Front: Culture-negative infective endocarditis organism associated with exposure to farm animals and presenting as a chronic, indolent infection?

Back: Coxiella burnetii.

Front: Most common causative organism of prosthetic valve endocarditis within the first year after valve replacement surgery?

Back: Staphylococcus epidermidis.

Front: Classic immunologically mediated physical exam finding in infective endocarditis presenting as painful nodules on the pads of fingers or toes?

Back: Osler nodes.

Front: Classic physical exam finding in infective endocarditis presenting as painless, erythematous macules on the palms or soles?

Back: Janeway lesions.

Front: Diagnostic criteria schema used to establish a clinical diagnosis of Infective Endocarditis?

Back: Modified Duke Criteria.

Travel Medicine & Prophylaxis

Front: Preferred malaria chemoprophylaxis regimen for a pregnant traveler visiting a chloroquine-resistant region?

Back: Mefloquine.

Front: Malaria chemoprophylaxis agent that must be started 1 to 2 weeks before travel and continued for 4 weeks after returning?

Back: Mefloquine or Doxycycline.

Front: Malaria chemoprophylaxis agent that can be started 1 to 2 days before travel and stopped 7 days after returning?

Back: Atovaquone-Proguanil.

Front: Live-attenuated vaccine required for entry into certain sub-Saharan African and South American countries, documented via an International Certificate of Vaccination?

Back: Yellow Fever vaccine.

Front: Most common bacterial cause of traveler's diarrhea?

Back: Enterotoxigenic Escherichia coli (ETEC).

Front: First-line antibiotic choice for empirical self-treatment of severe traveler's diarrhea accompanied by high fever or systemic symptoms?

Back: Azithromycin or a fluoroquinolone (e.g., Ciprofloxacin).

High-Yield Antibiotic Toxicities & Crossover Interactions

Front: Antibiotic class classic for causing tendonitis and Achilles tendon rupture, particularly in elderly patients or those taking glucocorticoids?

Back: Fluoroquinolones.

Front: Antibiotic known to cause serotonin syndrome when co-administered with selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) due to its weak monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) activity?

Back: Linezolid.

Front: Reversible hematologic adverse effect associated with prolonged ($>2$ weeks) Linezolid therapy?

Back: Bone marrow suppression, most commonly presenting as thrombocytopenia.

Front: Classic electrolyte abnormality associated with high-dose Trimethoprim-Sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX) administration?

Back: Hyperkalemia (due to trimethoprim blocking sodium channels in the distal nephron).

Front: Antimicrobial agent associated with a disulfiram-like reaction (flushing, tachycardia, nausea) when consumed with alcohol?

Back: Metronidazole.

Front: Class of antibiotics that carries a small risk of cross-reactivity in patients with a confirmed, IgE-mediated anaphylactic allergy to penicillins?

Back: Cephalosporins (specifically first-generation agents).

Adult Immunizations & Special Populations

Front: Pneumococcal vaccination strategy recommended for all immunocompetent adults aged 65 years or older who have never received a pneumococcal vaccine?

Back: A single dose of PCV20 OR a single dose of PCV15 followed by PPSV23 one year later.

Front: Vaccine recommended for all pregnant individuals during weeks 27 through 36 of each pregnancy to provide passive immunity to the neonate?

Back: Tdap (Tetanus, Diphtheria, Acellular Pertussis).

Front: Age at which the recombinant Zoster vaccine (Shingrix) is routinely recommended for immunocompetent adults to prevent shingles?

Back: 50 years and older (administered as a 2-dose series).

Front: Two core vaccinations that must be administered to patients prior to undergoing an elective splenectomy?

Back: Meningococcal conjugate and Pneumococcal vaccines (along with Haemophilus influenzae type b).

Fever of Unknown Origin & Culture-Negative Entities

Front: Classic definition of Fever of Unknown Origin (FUO) in an outpatient setting?

Back: Fever $\ge 38.3^\circ\text{C}$ ($101^\circ\text{F}$) on several occasions lasting for at least 3 weeks with no diagnosis after 3 days of in-hospital investigation or 3 outpatient visits.

Front: Most common overall category of diseases responsible for Fever of Unknown Origin in adults?

Back: Infections (followed closely by malignancies and non-infectious inflammatory diseases).

Front: Zoonotic bacterial cause of culture-negative endocarditis or FUO associated with consuming unpasteurized goat cheese?

Back: Brucella melitensis.

Front: Systemic infectious disease caused by Tropheryma whipplei, presenting with arthralgias, weight loss, chronic diarrhea, and hyperpigmentation?

Back: Whipple disease.

Front: Classic diagnostic finding on a small bowel biopsy for a patient with Whipple disease?

Back: Periodic acid–Schiff (PAS)-positive macrophages containing rod-shaped bacilli.


=====================================================================
=====================================================================

Section: Internal Medicine
Topic: Rheumatology

Rheumatoid Arthritis (RA)
Front: Initial diagnostic imaging of choice for a patient suspected of having Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Bilateral hand and wrist radiographs.

Front: Earliest radiographic finding seen in early Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Periarticular osteopenia and soft tissue swelling.

Front: Definitive late-stage radiographic features pathognomonic for Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Marginal joint erosions and joint space narrowing.

Front: Most specific autoantibody for diagnosing Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Anti-Cyclic Citrullinated Peptide (anti-CCP) antibody.

Front: Joint classification that is characteristically spared in Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Distal Interphalangeal (DIP) joints.

Front: Most common deformity of the thumb seen in advanced Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Boutonniere deformity.

Front: Name of the deformity characterized by PIP hyperextension and DIP flexion?
Back: Swan-neck deformity.

Front: Life-threatening cervical spine complication of Rheumatoid Arthritis that requires screening before elective surgery?
Back: Atlantoaxial subluxation.

Front: First-line disease-modifying antirheumatic drug (DMARD) used for moderate-to-severe Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Methotrexate.

Front: Supplement that must be co-prescribed with Methotrexate to reduce side effects?
Back: Folic acid.

Front: Serious pulmonary complication associated with chronic Methotrexate therapy?
Back: Interstitial lung disease (methotrexate pneumonitis).

Front: Best initial screening test required before initiating any TNF-alpha inhibitor therapy?
Back: Interferon-gamma release assay (IGRA) or PPD test for latent Tuberculosis.

Front: Triad defining Felty Syndrome?
Back: Rheumatoid Arthritis, splenomegaly, and neutropenia.

Front: Most accurate diagnostic test for Felty Syndrome when presenting with severe neutropenia?
Back: Bone marrow biopsy showing myeloid maturation arrest.

Front: Constellation of symptoms defining Caplan Syndrome?
Back: Rheumatoid Arthritis combined with coal worker's pneumoconiosis.

Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE)
Front: Most sensitive screening autoantibody for Systemic Lupus Erythematosus?
Back: Antinuclear Antibody (ANA).

Front: Most specific autoantibodies for diagnosing Systemic Lupus Erythematosus?
Back: Anti-double-stranded DNA (anti-dsDNA) and Anti-Smith (anti-Sm) antibodies.

Front: Autoantibody closely correlated with disease activity and lupus nephritis development in SLE?
Back: Anti-double-stranded DNA (anti-dsDNA).

Front: Autoantibody associated with Drug-Induced Lupus Erythematosus?
Back: Anti-histone antibody.

Front: Top three medications most frequently implicated in Drug-Induced Lupus?
Back: Procainamide, Hydralazine, and Isoniazid.

Front: Classic skin manifestation of SLE triggered or exacerbated by sunlight exposure?
Back: Malar (butterfly) rash sparing the nasolabial folds.

Front: Type of endocarditis characterized by sterile verrucous vegetations on both sides of cardiac valves in SLE patients?
Back: Libman-Sacks endocarditis.

Front: Best initial management for mild systemic manifestations of SLE like arthritis or cutaneous lesions?
Back: Hydroxychloroquine.

Front: Mandatory routine screening test for patients on long-term Hydroxychloroquine therapy?
Back: Annual ophthalmologic exam to screen for retinal toxicity.

Front: Most accurate diagnostic method to confirm and classify Lupus Nephritis?
Back: Renal biopsy.

Front: Standard induction therapy for severe, proliferative Lupus Nephritis (Class III or IV)?
Back: Mycophenolate mofetil or Cyclophosphamide combined with high-dose glucocorticoids.

Front: Serum complement levels characteristically seen during an active SLE flare?
Back: Decreased C3 and C4 levels.

Front: Primary mechanism of anemia commonly seen in active, severe SLE?
Back: Warm Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (Coombs-positive).

Front: Severe, life-threatening complication of systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis or adult SLE involving massive macrophage activation?
Back: Macrophage Activation Syndrome (MAS).

Front: Key laboratory hallmarks of Macrophage Activation Syndrome?
Back: Precipitous drop in ESR, skyrocketing Ferritin, cytopenias, and elevated triglycerides.

Antiphospholipid Syndrome (APS)
Front: Paradoxical laboratory finding seen in Antiphospholipid Syndrome despite an increased risk of thrombosis?
Back: Prolonged Activated Partial Thromboplastin Time (aPTT).

Front: The three diagnostic autoantibodies for Antiphospholipid Syndrome?
Back: Lupus anticoagulant, anti-beta-2-glycoprotein I, and anti-cardiolipin antibodies.

Front: Common false-positive screening result seen in patients with high titers of anti-cardiolipin antibodies?
Back: False-positive VDRL or RPR test for Syphilis.

Front: Preferred long-term anticoagulation therapy for a non-pregnant patient with confirmed Antiphospholipid Syndrome and a history of DVT?
Back: Warfarin.

Front: Target INR range for a patient with Antiphospholipid Syndrome experiencing a first venous thromboembolic event?
Back: INR of 2.0 to 3.0.

Front: Preferred anticoagulation regimen for a pregnant patient with Antiphospholipid Syndrome?
Back: Low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH) combined with low-dose aspirin.

Front: Rare, life-threatening form of APS causing simultaneous multi-organ microvascular failure?
Back: Catastrophic Antiphospholipid Syndrome (CAPS).

Front: First-line management triad for Catastrophic Antiphospholipid Syndrome?
Back: High-dose IV glucocorticoids, therapeutic anticoagulation, and plasma exchange or IVIG.

Scleroderma (Systemic Sclerosis)
Front: Autoantibody most specific for Diffuse Cutaneous Systemic Sclerosis?
Back: Anti-Scl-70 (anti-topoisomerase I) antibody.

Front: Autoantibody most specific for Limited Cutaneous Systemic Sclerosis (CREST syndrome)?
Back: Anti-centromere antibody.

Front: The components that comprise the acronym CREST syndrome?
Back: Calcinosis, Raynaud phenomenon, Esophageal dysmotility, Sclerodactyly, and Telangiectasia.

Front: Most common cause of death in patients with Diffuse Cutaneous Systemic Sclerosis?
Back: Interstitial Lung Disease (pulmonary fibrosis).

Front: Leading vascular cause of death in patients with Limited Cutaneous Systemic Sclerosis?
Back: Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension.

Front: Sudden, life-threatening renal complication of Diffuse Scleroderma presenting with malignant hypertension and microangiopathic hemolytic anemia?
Back: Scleroderma Renal Crisis.

Front: Drug class of choice that is lifesaving in the management of Scleroderma Renal Crisis?
Back: Angiotensin-Converting Enzyme (ACE) inhibitors.

Front: Class of medications strictly contraindicated in high doses in systemic sclerosis due to the risk of precipitating Scleroderma Renal Crisis?
Back: Glucocorticoids.

Front: First-line medical therapy for managing severe Raynaud phenomenon in scleroderma patients?
Back: Calcium channel blockers (e.g., Nifedipine).

Sjögren Syndrome
Front: Classic diagnostic triad of primary Sjögren Syndrome?
Back: Keratoconjunctivitis sicca (dry eyes), xerostomia (dry mouth), and parotid gland enlargement.

Front: The two autoantibodies most highly associated with Sjögren Syndrome?
Back: Anti-Ro (SS-A) and Anti-La (SS-B) antibodies.

Front: Fetal risk associated with pregnant patients carrying positive anti-Ro (SS-A) antibodies?
Back: Neonatal lupus and congenital complete heart block.

Front: Best objective initial test to evaluate for decreased tear production in suspected Sjögren Syndrome?
Back: Schirmer test.

Front: Most definitive diagnostic test to confirm Sjögren Syndrome if serology is ambiguous?
Back: Lip biopsy showing focal lymphocytic sialadenitis.

Front: Patients with Sjögren Syndrome have a markedly elevated risk for developing which specific malignancy?
Back: Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma (specifically B-cell MALT lymphoma).

Inflammatory Myopathies (Dermatomyositis & Polymyositis)
Front: Pattern of muscle weakness characteristically seen in Polymyositis and Dermatomyositis?
Back: Symmetrical, proximal muscle weakness affecting the shoulders and hips.

Front: Most specific autoantibody for Polymyositis and Dermatomyositis associated with interstitial lung disease?
Back: Anti-Jo-1 (anti-tRNA synthetase) antibody.

Front: Autoantibody highly specific for Dermatomyositis presenting with classic cutaneous signs?
Back: Anti-Mi-2 antibody.

Front: Pathognomonic violaceous rash over the eyelids seen in Dermatomyositis?
Back: Heliotrope rash.

Front: Pathognomonic erythematous, scaly papules found over the bony prominences of the MCP and PIP joints?
Back: Gottron papules.

Front: Cutaneous finding in Dermatomyositis characterized by hyperkeratosis and scaling on the lateral aspects of the fingers?
Back: Mechanic's hands.

Front: Key histological difference on muscle biopsy between Polymyositis and Dermatomyositis?
Back: Polymyositis shows endomysial inflammation, while Dermatomyositis shows perimysial/perivascular inflammation.

Front: Crucial age-appropriate screening required upon diagnosing a patient with Dermatomyositis?
Back: Internal malignancy screening.

Front: First-line systemic pharmacotherapy for both Polymyositis and Dermatomyositis?
Back: High-dose oral glucocorticoids.

Seronegative Spondyloarthropathies
Front: Genotypic human leukocyte antigen highly associated with all seronegative spondyloarthropathies?
Back: HLA-B27.

Front: Classic radiographic spine finding in advanced, long-standing Ankylosing Spondylitis?
Back: Bamboo spine due to marginal syndesmophytes and fusion.

Front: Clinical pattern of low back pain classic for Ankylosing Spondylitis?
Back: Chronic back pain that improves with exercise and worsens with rest.

Front: Most common extra-articular manifestation of Ankylosing Spondylitis?
Back: Acute anterior uveitis.

Front: Screening test of choice for early Ankylosing Spondylitis when plain radiographs are normal?
Back: MRI of the sacroiliac joints.

Front: Classic clinical triad defining Reactive Arthritis?
Back: Uveitis/conjunctivitis, urethritis, and arthritis ("Can't see, can't pee, can't climb a tree").

Front: Common infectious triggers preceding Reactive Arthritis?
Back: Chlamydia trachomatis, Campylobacter, Salmonella, Shigella, and Yersinia.

Front: Characteristic cutaneous lesion of Reactive Arthritis found on the palms or soles resembling pustular psoriasis?
Back: Keratoderma blennorrhagica.

Front: Unique radiographic and clinical finding of the digits seen in Psoriatic Arthritis?
Back: Dactylitis ("sausage digits").

Front: Classic radiographic feature of the phalanges seen in severe Psoriatic Arthritis?
Back: "Pencil-in-cup" deformity.

Front: Class of medications used as first-line therapy for the symptomatic management of Ankylosing Spondylitis?
Back: NSAIDs.

Crystal-Induced Arthropathies (Gout & Pseudogout)
Front: Polarized light microscopy appearance of monosodium urate crystals in Gout?
Back: Needle-shaped and negatively birefringent (yellow when parallel to the slow wave of the compensator).

Front: Polarized light microscopy appearance of calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate (CPPD) crystals in Pseudogout?
Back: Rhomboid-shaped and weakly positively birefringent (blue when parallel to the slow wave of the compensator).

Front: Name given to the classic involvement of the first metatarsophalangeal (MTP) joint in acute Gout?
Back: Podagra.

Front: Radiographic hallmark of chronic, tophaceous Gout?
Back: Punched-out lytic bone erosions with overhanging cortical edges.

Front: Radiographic hallmark of Pseudogout (CPPD) found within joint cartilage?
Back: Chondrocalcinosis.

Front: First-line abortive therapies for an acute flare of Gout in a patient with normal renal function?
Back: NSAIDs (e.g., Indomethacin) or systemic/intra-articular glucocorticoids.

Front: Mechanism of action of Colchicine in acute gout management?
Back: Inhibits microtubule polymerization, disrupting neutrophil chemotaxis and degranulation.

Front: Major toxicities of Colchicine that restrict its use in patients with severe renal failure?
Back: Diarrhea, neuromyopathy, and bone marrow suppression.

Front: Best long-term, first-line urate-lowering therapy for chronic gout prevention?
Back: Allopurinol.

Front: Severe, life-threatening hypersensitivity syndrome associated with Allopurinol therapy?
Back: DRESS syndrome (Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms) or Stevens-Johnson Syndrome.

Front: Genetic screening test recommended for Asian patients before initiating Allopurinol to prevent severe cutaneous adverse reactions?
Back: HLA-B*5801 allele testing.

Front: Primary mechanism of action of Allopurinol and Febuxostat?
Back: Xanthine oxidase inhibition.

Front: Dangerous phenomenon that can occur if urate-lowering therapy is started during an acute gout flare without anti-inflammatory prophylaxis?
Back: Precipitation or worsening of the acute gout flare.

Front: Three systemic metabolic conditions strongly associated with secondary Pseudogout (CPPD)?
Back: Hyperparathyroidism, Hemochromatosis, and Hypomagnesemia.

Vasculitis (Large, Medium, Small Vessel)
Front: Most dreaded complication of untreated Giant Cell (Temporal) Arteritis?
Back: Permanent blindness due to anterior ischemic optic neuropathy.

Front: Immediate management step when Giant Cell Arteritis is strongly suspected, prior to obtaining a biopsy?
Back: High-dose intravenous or oral systemic glucocorticoids.

Front: Most definitive diagnostic test for confirming Giant Cell Arteritis?
Back: Temporal artery biopsy.

Front: Condition characterized by proximal limb girdle stiffness and high ESR, occurring in 50% of patients with Giant Cell Arteritis?
Back: Polymyalgia Rheumatica (PMR).

Front: Large-vessel vasculitis predominantly affecting young Asian females, causing upper extremity pulse deficits?
Back: Takayasu Arteritis.

Front: Medium-vessel necrotizing vasculitis strongly associated with chronic Hepatitis B infection?
Back: Polyarteritis Nodosa (PAN).

Front: Classic angiographic finding seen in the mesenteric or renal vessels of a patient with Polyarteritis Nodosa?
Back: Microaneurysms with a "string-of-beads" appearance.

Front: Organ system characteristically spared in Polyarteritis Nodosa?
Back: The lungs.

Front: Pediatric medium-vessel vasculitis presenting with high fever, strawberry tongue, and desquamating rash?
Back: Kawasaki Disease.

Front: Most critical cardiac complication of untreated Kawasaki Disease?
Back: Coronary artery aneurysms.

Front: Standard first-line medical regimen for acute Kawasaki Disease to reduce coronary artery risks?
Back: Intravenous Immunoglobulin (IVIG) and high-dose Aspirin.

Front: Small-vessel vasculitis characterized by granulomatous inflammation of the respiratory tract and necrotizing glomerulonephritis?
Back: Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (GPA / Wegener's).

Front: Highly specific serum autoantibody marker for Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis?
Back: c-ANCA (PR3-ANCA).

Front: Small-vessel vasculitis presenting with asthma, prominent blood eosinophilia, and transient pulmonary infiltrates?
Back: Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis (EGPA / Churg-Strauss).

Front: Highly specific serum autoantibody marker for Eosinophilic Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis and Microscopic Polyangiitis?
Back: p-ANCA (MPO-ANCA).

Front: Most common systemic vasculitis in children, presenting with palpable purpura, arthralgias, abdominal pain, and hematuria?
Back: IgA Vasculitis (Henoch-Schönlein Purpura).

Front: Deposition finding on renal biopsy pathognomonic for IgA Vasculitis?
Back: Mesangial IgA immune complex deposition.

Front: Small-vessel vasculitis precipitated by cold temperatures, causing palpable purpura, arthralgias, and strongly associated with Hepatitis C?
Back: Mixed Cryoglobulinemia.

Front: Key serum complement profile finding in patients presenting with a Mixed Cryoglobulinemia flare?
Back: Markedly depressed C4 levels with normal or mildly low C3.

Other Rheumatologic Conditions
Front: Diagnostic criteria combination used to confirm Behcet Syndrome?
Back: Recurrent painful oral aphthous ulcers plus two of: recurrent genital ulcers, ocular lesions (uveitis), or pachyderma skin lesions.

Front: Simple, non-invasive physical exam test demonstrating skin hyperreactivity used to support Behcet Syndrome diagnosis?
Back: Pathergy test (pustule formation 24-48 hours after a sterile needle prick).

Front: Serious neurological manifestation of Behcet Syndrome that can mimic multiple sclerosis?
Back: Neuro-Behcet disease (meningoencephalitis or dural sinus thrombosis).

Front: Best initial screening tool for assessing bone mineral density to diagnose Osteoporosis?
Back: Dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry (DEXA) scan.

Front: T-score threshold on a DEXA scan defining the diagnostic cutoff for Osteoporosis?
Back: T-score of -2.5 or lower.

Front: First-line pharmacotherapy class for the prevention and treatment of postmenopausal Osteoporosis?
Back: Oral Bisphosphonates (e.g., Alendronate).

Front: Rare, severe jaw complication associated with long-term, high-dose Bisphosphonate therapy?
Back: Osteonecrosis of the jaw.

Front: Atypical skeletal fracture complication associated with prolonged Bisphosphonate therapy exceeding 5 years?
Back: Atypical subtrochanteric femoral fractures.

Front: Core chronic widespread pain syndrome characterized by specific tender points, normal inflammatory markers, and sleep disturbance?
Back: Fibromyalgia.

Front: First-line FDA-approved non-opioid medications for the management of Fibromyalgia?
Back: Amitriptyline, Duloxetine, or Pregabalin.

Rheumatology-Hematology Overlap & Drug Toxicities
Front: Most common drug-induced cytopenia associated with high-dose Azathioprine therapy?
Back: Leukopenia (bone marrow suppression).

Front: Genetic enzyme deficiency that exponentially increases the risk of severe bone marrow toxicity when taking Azathioprine?
Back: Thiopurine Methyltransferase (TPMT) deficiency.

Front: Mechanism by which Allopurinol co-administration causes life-threatening cytopenias in a patient taking Azathioprine?
Back: Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase, preventing the metabolism of active azathioprine metabolites.

Front: Signature hematologic abnormality seen in the peripheral blood smear of a patient with active Macrophage Activation Syndrome?
Back: Hemophagocytosis (macrophages engulfing erythrocytes, leukocytes, and platelets).

Front: The classic presentation of Adult-Onset Still’s Disease?
Back: Quotidien (daily) high fevers, salmon-pink evanescent rash, arthritis, and extreme leukocytosis.

Front: Serum biomarker that is characteristically elevated to extreme levels in Adult-Onset Still's Disease?
Back: Ferritin.

Front: Serious hematologic adverse effect of Cyclophosphamide therapy that requires monitoring with regular urinalyses?
Back: Hemorrhagic cystitis.

Front: Co-administered agent utilized to neutralize the metabolite acrolein and prevent Cyclophosphamide-induced hemorrhagic cystitis?
Back: Mesna (2-mercaptoethane sulfonate).

Front: Malignancy that patients treated with Cyclophosphamide have a significantly increased long-term risk of developing?
Back: Transitional cell carcinoma of the bladder.


Osteoarthritis (OA)
Front: Most important non-pharmacologic intervention for slowing the progression of knee and hip Osteoarthritis?
Back: Weight loss and low-impact exercise.

Front: First-line pharmacotherapy for symptomatic management of mild-to-moderate Osteoarthritis?
Back: Topical or oral NSAIDs (e.g., Diclofenac, Naproxen).

Front: Radiographic hallmarks of advanced Osteoarthritis?
Back: Asymmetric joint space narrowing, subchondral sclerosis, osteophytes, and subchondral cysts.

Front: Name given to the palpable osteophyte nodules located at the Distal Interphalangeal (DIP) joints in Osteoarthritis?
Back: Heberden nodes.

Front: Name given to the palpable osteophyte nodules located at the Proximal Interphalangeal (PIP) joints in Osteoarthritis?
Back: Bouchard nodes.

Front: Character of joint stiffness in Osteoarthritis compared to Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Morning stiffness lasting less than 30 minutes that worsens with use and improves with rest.

Infectious & Reactive Arthritides
Front: Most common causative organism of acute bacterial septic arthritis in the general adult population?
Back: Staphylococcus aureus.

Front: Most common causative organism of septic arthritis in sexually active young adults?
Back: Neisseria gonorrhoeae.

Front: Classic disseminated triad of Disseminated Gonococcal Infection (DGI)?
Back: Polyarthralgias, tenosynovitis, and painless pustular skin lesions.

Front: Synovial fluid analysis findings pathognomonic for acute Septic Arthritis?
Back: Purulent fluid, WBC count greater than 50,000/µL, and greater than 75% polymorphonuclear leukocytes.

Front: Empiric antibiotic regimen of choice for a patient with suspected acute Septic Arthritis while cultures are pending?
Back: Intravenous Vancomycin combined with a third-generation cephalosporin (e.g., Ceftriaxone).

Front: Standard diagnostic protocol for a patient presenting with an acute, hot, swollen monoarticular joint?
Back: Immediate joint aspiration (arthrocentesis) and synovial fluid analysis.

Front: Classic rheumatologic manifestation of late-stage, untreated Lyme Disease?
Back: Intermittent or chronic monoarticular or oligoarticular arthritis, most commonly affecting the knee.

Front: Preferred first-line oral antibiotic for early localized Lyme disease and early Lyme arthritis?
Back: Doxycycline.

Sarcoidosis
Front: Classic chest radiographic finding pathognomonic for Stage I Sarcoidosis?
Back: Bilateral hilar adenopathy.

Front: Distinct triad defining Löfgren Syndrome (an acute presentation of Sarcoidosis)?
Back: Bilateral hilar adenopathy, erythema nodosum, and migratory polyarthritis.

Front: Histopathological hallmark finding on biopsy of an affected organ in Sarcoidosis?
Back: Non-caseating granulomas.

Front: Serum electrolyte and enzyme abnormalities classic for active systemic Sarcoidosis?
Back: Hypercalcemia, hypercalciuria, and elevated Angiotensin-Converting Enzyme (ACE) levels.

Front: Primary mechanism causing hypercalcemia in patients with Sarcoidosis?
Back: Unregulated 1-alpha-hydroxylase activity in granulomatous macrophages converting vitamin D to its active form.

Front: First-line systemic treatment for symptomatic or progressive organ-threatening Sarcoidosis?
Back: Systemic glucocorticoids.

Adult-Onset Still's Disease (AOSD)
Front: Classic triad of symptoms defining Adult-Onset Still's Disease?
Back: Quotidian (daily) high spiking fevers, an evanescent salmon-pink maculopapular rash, and arthritis.

Front: The unique timing characteristic of the salmon-pink rash in Adult-Onset Still's Disease?
Back: The rash appears or worsens concurrently with the daily fever spikes.

Front: Extreme laboratory abnormality characteristic of Adult-Onset Still's Disease that helps differentiate it from other rheumatologic conditions?
Back: Markedly elevated serum Ferritin levels (often exceeding 5,000 ng/mL) and profound leukocytosis.

Front: First-line pharmacotherapy for mild manifestations of Adult-Onset Still's Disease?
Back: High-dose NSAIDs.

Front: Preferred biologic drug targets for refractory or severe cases of Adult-Onset Still's Disease?
Back: Interleukin-1 (IL-1) inhibitors (e.g., Anakinra) or Interleukin-6 (IL-6) inhibitors (e.g., Tocilizumab).

Systemic Autoinflammatory & IgG4 Diseases
Front: Most common hereditary autoinflammatory periodic fever syndrome, caused by mutations in the MEFV gene?
Back: Familial Mediterranean Fever (FMF).

Front: Classic clinical presentation of a Familial Mediterranean Fever flare?
Back: Recurrent, self-limiting episodes of fever accompanied by peritonitis, pleuritis, or synovitis lasting 1 to 3 days.

Front: First-line maintenance medication used to decrease the frequency of flares and prevent systemic amyloidosis in Familial Mediterranean Fever?
Back: Colchicine.

Front: Type of systemic amyloidosis that develops as a long-term complication of untreated Familial Mediterranean Fever?
Back: AA (Secondary) Amyloidosis.

Front: Fibroinflammatory condition characterized by tumefactive lesions, elevated serum titers, and lymphoplasmacytic infiltrates?
Back: IgG4-Related Disease.

Front: Pathognomonic histopathological features of IgG4-Related Disease on tissue biopsy?
Back: Dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate rich in IgG4+ plasma cells, storiform fibrosis, and obliterative phlebitis.

Front: Common clinical manifestations or organ involvements seen in IgG4-Related Disease?
Back: Autoimmune pancreatitis (Type 1), Riedel thyroiditis, Mikulicz syndrome (enlarged lacrimal and salivary glands), and retroperitoneal fibrosis.

Advanced Biologics & Mechanisms of Action
Front: Specific molecular target of Rituximab used in severe granulomatosis with polyangiitis and refractory SLE?
Back: CD20 antigen on B lymphocytes.

Front: Mechanism of action of Belimumab used as an adjunctive therapy in systemic lupus erythematosus?
Back: Inhibits B-lymphocyte stimulator (BLyS), preventing B-cell survival and differentiation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Abatacept used in refractory Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Binds to CD80 and CD86 on antigen-presenting cells, blocking the costimulatory signal required for T-cell activation.

Front: Mechanism of action of Tocilizumab used in Giant Cell Arteritis and Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Interleukin-6 (IL-6) receptor antagonist.

Front: Mechanism of action of Secukinumab used in the treatment of severe Psoriatic Arthritis and Ankylosing Spondylitis?
Back: Interleukin-17A (IL-17A) inhibitor.

Front: Mechanism of action of Ustekinumab utilized in Psoriatic Arthritis?
Back: Inhibits the p40 subunit of both Interleukin-12 (IL-12) and Interleukin-23 (IL-23).

Front: Mechanism of action of Tofacitinib and Upadacitinib used in severe Rheumatoid Arthritis?
Back: Janus Kinase (JAK) inhibitors, blocking intracellular cytokine signaling.

Front: Black box warning or screening required before starting a patient on Janus Kinase (JAK) inhibitors?
Back: Screening for latent tuberculosis and monitoring for an increased risk of deep vein thrombosis (DVT) and malignancy.

=====================================================================
=====================================================================

Section: Internal Medicine

Topic: Neurology



Section 1: Cerebrovascular Diseases (Strokes & Hemorrhages)
Front: Most common overall site for a hypertensive intraparenchymal hemorrhage?
Back: Putamen.

Front: Classical presentation of a putaminal hemorrhage?
Back: Contralateral hemiparesis and gaze deviation toward the side of the lesion.  

Front: Clinical presentation distinguishing cerebellar hemorrhage from other intracranial bleeds?
Back: Sudden ataxia and vomiting without hemiparesis.  

Front: Definitive emergency intervention for an expanding cerebellar hemorrhage with neurological decline?
Back: Surgical decompression.

Front: Initial neuroimaging diagnostic step when evaluating an acute stroke-like presentation?
Back: Non-contrast CT scan of the head to rule out hemorrhage.  

Front: Time window for administering intravenous alteplase in acute ischemic stroke?
Back: Within 4.5 hours of when the patient was last seen normal.  

Front: Primary absolute contraindication to thrombolytic therapy in acute ischemic stroke?
Back: History of any intracranial hemorrhage.

Front: Eligibility window for mechanical thrombectomy in large vessel occlusion of the anterior circulation?
Back: Within 24 hours of symptom onset.

Front: Preferred initial antiplatelet management for acute ischemic stroke outside the thrombolytic window?
Back: Aspirin.

Front: Pathophysiological structure occluded in Lateral Medullary (Wallenberg) Syndrome?
Back: Posterior Inferior Cerebellar Artery or vertebral artery.

Front: Classic clinical triad of Lateral Medullary Syndrome?
Back: Ipsilateral Horner syndrome, ipsilateral facial loss of pain/temperature, and contralateral body loss of pain/temperature.

Front: Cranial nerve deficits explaining hoarseness and dysphagia in Wallenberg Syndrome?
Back: Nucleus ambiguus involvement affecting cranial nerves IX and X.

Front: Clinical presentation of a total basilar artery occlusion?
Back: Locked-in syndrome with preserved consciousness and vertical eye movements.

Front: Classic visual field deficit associated with a posterior cerebral artery occlusion?
Back: Homonymous hemianopsia with macular sparing.

Front: Characteristic shape of a subdural hematoma on a non-contrast head CT?
Back: Crescent-shaped collection crossing suture lines.

Front: Vessel ruptured causing a subdural hematoma?
Back: Bridging cortical veins.

Front: Predisposed patient populations for chronic subdural hematomas?
Back: Elderly individuals and chronic alcoholics.

Front: Characteristic shape of an epidural hematoma on a non-contrast head CT?
Back: Biconvex or lens-shaped collection restricted by suture lines.

Front: Vessel ruptured causing an epidural hematoma?
Back: Middle meningeal artery.

Front: Classical clinical progression of an epidural hematoma?
Back: Head trauma followed by a lucid interval and then rapid neurological demise.

Front: Most common etiology of a non-traumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage?
Back: Rupture of a saccular or berry aneurysm.

Front: First-line medication used to prevent vasospasm following a subarachnoid hemorrhage?
Back: Nimodipine.

Section 2: Neurodegenerative Disorders & Dementias
Front: Definitive histopathological features of Alzheimer disease?
Back: Extracellular amyloid-beta plaques and intracellular neurofibrillary tangles of hyperphosphorylated tau protein.

Front: Core cognitive presentation that differentiates Alzheimer disease from frontotemporal dementia?
Back: Early loss of short-term memory with preserved personality in early stages.

Front: Characterizing presentation of frontotemporal dementia?
Back: Early changes in personality, social disinhibition, and executive dysfunction.

Front: Clinical triad defining Dementia with Lewy Bodies?
Back: Fluctuating cognition, visual hallucinations, and spontaneous Parkinsonism.

Front: Classical clinical triad of Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus?
Back: Gait apraxia, urinary incontinence, and cognitive decline.

Front: Diagnostic and temporary therapeutic intervention for Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus?
Back: Large-volume lumbar puncture.

Front: Definitive definitive long-term treatment for Normal Pressure Hydrocephalus?
Back: Ventriculoperitoneal shunting.

Front: Distinguishing clinical feature of vascular dementia?
Back: Stepwise decline in cognitive function matching focal ischemic events.

Front: Characterizing clinical feature of Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease?
Back: Rapidly progressive dementia accompanied by myoclonus and ataxia.

Front: Diagnostic EEG finding associated with Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease?
Back: Periodic sharp wave complexes.

Front: Core diagnostic criteria required for a diagnosis of Parkinson disease?
Back: Bradykinesia combined with either a resting tremor or rigidity.  

Front: Mechanism of action of carbidopa when co-administered with levodopa?
Back: Inhibits peripheral DOPA decarboxylase to maximize central nervous system bioavailability.

Front: Genetic inheritance pattern and mutation associated with Huntington disease?
Back: Autosomal dominant CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion on chromosome 4.

Front: Specific neuroanatomical structure that atrophies in Huntington disease?
Back: Caudate nucleus.

Section 3: Neuromuscular & Peripheral Nerve Disorders
Front: Classical cerebrospinal fluid analysis finding in Guillain-Barré Syndrome?
Back: Albuminocytologic dissociation featuring elevated protein with normal white blood cell count.

Front: Most common infectious trigger associated with Guillain-Barré Syndrome?
Back: Campylobacter jejuni enteritis.

Front: Most critical monitoring parameters for a patient admitted with acute Guillain-Barré Syndrome?
Back: Forced vital capacity and negative inspiratory force to monitor respiratory failure.

Front: Proven effective treatments for Guillain-Barré Syndrome?
Back: Intravenous immunoglobulin or plasmapheresis.

Front: Contraindicated medication class in the management of acute Guillain-Barré Syndrome?
Back: Systemic corticosteroids.

Front: Underlying pathophysiology of Myasthenia Gravis?
Back: Autoantibodies directed against postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors.

Front: Classic presentation of muscle weakness in Myasthenia Gravis?
