Back: Fluctuating skeletal muscle weakness that worsens with repetitive use and improves with rest.

Front: Screening test that must be performed on all patients newly diagnosed with Myasthenia Gravis?
Back: CT scan of the chest to rule out a thymoma.

Front: First-line long-term medical management for symptomatic Myasthenia Gravis?
Back: Pyridostigmine.

Front: Immediate medical management for a patient in myasthenic crisis?
Back: Endotracheal intubation followed by plasmapheresis or intravenous immunoglobulin.

Front: Underlying pathophysiology of Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome?
Back: Autoantibodies directed against presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels.

Front: Classical physical exam finding that differentiates Lambert-Eaton from Myasthenia Gravis?
Back: Proximal muscle weakness that improves with repetitive contraction.

Front: Most common neoplastic condition associated with Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome?
Back: Small cell lung carcinoma.

Front: Sensory distribution altered in a classic stocking-glove pattern?
Back: Length-dependent peripheral polyneuropathy.

Front: First-line pharmacological management for painful diabetic neuropathy?
Back: Gabapentin, pregabalin, or duloxetine.

Front: Sensory and motor deficits found in a patient with a common peroneal nerve injury?
Back: Foot drop with loss of sensation on the dorsum of the foot.

Front: Physical exam finding pointing to a lesion of the long thoracic nerve?
Back: Winging of the scapula.

Front: Nerve compressed in carpal tunnel syndrome?
Back: Median nerve.

Section 4: Demyelinating & Neuroinflammatory Diseases
Front: Core diagnostic requirement for Multiple Sclerosis according to McDonald criteria?
Back: Neurological lesions disseminated in time and space.

Front: Gold standard neuroimaging modality for diagnosing Multiple Sclerosis?
Back: MRI of the brain and spine with gadolinium.

Front: Classic cerebrospinal fluid finding in patients with Multiple Sclerosis?
Back: Oligoclonal IgG bands on electrophoresis.

Front: Preferred initial treatment for an acute exacerbation of Multiple Sclerosis?
Back: Intravenous high-dose methylprednisolone.

Front: Mechanism of action of interferon-beta used in relapsing-remitting Multiple Sclerosis?
Back: Immunomodulation to reduce the frequency of clinical relapses.

Front: Defining clinical feature of Internuclear Ophthalmoplegia?
Back: Impaired adduction of the ipsilateral eye with nystagmus of the contralateral abducting eye during lateral gaze.

Front: Specific neuroanatomical structure damaged in Internuclear Ophthalmoplegia?
Back: Medial longitudinal fasciculus.

Front: Target antigen in Neuromyelitis Optica (Devic disease)?
Back: Aquaporin-4 water channels.

Front: Typical presentation of acute disseminated encephalomyelitis?
Back: Multifocal neurological deficits and encephalopathy following a viral infection or vaccination.

Section 5: Epilepsy & Seizures
Front: First-line pharmacological agent used to abort status epilepticus?
Back: Intravenous lorazepam or diazepam.

Front: Clinical definition of status epilepticus?
Back: Continuous seizure activity lasting greater than 5 minutes or recurrent seizures without recovery between episodes.

Front: Preferred long-term antiepileptic drug for generalized tonic-clonic seizures?
Back: Levetiracetam, valproic acid, or lamotrigine.

Front: Characteristic EEG abnormality seen in absence seizures?
Back: Generalized 3-Hz spike-and-wave discharges.

Front: First-line pharmacological treatment for absence seizures?
Back: Ethosuximide.

Front: Antiepileptic medication known to cause gingival hyperplasia and hirsutism?
Back: Phenytoin.

Front: Teratogenic risk uniquely associated with valproic acid exposure in utero?
Back: Neural tube defects.

Front: Severe dermatological adverse reaction associated with rapid titration of lamotrigine?
Back: Stevens-Johnson syndrome.

Front: Electrolyte abnormality classically induced by carbamazepine therapy?
Back: Hyponatremia due to syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion.

Front: Defining feature that separates a complex focal seizure from a simple focal seizure?
Back: Impairment of consciousness or awareness.

Section 6: Headache Syndromes & Cranial Neuralgias
Front: Classical clinical presentation of a cluster headache?
Back: Unilateral, excruciating periorbital pain accompanied by ipsilateral lacrimation and rhinorrhea.

Front: First-line abortive therapy for an acute attack of cluster headache?
Back: 100% high-flow oxygen via non-rebreather mask or subcutaneous sumatriptan.

Front: First-line prophylactic medication used to prevent cluster headaches?
Back: Verapamil.

Front: Abortive medication class for severe migraines contraindicated in patients with coronary artery disease?
Back: Triptans.

Front: Prophylactic medication options for frequent migraine headaches?
Back: Topiramate, valproic acid, propranolol, or amitriptyline.

Front: Clinical presentation of a tension headache?
Back: Bilateral, band-like, non-pulsatile headache without photophobia or phonophobia.

Front: First-line abortive treatment for a tension-type headache?
Back: Acetaminophen or nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs.

Front: Clinical presentation of trigeminal neuralgia?
Back: Unilateral, lancinating, shock-like facial pain triggered by light touch or chewing.

Front: First-line pharmacological management for trigeminal neuralgia?
Back: Carbamazepine.

Front: Diagnostic criteria that must be checked immediately in a patient over 50 presenting with a new headache and jaw claudication?
Back: Erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein to screen for Giant Cell Arteritis.

Section 7: CNS Infections & Altered Mental Status
Front: Classic triad of bacterial meningitis?
Back: Fever, altered mental status, and nuchal rigidity.

Front: Cerebrospinal fluid profile typical of acute bacterial meningitis?
Back: Elevated polymorphonuclear leukocytes, markedly decreased glucose, and elevated protein.

Front: Empirical antibiotic regimen for suspected bacterial meningitis in an adult aged 18 to 50?
Back: Ceftriaxone plus vancomycin.

Front: Additional empirical antibiotic required for meningitis in patients over age 50 or immunocompromised?
Back: Ampicillin to cover Listeria monocytogenes.

Front: Adjunctive medication that should be administered prior to or concurrently with antibiotics in suspected pneumococcal meningitis?
Back: Dexamethasone.

Front: Cerebrospinal fluid profile typical of viral meningitis?
Back: Elevated lymphocytes, normal glucose, and normal to mildly elevated protein.

Front: Most common focal lobe involved in Herpes Simplex Virus encephalitis?
Back: Temporal lobe.

Front: Preferred antiviral therapy for confirmed or highly suspected HSV encephalitis?
Back: Intravenous acyclovir.

Front: Classic diagnostic finding on cerebrospinal fluid analysis for Cryptococcal meningitis?
Back: Positive India ink preparation or cryptococcal antigen assay.

Front: Defining clinical difference between delirium and dementia?
Back: Delirium features an acute onset with fluctuating attention and awareness, whereas dementia is progressive and chronic.

Section 8: Spinal Cord & Neuro-oncology/Vascular Compression
Front: Clinical syndrome presenting with ipsilateral loss of motor function and contralateral loss of pain/temperature sensation?
Back: Brown-Séquard syndrome (spinal cord hemisection).

Front: Specific spinal cord pathways affected in Vitamin B12 deficiency?
Back: Dorsal columns and lateral corticospinal tracts.

Front: Clinical triad of spinal epidural abscess?
Back: Severe localized spinal pain, fever, and progressive neurological deficits.

Front: Best next step in management for a patient with suspected neoplastic spinal cord compression?
Back: Intravenous dexamethasone followed by an emergency MRI of the spine.

Front: Clinical features differentiating Cauda Equina Syndrome from Conus Medullaris Syndrome?
Back: Cauda Equina presents with asymmetric, hyporeflexic lower motor neuron signs and saddle anesthesia, whereas Conus Medullaris presents with symmetric, hyperreflexic signs and early bowel/bladder dysfunction.

Front: Pathophysiological cause of Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension?
Back: Decreased resorption of cerebrospinal fluid in the presence of normal ventricular size.

Front: Medication used as first-line therapy to decrease cerebrospinal fluid production in Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension?
Back: Acetain-induced or acetazolamide.

Front: Primary ophthalmologic risk associated with untreated Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension?
Back: Permanent vision loss due to optic atrophy from chronic papilledema.

Section 9: Neuro-Ophthalmology, Otology, & Autonomic Disorders
Front: Clinical test used to differentiate benign paroxysmal positional vertigo from central causes of vertigo?
Back: Dix-Hallpike maneuver.

Front: Definitive bedside repositioning maneuver used to treat benign paroxysmal positional vertigo?
Back: Epley maneuver.

Front: Distinguishing clinical feature that separates vestibular neuritis from labyrinthitis?
Back: Labyrinthitis presents with unilateral sensorineural hearing loss, while vestibular neuritis preserves hearing.

Front: Classic presentation of Meniere disease?
Back: Episodic vertigo, sensorineural hearing loss, and tinnitus accompanied by a sensation of fullness in the ear.

Front: Clinical triad defining Horner syndrome?
Back: Ptosis, miosis, and anhidrosis.

Front: Underlying pupillary defect seen in an Argyll Robertson pupil?
Back: Pupil constricts to accommodation but fails to constrict to direct light.

Front: Most common infectious disease associated with an Argyll Robertson pupil?
Back: Neurosyphilis ( tertiary syphilis).

Front: Diagnostic pupillary finding seen in an afferent pupillary defect (Marcus Gunn pupil)?
Back: Paradoxical dilation of the affected eye when light is swung from the unaffected eye to the affected eye.


Section 10: Toxic, Metabolic, & Nutritional Encephalopathies
Front: Classical clinical triad of Wernicke encephalopathy?
Back: Encephalopathy, oculomotor dysfunction, and gait ataxia.

Front: Irreversible cognitive syndrome that develops if Wernicke encephalopathy is left untreated?
Back: Korsakowski syndrome, characterized by anterograde amnesia and confabulation.

Front: Crucial administration sequence rule for thiamine and glucose in an unresponsively malnourished patient?
Back: Administer thiamine before or concurrently with glucose to prevent worsening of encephalopathy.

Front: Specific neuroanatomical structures damaged in Wernicke encephalopathy?
Back: Mammillary bodies and medial thalamic nuclei.

Front: Pathophysiological complication of correcting chronic hyponatremia too rapidly?
Back: Central pontine myelinolysis or osmotic demyelination syndrome.

Front: Clinical presentation of central pontine myelinolysis?
Back: Acute paralysis, dysphagia, dysarthria, and locked-in syndrome occurring days after electrolyte correction.

Front: Pathophysiological complication of correcting acute hypernatremia too rapidly?
Back: Cerebral edema and seizures.

Front: Characteristic physical exam finding pathognomonic for hepatic encephalopathy?
Back: Asterixis, or flapping tremor of the extended wrists.

Front: First-line pharmacological agent used to decrease serum ammonia in hepatic encephalopathy?
Back: Lactulose.

Front: Secondary mechanism of action of rifaximin in the management of hepatic encephalopathy?
Back: Decreases ammonia-producing gut bacteria.

Front: Classic neuroimaging finding in carbon monoxide poisoning?
Back: Bilateral necrosis of the globus pallidus.

Section 11: Atypical Movement Disorders & Cerebellar Pathology
Front: Core clinical features of Progressive Supranuclear Palsy?
Back: Parkinsonism, early falls, and impairment of vertical downgaze.

Front: Distinguishing clinical feature of Multiple System Atrophy?
Back: Parkinsonism accompanied by severe, early autonomic failure and orthostatic hypotension.

Front: First-line medical treatment for an isolated essential tremor?
Back: Propranolol or primidone.

Front: Classic physical characteristic of an essential tremor?
Back: Action or postural tremor that improves with alcohol consumption.

Front: Pathophysiological cause of Wilson disease?
Back: Autosomal recessive mutation leading to impaired copper transport and toxic accumulation.

Front: Ocular physical exam finding diagnostic for Wilson disease?
Back: Kayser-Fleischer rings in the cornea.

Front: Preferred initial medical treatment for symptomatic Wilson disease?
Back: Penicillamine or trientine copper chelators.

Front: Classic gait abnormality seen in a patient with a cerebellar midline hemispheric lesion?
Back: Wide-based, unsteady gait known as truncal ataxia.

Section 12: Neuro-Oncology & Paraneoplastic Syndromes
Front: Most common primary malignant brain tumor in adults?
Back: Glioblastoma multiforme.

Front: Classic neuroimaging appearance of a glioblastoma multiforme on MRI?
Back: Ring-enhancing lesion crossing the corpus callosum with a necrotic center.

Front: Most common benign primary brain tumor in adults?
Back: Meningioma.

Front: Histopathological hallmark of a meningioma?
Back: Whorled pattern of cells and psammoma bodies.

Front: Most common sources of metastatic tumors found in the central nervous system?
Back: Lung cancer, breast cancer, and melanoma.

Front: Paraneoplastic syndrome presenting with opsoclonus-myoclonus ataxia in children?
Back: Neuroblastoma.

Front: Target antigen in paraneoplastic limbic encephalitis associated with ovarian teratomas?
Back: Anti-NMDA receptors.

Section 13: Neuro-Trauma, Intracranial Pressure, & Brain Death
Front: Clinical triad pointing to increased intracranial pressure and impending herniation?
Back: Cushing triad, featuring bradycardia, respiratory irregularity, and hypertension.

Front: Immediate medical intervention used to acutely hyperventilate a patient with elevated intracranial pressure?
Back: Lowering arterial carbon dioxide tension to induce cerebral vasoconstriction.

Front: Osmotic diuretic agent used to acutely reduce brain volume in cerebral edema?
Back: Intravenous mannitol.

Front: Specific cranial nerve compromised when uncal herniation causes a fixed and dilated pupil?
Back: Oculomotor nerve.

Front: Clinical definition of brain death?
Back: Irreversible loss of all brain function, including the brainstem, confirmed by an apnea test.

Section 14: Neuro-Rheumatology & Autoimmune Neuro-Pathology
Front: Classical presentation of Central Nervous System Vasculitis?
Back: Multifocal ischemic strokes of varying ages combined with chronic headache and encephalopathy.

Front: Preferred initial management for confirmed Giant Cell Arteritis to prevent ischemic blindness?
Back: High-dose intravenous or oral systemic corticosteroids.

Front: Neurological presentation of neuro-Behçet disease?
Back: Recurrent meningoencephalitis and brainstem lesions in a patient with oral and genital ulcers.

Front: Classic neurological manifestation of Neuro-Sarcoidosis?
Back: Unilateral or bilateral cranial nerve VII palsies with granulomatous basilar meningitis.

Section 15: Autonomic, Sleep, & Cranial Nerve Disorders
Front: Clinical triad diagnostic for Narcolepsy?
Back: Excessive daytime sleepiness, cataplexy, and hypnagogic hallucinations.

Front: First-line pharmacological treatment for the maintenance of wakefulness in Narcolepsy?
Back: Modafinil.

Front: Preferred medical intervention used to treat acute cataplexy in narcoleptic patients?
Back: Sodium oxybate or selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors.

Front: Clinical presentation of Bell Palsy?
Back: Sudden onset unilateral facial paralysis involving both the upper and lower face.

Front: Preferred early medical intervention for optimal recovery from Bell Palsy?
Back: Oral corticosteroids administered within 72 hours of symptom onset.

Front: Primary physical sign distinguishing a peripheral Bell Palsy from a central stroke lesion?
Back: Bell palsy involves the forehead muscles, whereas a stroke spares the forehead due to bilateral cortical innervation.

Section 16: Systemic Disease Manifestations & Spinal Pathology
Front: Most common spinal cord tract involved in tabes dorsalis?
Back: Posterior or dorsal columns.

Front: Clinical signs of tabes dorsalis?
Back: Sensory ataxia, lightning pains, and loss of proprioception.

Front: Classic presentation of syringomyelia?
Back: Capelike, bilateral loss of pain and temperature sensation across the shoulders and upper extremities with preserved touch.

Front: Neuroanatomical structure compressed first by an expanding syrinx in syringomyelia?
Back: Anterior white commissure.

Front: Spinal cord tracts degenerated in Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis?
Back: Corticospinal tracts and anterior horn cells.

Front: Clinical physical exam findings characterizing Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis?
Back: Combined upper motor neuron and lower motor neuron signs without sensory loss.

Front: Only approved medication demonstrated to modestly prolong survival in Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis?
Back: Riluzole.

Front: Mechanism of action of riluzole in Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis?
Back: Decreases presynaptic glutamate release.

Front: Spinal cord structures damaged in anterior spinal artery occlusion?
Back: Anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord, sparing the dorsal columns.

Front: Clinical presentation of an anterior spinal artery infarction?
Back: Sudden bilateral loss of motor function and pain/temperature sensation below the lesion with preserved proprioception.

Front: Diagnostic imaging step for a patient presenting with new onset spinal cord compression symptoms?
Back: Urgent emergent MRI of the entire spine.

Front: Most common cause of neurogenic claudication?
Back: Lumbar spinal stenosis.

Front: Classic physical positioning that relieves the pain of lumbar spinal stenosis?
Back: Flexion of the spine or leaning forward.

Front: Clinical feature distinguishing neurogenic claudication from vascular claudication?
Back: Neurogenic claudication is strictly positional, while vascular claudication is volume-of-exertion dependent.

Front: Classic neurological complication of untreated idiopathic intracranial hypertension?
Back: Progressive optic atrophy and permanent blindness.

Section 17: Neuro-Toxicology & Drug-Induced Emergencies
Front: Classic neuroimaging finding in acute methanol poisoning?
Back: Bilateral necrosis of the putamen.

Front: Clinical presentation of methanol poisoning distinguishing it from ethylene glycol toxicity?
Back: Severe visual impairment or blindness described as "being in a snowstorm."

Front: Classic neuroimaging and clinical finding in ethylene glycol poisoning?
Back: Acute kidney injury with calcium oxalate crystals in the urine and cranial neuropathy.

Front: Antidote used to competitively inhibit alcohol dehydrogenase in toxic alcohol ingestions?
Back: Fomepizole.

Front: Life-threatening condition characterized by severe rigidity, hyperthermia, and autonomic instability following dopamine antagonist administration?
Back: Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome.

Front: First-line medical treatment for severe Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome?
Back: Dantrolene or bromocriptine.

Front: Pathophysiological cause of Serotonin Syndrome that distinguishes its physical exam from Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome?
Back: Presence of neuromuscular hyperreactivity featuring hyperreflexia and clonus.

Front: First-line pharmacological management for severe Serotonin Syndrome?
Back: Cyproheptadine.

Front: Acute movement emergency presenting with involuntary sustained muscle contractions of the neck or tongue within hours of taking an antipsychotic?
Back: Acute dystonia.

Front: First-line medication used to reverse an acute dystonic reaction?
Back: Intravenous or intramuscular benztropine or diphenhydramine.

Section 18: Neurocutaneous Syndromes & Transitional Pathology
Front: Autosomal dominant neurocutaneous disorder presenting with café-au-lait spots, axillary freckling, and Lisch nodules?
Back: Neurofibromatosis Type 1.

Front: Most common central nervous system tumors associated with Neurofibromatosis Type 2?
Back: Bilateral vestibular schwannomas.

Front: Clinical presentation of Tuberous Sclerosis?
Back: Infantile spasms, ash-leaf spots, shagreen patches, and facial angiofibromas.

Front: Classic neuroimaging findings associated with Tuberous Sclerosis?
Back: Cortical tubers and subependymal nodules.

Front: Neurocutaneous syndrome characterized by a facial port-wine stain in the V1/V2 distribution and leptomeningeal angiomas?
Back: Sturge-Weber syndrome.

Front: Primary neurological complication associated with Sturge-Weber syndrome?
Back: Refractory seizures and contralateral hemiparesis.

Front: Central nervous system vascular tumors characteristically found in Von Hippel-Lindau disease?
Back: Hemangioblastomas of the retina and cerebellum.

Section 19: Autonomic Bladder Disorders & Advanced Stroke Mimics
Front: Bladder dysfunction characterized by an overactive detrusor muscle due to a spinal cord lesion above T12?
Back: Neurogenic detrusor overactivity or spastic bladder.

Front: Bladder dysfunction characterized by an underactive detrusor muscle due to a conus medullaris or cauda equina lesion?
Back: Flaccid bladder or detrusor arreflexia resulting in overflow incontinence.

Front: Clinical condition presenting with acute stroke-like deficits following severe hypoglycemia?
Back: Neuroglycopenic focal deficit or hypoglycemia stroke mimic.

Front: Immediate diagnostic step for any patient presenting with acute focal neurological deficits prior to ordering a head CT?
Back: Point-of-care fingerstick blood glucose measurement.

Front: Clinical syndrome presenting with severe headache, visual changes, seizures, and posterior white matter edema on MRI?
Back: Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy Syndrome.

Front: Most common systemic trigger for Posterior Reversible Encephalopathy Syndrome?
Back: Acute, severe hypertension or hypertensive emergency.

Front: Clinical presentation of Transient Global Amnesia?
Back: Sudden onset of anterograde amnesia with repetitive questioning that completely resolves within 24 hours.

Front: Diagnostic criteria distinguishing transient ischemic attacks from ischemic strokes?
Back: Transient ischemic attacks feature focal deficits lasting under 24 hours with absolutely no evidence of infarction on diffusion-weighted MRI.
